71: For example, the conjugates of ate are {ate, tea, eat}.
72:    Here is the most fundamental definition of our paper:
73: Definition 1. The circular critical exponent of a word w, denoted by cce(w), is the supre-
74: mum of exp(x) over all finite nonempty factors x of all conjugates of w.
75:    Note that cce(w) can be as much as twice as large as ce(w). See [9] for more about this
76: notion for infinite words.
77: 
78: 2.1    The Thue-Morse word
79: The Thue-Morse word t has many equivalent definitions [3], but for us it will be sufficient
80: to describe it as the fixed point, starting with 0, of the morphism µ mapping 0 → 01 and
81: 1 → 10.
82:    A basic fact about the binary alphabet is that every word of length ≥ 4 has critical
83: exponent at least 2. Thue proved that the Thue-Morse word has no overlaps. Thus we get
84: the following (trivial) result about factors of the Thue-Morse word.
85: 
86:                                                   2
679:    In principle, we could also treat the Rudin-Shapiro sequence. For example, one might be
680: able to prove the following.
681: 
682: Conjecture 19. Every nonempty factor of the Rudin-Shapiro sequence has a circular critical
683: exponent lying in
684:                             5 8    10 7 11 15 21 13 14
685:                       {1, 2, , , 3, , , , , 4, , , , 5, 6, 7, 8}.
686:                             2 3     3 2 3 4    5 3 3
687: However, so far we have not been able to complete the computations with Walnut (it runs
688: out of space).
689:     For some infinite words, the sets under consideration will be infinite, and hence another
690: kind of analysis will be needed. As an example, consider the infinite word 210201 · · · that
691: is a fixed point of 2 → 210, 1 → 20, 0 → 1. It is well-known that this word is squarefree,
692: but contains factors with exponent arbitrarily close to 2. In this case there will be no finite
693: analogue of our Proposition 2 and Theorem 6. The same case occurs for the Fibonacci word
694: (the fixed point of 0 → 01 and 1 → 0).

133: Conjecture 1 (Main Conjecture). m(n) ≥ 21 .
134:       It is known that this bound is tight, and the current best known lower bound
135: is 0.09 [1].
136:       We can show that m(n) ≤ m(n + 1). For any configuration S with n points,
137: choose any point P = (x, y). Then, add a point P 0 = (x + , y + ), for some
138: small  > 0, to make a configuration S 0 with n + 1 points. For any packing of
139: S 0 , we can construct a packing of S as follows: In the packing of S 0 , let Q be
140: the upper right corner of the rectangle anchored at P 0 . Then, delete the point
141: P 0 , and replace the rectangle anchored at P with a rectangle with upper right
142: corner Q. This creates a packing of S.
143:       For sufficiently small , this new rectangle will not have any points on its
144: interior. The rectangle anchored at P in the packing of S is strictly larger than
145: the one anchored at P 0 in the packing of S 0 , so the only extra area the packing
146: of S 0 has is the rectangle anchored at P , which has area at most . Thus, for
149:       Depending on the number of points, the calculations show that we can make
150: a more precise conjecture.

155: 2.2    Maximizing and minimizing area
156: As we are only interested in the maximum possible area, we only consider config-
157: urations whose areas can not be increased with small changes. Define a maximal
158: rectangle to be one whose upper edge touches either the upper boundary of the
159: unit square, or another point, and whose right edge touches either the right
160: boundary of the unit square, or another point. From now on we only con-
161: sider maximal anchored rectangles. As before, we denote a maximal rectangle
162: anchored at point Pk as rk .
163:     Given a set of points, there might be several ways to draw anchored rectan-
164: gles. For example, Figure 2 has two different ways to use maximal rectangles
165: and the first packing provides better area.

347:    • All increasing permutations correspond to the identity permutation. We

360:     We discuss other more general cases. First we concentrate on the case of
361: layered permutations. We begin with a special case of a permutation starting
362: with a layer of size m, followed by a layer of size 1, possibly with other points
363: after that.
364: 
365:    • A prelayered permutation is one that starts with (m, m − 1, . . . , 1, m + 1),
366:      for some m. These are covered in Section 7.1. When constructing an
367:      anchored rectangle packing of maximum area, it suffices to separately
368:      maximize the area of the rectangles anchored on P1 , . . ., Pm , and Pm+1 ,
369:      . . ., Pn−1 .

393: 
394:     • A mountain is a permutation (a1 , . . . , an−1 ) for which there exists some
395:       m such that a1 < a2 < · · · < am > am+1 > · · · > an−1 . We show that the
396:       Main Conjecture 1 for the mountain case in Section 8.
397: 
398:      Permutations that are both split-layer and mountain permutations must be
399: cliff permutations.
400:      The greedy decreasing subsequence of a permutation is the subsequence of
401: elements that are less than all preceding elements in the permutation. Note that
402: the elements of the greedy decreasing subsequence correspond to points which
403: bound a possible maximal rectangle anchored at the origin. We also consider
404: the case where elements of this greedy decreasing subsequence are close together
405: in the permutation.
406: 
407:     • An m-sparse decreasing permutation is one where there are fewer than
408:       m elements between consecutive members of the greedy decreasing sub-
409:       sequence. We find lower bounds for the area in this case in terms of m
410:       in Section 10. For m ≤ 3, we show that the Main Conjecture 1 holds for
411:       m-sparse decreasing case.
412: 
413:     The sparse case allows to prove the Main Conjecture 1 for n ≤ 9. We do
414: this in Section 10.1.

416: 2.6    Examples
417: We consider examples with small number of dots.
418:     If there is only one dot, we can fill the entire square by considering it to be
419: a rectangle anchored at the origin.
420:     Suppose we have two dots: the origin and (x, y). The rectangle at (x, y) has
421: size (1 − x)(1 − y). The other is x × 1 or 1 × y. Thus, the maximum area is
422: (1 − x)(1 − y) + max{x, y}.
423:     Without loss of generality, x ≥ y. Then, the area is 1−y+xy ≥ 1−y+y 2 ≥ 34 .
424: Equality is achieved if and only if x = y = 12 .

1623: 10     Sparse decreasing case
1624: We now consider permutations that have special decreasing subsequences, but
1625: are not necessarily decreasing themselves.
1626: Definition 1. For a permutation (a1 , . . . , an−1 ), we define its greedy decreasing
1627: subsequence to be the sequence containing every element, including a1 , that is
1628: less than all preceding elements.
1629:     Equivalently, we can build this subsequence starting from the end of the
1630: permutation. We include 1, and then pick the smallest number to the left of
1631: one. Continue recursively.
1632:     For example, the greedy decreasing subsequence of (7, 5, 9, 6, 4, 1, 3, 2) is
1633: (7, 5, 4, 1), and the greedy decreasing subsequence of any permutation starting
1634: with 1 is just (1).
1635: Definition 2. For a set of points in the unit square, the untouchable points are
1636: the points corresponding to the greedy decreasing subsequence of the permuta-
1637: tion.
1638: 
1639: 
1640:                                          35
1641: 
1642:                         Figure 20: Example with m = 3
1643: 
1644: 
1645:    Note that only rectangles anchored at the origin can be bounded by the
1646: untouchable points. No other maximal rectangles can touch the untouchable
1647: points.
1648:    Every other point, excluding the origin, is above and to the right of at least
1649: one untouchable point.
1650: Definition 3. We say that a permutation (a1 , a2 , . . . , an−1 ) is m-sparse de-
1651: creasing if there are no subsequences of m or more consecutive terms, none of
1652: which are in the greedy decreasing subsequence.
1653:     Informally, a permutation is m-sparse decreasing if we can delete consecutive
1654: strings of fewer than m elements to get the greedy decreasing subsequence.
1655:     Note that 1-sparse decreasing permutations are simply decreasing permuta-
1656: tions.
1657:     For example, (2, 1, 4, 3) is 3-sparse, as we can delete the last two elements
1658: to get (2, 1), which is the greedy decreasing subsequence. However, (2, 1, 4, 3) is
1659: not 2-sparse, as we would need to delete both 4 and 3, which are 2 consecutive
1660: elements.
1661:     We now show that we can always fill some fraction, dependent only on m,
1662: of the staircase region defined by the greedy decreasing subsequence, for any
1663: m-sparse permutation.

113: 2.1    Definition and properties of Hoffman graphs
114: Definition 2.1. A Hoffman graph h is a pair (H, ℓ) of a graph H and a
115: labeling map ℓ : V (H) → {fat, slim} satisfying two conditions:
116: 
117:  (i) the vertices with label fat are pairwise non-adjacent,
118: 
119:  (ii) every vertex with label fat has at least one neighbor with label slim.
120: 
121:     The vertices with label fat are called fat vertices, and the set of fat
122: vertices of h are denoted by Vfat (h). The vertices with label slim are called
123: slim vertices, and the set of slim vertices are denoted by Vslim (h). Now, we
124: give some definitions.
125: 
126: 
127:                                       3
128: 
129: Definition 2.2. For a Hoffman graph h, a Hoffman graph h1 = (H1 , ℓ1 ) is
130: called an induced Hoffman subgraph of h if H1 is an induced subgraph of H
131: and ℓ(x) = ℓ1 (x) for all vertices x of H1 .
132: 
133: Definition 2.3. Two Hoffman graphs h = (H, ℓ) and h′ = (H ′, ℓ′ ) are called
134: isomorphic if there exists a graph isomorphism ψ from H to H ′ such that
135: ℓ(x) = ℓ′ (ψ(x)) for all vertices x of H.
136: 
137: Definition 2.4. For a Hoffman graph h = (H, ℓ), let A(H) be the adjacency
138: matrix of H with a labeling in which the fat vertices come last. Then
139:                                                  
140:                                           Aslim C
141:                             A(H) =                  ,
142:                                           CT O
143: where Aslim is the adjacency matrix of the subgraph of H induced by slim
144: vertices and O is the zero matrix.
145:     The real symmetric matrix S(h) = Aslim −CC T is called the special matrix
146: of h, and the eigenvalues of h are the eigenvalues of S(h).
147: 
148:    For a Hoffman graph h, we focus on its smallest eigenvalue in this pa-
149: per. Let λmin (h) denote the smallest eigenvalue of h. Now, we discuss some
150: spectral properties of λmin(h) without proofs.

375: Definition 6.1. For an integer q > 1, the q-coclique extension G̃q of a graph
376: G is the graph obtained from G by replacing each vertex x ∈ G by a coclique
377: X̃ with q vertices, such that x̃ ∈ X̃ and ỹ ∈ Ỹ are adjacent if and only if x
378: and y are adjacent in G.
379:    If G̃q is the q-coclique extension of G, then G̃q has adjacency matrix
380: A ⊗ Jq , where Jq is the all one matrix of size q and ⊗ denotes the Kronecker
381: product. This shows that, if a graph G has spectrum

403: sider co-edge regular graphs with parameters (v, k, c2), which are k-regular
404: graphs with v vertices and the property such that every pair of non-adjacent
405: vertices has exactly c2 common neighbors. By applying Proposition 1.3 and
406: Theorem 1.1, we obtain the following theorem.

411: regular graph with parameters (v, k, c2). Then there exists a real number

15:                                                    the set of fertility numbers is closed under multiplication and contains every nonnegative integer
16:                                                    that is not congruent to 3 modulo 4. We show that the lower asymptotic density of the set of
17:                                                    fertility numbers is at least 1954/2565 ≈ 0.7618. We also exhibit some positive integers that are
18:                                                    not fertility numbers and conjecture that there are infinitely many such numbers.

23:                                                                                             1. Introduction
24: 
25:                                               Throughout this article, the word “permutation” refers to a permutation of a finite set of positive
26:                                            integers. We write permutations as words in one-line notation. Let Sn denote the set of permuta-
27:                                            tions of {1, . . . , n}. We say a permutation is normalized if it an element of Sn for some n (e.g., the
28:                                            permutation 12547 is not normalized).
29: 
30:                                              The study of permutation patterns, which has now developed into a vast area of research, began
31:                                            with Knuth’s investigation of stack-sorting in [12]. In his 1990 Ph.D. thesis, Julian West [16]
85: sorting map (building upon results from [11]). For completeness, we repeat the short description
86: of valid hook configurations from [9] in Section 2. See also [4, 5, 9, 14, 15] for further investigation
87: of the combinatorics of valid hook configurations.
88: Definition 1.1. Say a nonnegative integer f is a fertility number if there exists a permutation
89: with fertility f . Say a nonnegative integer is an infertility number if it is not a fertility number.
90: 
91:    For example, 0, 1, and 2 are fertility numbers because |s−1 (21)| = 0, |s−1 (1)| = 1, and |s−1 (12)| =
92: 2. In Section 3, we prove the following statements about fertility numbers. These are Theorems
93: 3.1–3.5 below.
94: 
95:       • The set of fertility numbers is closed under multiplication.
96:       • If f is a fertility number, then there are arbitrarily long permutations with fertility f .
97:       • Every nonnegative integer that is not congruent to 3 modulo 4 is a fertility number. The
98:         lower asymptotic density of the set of fertility numbers is at least 1954/2565 ≈ 0.7618.
99:       • The smallest fertility number that is congruent to 3 modulo 4 is 27.
100:       • If f is a positive fertility number, then there exist a positive integer n ≤ f + 1 and a
101:         permutation π ∈ Sn such that f = |s−1 (π)|.
299: southwest endpoint (i, πi ) and northeast endpoint (j, πj ). When referring to a point “below” H,
300: we mean a point (x, y) with i < x < j and y < πj . In particular, the endpoints of a hook do not
301: lie below that hook.
302: Definition 3.1. Let π = π1 · · · πn be a permutation, and let H be a hook drawn on the plot of π.
303: We say H is a stationary hook if it appears in every valid hook configuration of π.
304: 
305:    For example, suppose π ∈ Sn , πn = n and πi = n − 1, where i ≤ n − 2. Let H be the hook with
306: southwest endpoint (i, n − 1) and northeast endpoint (n, n). The point (i, n − 1) is a descent top of
307: π, so every valid hook configuration of π must have a hook whose southwest endpoint is (i, n − 1).
308: The northeast endpoint of such a hook must be (n, n), so it follows that H is a stationary hook of
309: π. One can check that the hook drawn in Figure 6 is another example of a stationary hook.
310: Proposition 3.1. Let π = π1 · · · πn be a permutation with a stationary hook H. Let (i, πi ) and
311: (j, πj ) be the southwest and northeast endpoints of H, respectively. Let σ = π1 · · · πi+1 πj · · · πn and
312: τ = πi+1 · · · πj−1 . We have
313:                                      |s−1 (π)| = |s−1 (σ)||s−1 (τ )|.

797: 
798:   We also have some conjectures spawning from our main theorems.
799: Conjecture 4.1. There are infinitely many infertility numbers.
800: 
801:    The proof of Theorem 3.3 made use of the fact that 27 and 95 are fertility numbers. We saw in
802: Theorem 3.4 that 27 is the smallest fertility number that is congruent to 3 modulo 4, so we are led
803: to make the following conjecture.
804: Conjecture 4.2. The smallest fertility number that is congruent to 3 modulo 4 and is greater than
805: 27 is 95.
806: 
807:    It is desirable to have more efficient methods for determining whether or not a given positive
808: integer is a fertility number. It is possible that such a method could arise by extending the tech-
809: niques used in the proof of Theorem 3.4. Such methods could certainly be useful for answering
810: the above conjectures. This also leads to the problem of improving Theorem 3.5. Given a fertility
811: number f , let N (f ) denote the smallest positive integer n such that there exists a permutation in
812: Sn with fertility f . Theorem 3.5 states that N (f ) ≤ f + 1 for every fertility number f . We would
813: like to have better estimates for N (f ). In particular, we have the following conjecture.
814: Conjecture 4.3. We have
815:                                              lim N (f )/f = 0,
816:                                             f →∞
817: 
818: where the limit is taken along the sequence of positive fertility numbers.
819: 
820:    Finally, recall that Theorem 3.1 tells us that the product of two fertility numbers is again a
821: fertility number. We would like to have additional methods for combining fertility numbers in
822: order to produce new ones.

254: 1.3    Graph powering
255: The solution proposed in this paper is as follows.
256: 
257: Definition 1 (Graph powering). We give two equivalent definitions:
258: 
259:    • Given a graph G and a positive integer r, define the r-th graph power of G as the
260:      graph G(r) with the same vertex set as G and where two vertices are connected if there
261:      exists a path of length ≤ r between them in G.
262:       Note: G(r) contains the edges of G and adds edges between any two vertices at distance
263:       ≤ r in G. Note also that one can equivalently ask for a walk of length ≤ r, rather than
264:       a path.
265: 
266:    • If A denotes the adjacency matrix of G with 1 on the diagonal (i.e., put self-loops to
267:      every vertex of G), then the adjacency matrix A(r) of G(r) is defined by
268: 
269:                                           A(r) = 1(Ar ≥ 1).                                   (1)
270: 
271:       Note: Ar has the same spectrum as A (up to rescaling), but the action of the non-
272:       linearity 1(· ≥ 1) gives the key modification to the spectrum.
273: 
274: Definition 2. For a graph G, an r-power-cut in G corresponds to a cut in G(r) , i.e.,
275: 
276:                     ∂r (S) = {u ∈ S, v ∈ S c : (A(r) )u,v = 1},      S ⊆ V (G).               (2)
277: 
278:     Note that powering is mainly targeted for sparse graphs, and is useful only if the power
279: r is not too small and not too large. If it is too small, the powered graph may not be
280: sufficiently different from the underlying graph. If it is too large, say r ≥ diameter(G), then
281: powering turns any graph to a complete graph, which destroys all the useful information.
282: However, powering with r less than the diameter but large enough    p will be effective on the
283: benchmarks (1)-(2)-(3). As a rule of thumb, one may take r = diam(G). We now discuss
284: the main insight behind graph powering.
285: 
286:    (1) Power-cuts as Bayes-like cuts. The spectral algorithms based on A, L or Lnorm can
287: be viewed as relaxations of the MAP estimator, i.e., the min-bisection:
403: i.e., to reconstruct the communities by observing the connections. The focus of this paper is
404: on the sparse regime and the weak recovery problem, defined below.
405:                                                                         [n]
406: Definition 3. In the case of k communities, an algorithm X̂ : 2( 2 ) → [k]n recovers
407: communities with accuracy f (n) in M (n) if, for (X, G) ∼ M (n) and Ωi := {v ∈ [n] : Xv = i},
408: i ∈ [k],

416: We say that an algorithm solves weak recovery if it recovers communities with accuracy
417: 1/k + Ω(1).
418: Definition 4. Let n be a positive integer (the number of vertices), k be a positive integer
419: (the number of communities), p = (p1 , . . . , pk ) be a probability vector on [k] := {1, . . . , k}
420: (the prior on the k communities) and W be a k × k symmetric matrix with entries in [0, 1]
421: (the connectivity probabilities). The pair (X, G) is drawn under SBM(n, p, W ) if X is an
422: n-dimensional random vector with i.i.d. components distributed under p, and G is an n-vertex
423: simple graph where vertices i and j are connected with probability WXi ,Xj , independently of
424: other pairs of vertices. We also define the community sets by Ωi = Ωi (X) := {v ∈ [n] : Xv =
425: i}, i ∈ [k].
426:     In this paper, we refer to the above as the “general stochastic block model” and use
427: “stochastic block model” for the version with two symmetric and sparse communities, as
428: follows.
429: Definition 5. (X, G) is drawn under SBM(n, a, b) if k = 2, W takes value a/n on the
430: diagonal and b/n off the diagonal, and if the community prior is p = {1/2, 1/2}. That is, G
431: has two balanced clusters, with edge probability a/n inside the clusters and b/n across the
432: clusters.
433:     Note that in this case, if defining X, X̂ to take values in {−1, +1}n , we have that weak
434: recovery is solvable if and only if
435: 
436:                                        |hX, X̂(G)i| = Ω(1)                                      (5)
437: 
438: with high probability.
439: Definition 6. We denote by ER(n, d) the distribution of Erdos-Renyi random graphs with
440: n vertices and edge probability d/n.

608: proof sketch, but leave the result as a conjecture. We start by defining and motivating the
609: model.
610: 
611: Definition 7. Given a positive integer n and s, t ≥ 0, we define GBM(n, s, t) as the probabil-
612: ity distribution over ordered pairs (X, G) as follows. First, each vertex v ∈ V is independently
613: assigned a community Xv ∈ {1, 2} with equal probability (as for the SBM). Then, each vertex
614: v is independently assigned a location Uv in R2 according to N (((−1)Xv s/2, 0), I2 ). Finally,
615:                                                                                  √
616: we add an edge between every pair of vertices u, v such that kUu − Uv k ≤ t/ n.
617: 
618:     The parameter s is the separation between the two isotropic Gaussian means, the first
619: centered at (−s/2, 0) and the second at (s/2, 0). In order to have a sparse graph with a
620:                                       √
621: giant, we work with the scaling t/ n for a constant t. There is no known closed form
622: expression for the threshold in order to have a giant, but such a threshold exists [Pen03].
623: We will simply assume that t is above that threshold.
624:     Note that the expected adjacency matrix of the GBM, conditioned on the vertex labels,
659: communities. This makes the GBM simpler to analyze than the model of [ABS17a]. In fact,
660: we claim the following.
661: 
662: Conjecture 1. Let s, t ≥ 0 and t such that GBM(n, s, t) has a giant component. Weak
663: recovery is efficiently solvable in GBM(n, s, t) if and only if s > 0.
664: 
665: Justification. Obviously weak recovery is not solvable if s = 0, so the claim follows by
666: showing that weak recovery is efficiently solvable if s > 0. A first simple case is when
667: the graph has two separate giant components, in which case we can simply assign each
668: component to a different community. When there is a single giant component, one does not
669: need an elaborate algorithm either.
670:     As a first case, take a vertex uniformly at random among all n vertices and assign that
671: vertex to community 1; then assign the n/2 closest vertices to that vertex (in the graph
672: distance) to community 1, and the rest to community 2. With probability 1/2 + Ω(1),
673: this will already solve weak recovery. To succeed with probability 1 − o(1), one can pick
674: two anchor vertices that are “far away” in graph-distance, and assign each to a different
675: community and the rest of the vertices to the same community as their closest anchor vertex.
707: 
708: 
709:

711: second largest eigenvector of the powered adjacency matrix A(r) with r = ε · diam(G), for ε
712: small enough, and rounding it by the sign or by the median (dividing the vertices into those
713:                                                                                         √
714: with above- and below-median sums of the entries) solves weak recovery in GBM(n, s, t/ n)
715: whenever weak recovery is solvable.
716: 
717:    We refer to Section 5.4 for a proofpplan for this conjecture, and to Section 6 for discussions
718: on how to reduce r, such as to r = diam(G).

1337: Conjecture 3. Let G ∼ GBM(n, s, t), with s and t chosen such that G has a single giant
1338: component with high probability. Also, let B be the nonbacktracking walk matrix of the main
1339: component of G. Then computing the eigenvector of B with the second largest eigenvalue
1340: and dividing the vertices of G into those with above-median sums of their entries in the
1341: eigenvector and those with below-median sums of entries does not recover communities with
1342: optimal accuracy.
1343: 
1344: Justification. We believe that the eigenvectors of G with the largest eigenvalues will each
1345: have a single small region of the graph such that the entries of the graph corresponding
1346: to edges in that region will have large absolute values. Then, the entries corresponding to
1347: directed edges outside the region will decrease exponentially with distance from that region.
1348: As a result, dividing the vertices into those that have above-median and below-median sums
1349: of entries will divide the vertices into those that are geometrically near some such region
1350: and those that are not. This is a suboptimal way to assign vertices to communities, so the
1518:   that happens, then eventually most of the variation in values will be determined by
1519:   how close vertices are to that region. Recall Figure 2 for such an outcome. In fact, we

2214: 6.2    The spectral meta-algorithm
2215: Based on the points from previous sections, we arrive at the following algorithm.
2216: 
2217: Definition 11. For a connected graph G and parameters τ, c ≥ 0, define ψ(G) as the matrix
2218: output by the following procedure, where r := c ln3 (diam(G)).
2219: 
2220:   1. Cleaning:
2221: 
2222:          • Delete all vertices of degree more than τ .
2223:          • Repeat the following until no vertex is deleted:
2224:              – Delete all the leaves from G;
2225:                           √
2226:              – Delete all r-segments from G: If G has a vertex v such that every vertex
2227:                       √
2228:                within r edges of v has degree 2 or less, delete v.
2229:          • If the graph has multiple components, delete all but the largest component.

2336: 7.2   Conjectures
2337: We showed in this paper that raising a graph to a power proportional to its diameter and
2338: then taking the second eigenvector of its adjacency matrix solves weak recovery on the SBM.
2339: We also believe that it solves weak recovery on the GBM as stated by Conjecture 2 and
2340: heuristically justified.
2341:    However, since the power used is inconveniently large, we would prefer to use the spectral
2342: meta-algorithm because it allows us to use a power that is polylogarithmic in the graph’s
2343: diameter. We believe that the spectral meta-algorithm also succeeds at weak recovery on
2344: these models as stated below.
2345: Conjecture 10. Choose a and b such that there is an efficient algorithm that solves
2346: weak recovery on SBM(n, a, b). Then the spectral meta-algorithm solves weak recovery on
2347: SBM(n, a, b).
2348: Conjecture 11. For any s and r such that GBM(n, s, t) has a giant component, there exists
2349: δ > 1/2 such that the following holds. The spectral meta-algorithm recovers communities
2350: with accuracy δ on GBM(n, s, t) and there is no algorithm that recovers communities with
2351: accuracy δ + Ω(1) on GBM(n, s, t).
2352: 
2353: 
2354: 8     Proofs
2355: 8.1   Proof of Theorem 1
2356: Our proof of Theorem 1 is based on the following theorem of Massoulié [Mas14], which is
2357: analogous to Theorems 1 and 2, but for the matrix A{r} counting self-avoiding walks (i.e.,
2358: paths) of length r:
2359: 
2360:                                              44
2361: 
2767: based on counting closed walks, as in the approach of J. Friedman for the Alon-Boppana
2768: Theorem [Fri93].
2769:                      (r)
2770: Definition 12. t2k is the minimum, taken over all vertices x ∈ V (G), of the number of
2771: closed walks of length 2k in G(r) terminating at x.
2772:                                                                (r)
2773:     First, we will bound λ2 (G(r) ) in terms of t2k .
2774: 
2775: Theorem 7. Let G be a graph and r ≥ 1. Let D be the diameter of G and let k satisfy
2776: 2k < dD/re. Then
2777:                                                 (r)
2778:                                 λ2 (G(r) )2k ≥ t2k .
2779: 
2780: Proof. Let A(r) be the adjacency matrix for G(r) . Let f1 be an eigenfunction satisfying
2781: A(r) f1 = λ1 (G(r) )f1 . By the Perron-Frobenius theorem, we can choose f1 so that f1 (z) > 0
2782: for all z ∈ V .
2783:     Because A(r) is symmetric, we can express λ2 (G(r) ) by the Rayleigh quotient
2919: a system of canonical paths between neighbors in G(r) that we follow when generating an
2920: underlying walk on G.
2921: 
2922: Definition 13 (Canonical i-path). For all ordered pairs of distinct vertices (v, w) with
2923: dG (v, w) = i, we arbitrarily choose a canonical path of length i from v to w.
2924: 
2925:     We now define the set of tree-like walks in G(r) , which we will later count. The tree-like
2926: walks are analogous to walks on the cover graph of G. We first define the related concept of
2927: a sequence of canonically constructed walks.
2928: 
2929: Definition 14 (Sequence of r-canonically constructed walks). Let k > 0. A sequence
2930: of r-canonically constructed walks is a sequence W0 , . . . , W2k of walks that is constructed
2931: according to the following process:
2932:       Assume Wi has length at least r, so it can be expressed as Wi = . . . , v0 , v1 , . . . , vr . We
2933: can then make a move of type m to obtain Wi+1 , where m is an integer satisfying 0 ≤ m ≤ r.
2934:       In a move of type m, we start by removing the last r − m vertices from Wi , leaving
2935: . . . v0 , v1 , . . . , vm . Let y be a vertex with dG (vm , y) = m and dG (vm−1 , y) ≥ m. We can
2936: 
2937:                                                       54
2938: 
2939: append to our sequence the canonical walk from vm to y of length m to obtain Wi+1 . Observe
2940: that dG (vr , y) ≤ dG (vr , vm ) + dG (vm , y) = (r − m) + m = r, so that vr and y are neighbors
2941: in G(r) .
2942:     We require W0 = W2k = x. To find W1 , we are allowed to make a move of type r
2954:     Also note that, given Wi is long enough that moves of type m are allowed, the number
2955: of possible moves of type m that can generate distinct possibilities for Wi+1 is at least δ (m) .
2956: 
2957: Definition 15 (Tree-like walk in G(r) ). Let k > 0. A tree-like walk of length 2k in G(r)
2958: is the closed walk made by the endpoints of an sequence of r-canonically constructed walks
2959: starting at x and having length 2k.
2960: 
2961:    We will count the number of tree-like walks in G(r) . First, it is useful to prove an
2962: equivalence between the tree-like walk x0 , . . . , x2k and underlying sequence of r-canonically
2963: constructed walks W0 , . . . , W2k .
2964:

2967: r-canonically constructed walks and outputs the tree-like walk consisting of its final vertices
2968: is a bijection between W and X .

23:                                                  an analog of Kleitman’s theorem, Samotij and Sudakov asked whether among subsets
24:                                                  of Z2n of given size M , the sets that minimize the number of Schur triples (2-cubes)
25:                                                  are those that are obtained by filling up the largest layers consecutively. We prove the

34: 
35:                                           1.1. Theorems in Zn2 . We will consider four important results in the Boolean lattice,
36:                                           which we identify in the usual way with the elements of Zn2 .
37:                                              We begin with Sperner’s theorem from 1928, a cornerstone result in extremal com-
38:                                           binatorics. Here we recall that two distinct sets A, B ⊆ [n] form a 2-chain if they are
39:                                           comparable, i.e. if A ⊂ B or B ⊂ A. Similarly, k distinct sets form a k-chain if any two
139: conjecture appeared in the very last line of Kleitman’s paper!
140:                                                    3
141: 
142: Conjecture 1.7 (Analog of Kleitman’s theorem in Z2n ). Let n ≥ 2 and M be integers.
143: Amongst all families F ⊆ Z2n of size |F| = M , centred families minimise the number of
144: 2-cubes.
145: 
146:    For M ≤ 2n−1 Conjecture 1.7 is trivial, our modest contribution is that the first non-
147: trivial case of this conjecture is true. Theorem 1.8 states that the M = 2n−1 + 1 case of
148: Conjecture 1.7 is true.
149: 
150: Theorem 1.8. All sets of size 2n−1 + 1 in Z2n contain at least 3 · 2n−1 Schur triples.
151: 
152:   Following [19], we define the number of 2-cubes in a set A ⊆ Z2n by
153:                                   
154:                         ST(A) = (x, y, z) ∈ A3 : x + y = z ,
155: 
156: so that if x + y = z and x 6= y then we consider (x, y, z) and (y, x, z) as different triples.
157: Observe that a centred set of size 2n−1 +1, e.g. the set L1 ∪{2}, contains precisely 3·2n−1
158: such 2-cubes, hence Theorem 1.8 is sharp. The number of k-cubes in a set can be defined
159: similarly, and indeed we conjecture that the analog of Samotij’s theorem (Theorem 1.4)
160: also holds.
161: 
162: Conjecture 1.9 (Analog of Samotij’s theorem in Z2n ). Let n ≥ k ≥ 2 and M be integers.
163: Amongst all families F ⊆ Z2n of size |F| = M , centred families minimise the number of
164: 2k -cubes.
165: 
166: 1.3. When d is not a power of two. While we have seen that 2k−1 -cubes in Z2n
167: correspond to k-chains in Zn2 , the case of d-cubes where d is not a power of two does not
168: seem to have an analog in Zn2 . Hence it is not obvious what the extremal constructions
169: should be, and indeed this case exhibits a much more interesting behaviour. Table 1
170: illustrates our conjectured largest d-cube-free constructions, which we refer to as Cd . We
171: will always assume that n is sufficiently large for our constructions to make sense, in
172: particular n ≥ d is always enough. Recall that Theorem 1.6 establishes that Cd is indeed
173: best possible for d = 2, 4, 8, . . ..
174:   We give an explicit description of this construction Cd for all d in Section 3. While
175: we cannot prove that these constructions are best possible (except when d = 2ℓ ), we can
204:    Given Theorem 1.10, we would be surprised if these constructions were not best pos-
205: sible amongst all sets.

208: free subset of Z2n .

820:    Our goal in this section is to prove Theorem 1.8. Given n, let S ⊂ Z2n be a set of
821: size M = 2n−1 + 1. Recall that for M ≤ 2n−1 the centred family of size M is a subset
822: of L1 and hence contains no Schur triples, our M = 2n−1 + 1 case is the first non-trivial
823: case of Conjecture 1.7. Our goal is to prove that any family of size 2n−1 + 1 contains
824: at least 3 · 2n−1 Schur triples, which is the number of Schur triples in the centred set
825: S = L1 ∪ {2}. We will use for all i the notation Si = S ∩ Li where Li is the i’th layer
826: as before, and similarly Si+ = S ∩ (Li+1 ∪ Li+2 ∪ . . . ∪ Ln+1 ) = Si+1 ∪ Si+2 ∪ . . . ∪ Sn+1 .
827: Note that whenever the numbers x, y, z form a Schur triple, they cannot be in three
828: different layers, nor all in the same layer.

830:                                        

933:                            5. Conclusion and open questions
934: 
935:    The main conjectures raised in the present paper are the analog of Samotij’s theorem
936: in Z2n (Conjecture 1.9) and that the constructions Cd are best possible (Conjecture 1.12).
937: The first open case of Conjecture 1.12 is the d = 3 case, which we restate here.
938: 
939: Conjecture 5.1. The largest 3-cube-free family in Z2n has size (5/8) · 2n .
940: 
941:   Recall that L1 ∪L3 is 3-cube-free, so this conjecture, if true, is sharp. Using Gurobi [12]
942: we could check that for n ≤ 7 the following stronger conjecture is also true.
943: 
944: Conjecture 5.2. If S ⊂ Z2n is a set of size |S| > (5/8) · 2n then there exist x, y such
945: that Σ∗ {x, x, x} ⊂ A or Σ∗ {x, 3x, y} ⊂ A.
946:                                                   21
947: 
948:   If true, Conjecture 5.2 might be easier to prove than Conjecture 5.1. Our hope is that
949: an insightful proof of Conjecture 5.1 may quickly lead to a full proof of Conjecture 1.12.
950:    Following [2, 7, 13], a natural complementary problem is to determine for all M, n
951: (and k) the set S ⊂ Z2n of size |S| = M with the largest number of Schur triples (or
952: k-chains). In the Boolean lattice for a wide range of M the constructions with the
953: largest number of comparable pairs are essentially towers of cubes. In Z2n , say that a
954: set S ⊂ Z2n is anti-centred if there exists an i ∈ [n + 1] such that for all j with n ≥ j > i
955: we have Lj ⊆ S, and for all j with i > j ≥ 1 we have Lj ∩ S = ∅. It seems plausible that
956: anti-centred families maximize the number of Schur triples and perhaps even k-cubes.
957: Note that if M = 2n−ℓ for some ℓ then an anti-centred family is the union of the n − ℓ + 1
958: smallest layers of Z2n and hence contains M 2 Schur triples and M k distinct k-cubes,
959: both of which are optimal.
960:    Another related problem, following [8, 15], is to determine the smallest maximal k-
961: cube free set in Z2n . That is, the smallest S ⊂ Z2n that is k-cube free, but the additon
962: of any new element to S makes it not k-cube-free. Here we do not even have a good

153:                                        2. Conjectures
154:    We formulate conjectures for fusion systems which are motivated by conjectural or known
155: statements in block theory. For each of these conjectures, the link with a block theoretic
156: conjecture is made either via AWC or via the Ordinary Weight Conjecture, the statement of
157: which will be recalled below. Note that by work of Robinson the Ordinary Weight Conjecture
158: implies the AWC.
159:    These conjectures make precise the idea that the gap between various local-global block
160: theoretic conjectures is purely local. Proving or disproving any of these is a win-win scenario.
161: If one can prove one of these conjectures at the fusion system level, then one would get that
162: AWC (or the ordinary weight conjecture) implies the corresponding block theoretic version.
163: If on the other hand one could disprove any of these, one would either have found a counter
164: example to the corresponding block theoretic conjecture, or one would have found a way to
165: distinguish exotic fusion systems from block realizable fusion systems. Either outcome would
166: be interesting.
167:    We keep the notation of the previous section. Let F be a saturated fusion system on a finite
168: p-group S and let α be an F -compatible family (see Definition 4.1). Recall from Proposition
169: 4.5 that if (S, F , α) is realized by a block B which satisfies AWC, then w(F , α) = ℓ(B), and
170: if all Brauer correspondents of B also satisfy AWC, then k(F , α) = k(B).
171: Conjecture 2.1. Let F be a saturated fusion system on a finite p-group S and let α be an
172: F -compatible family. Then k(F , α) ⩽ |S|.
173:   By the above remark, if (S, F , α) is realizable by a block B such that AWC holds for all
174: B-Brauer pairs, then Conjecture 2.1 holds if and only if B satisfies Brauer’s k(B)-conjecture,
175: which predicts the inequality k(B) ⩽ |S|. Also, note that by Theorem 1.1, the inequality of
176: Conjecture 2.1 is equivalent to the inequality m(F , α) ⩽ |S|. In view of Theorem 1.2 and
177: Corollary 1.3 (see also Conjecture 2.3), one could consider versions of the inequality with
178: k(F , α) replaced by m(F , α).
179: Conjecture 2.2. Let F be a saturated fusion system on a finite p-group S and let α be an
180: F -compatible family. Then w(F , α) ⩽ ps , where s is the sectional rank of S.
181:   If (S, F , α) is realizable by a block B such that AWC holds for B, then the above is
182: equivalent to the statement that B satsifies the conjecture by Malle and Robinson [MR17]
183: predicting the inequality ℓ(B) ⩽ ps . Conjecture 2.2 has been shown to hold for the exotic
184: Solomon fusion systems by Lynd and Semeraro [LS17].
185:   Next, we refine the integers w(F , α), m(F , α), m∗ (F , α) by taking into account defects of
186: characters. For Q a subgroup of S and d a non-negative integer, we set
187:                         IrrdK (Q) := {µ ∈ IrrdK (Q) | vp (|Q|/µ(1)) = d} ;
188: this is the set of ordinary irreducible characters of Q of defect d. Note that this set is
189: OutF (Q)-stable. As in the previous section, we denote by NQ the set of nonempty normal
190: chains of p-subgroups of OutF (Q) starting with the trivial subgroup of OutF (Q). Given such
191: 
192:                          WEIGHT CONJECTURES FOR FUSION SYSTEMS                                 5
193: 
194: a chain σ and an irreducible character µ of Q, we denote by I(σ) and I(σ, µ) the stabilisers
195: of σ and of the pair (σ, µ) in OutF (Q).
196:    Given a saturated fusion system F on a finite p-group S, an F -compatible family α, and
219: F and family of Külshammer–Puig classes α, then m(F , α) = k(B), the number of ordinary
220: irreducible characters of G associated with B. On the other hand, AWC predicts that k(F , α)
221: equals k(B). This leads to the following conjecture.
222: Conjecture 2.3. Let F be a saturated fusion system on a finite p-group S and let α be an
223: F -compatible family. We have
224:                                  k(F , α) = m(F , α).
225:   Now Corollary 1.3 may be restated as follows.
226: Theorem 2.4. Suppose that AWC holds for all blocks. Then Conjecture 2.3 holds for all
227: (S, F , α), S a finite p-group, F a saturated fusion system on S and α an F -compatible family.
228:   By [Rob96], [Rob04], AWC is equivalent to SOWC in the sense that a minimal counter-
229: example to AWC is a a minimal counter-example to the other. The difficult implication is
230: that AWC implies SOWC. Theorem 2.4 may be viewed as an extension of Robinson’s result
231: to arbitrary fusion systems.
232: Conjecture 2.5. Let F be a saturated fusion system on a finite p-group S and let α be an
233: F -compatible family. For each positive integer d, we have m(F , α, d) ≥ 0.
234: Remark 2.6. With the above notation, suppose that d is the integer such that |S| = pd .
235: The only chain contributing to the expression for m(F , α, d) is the chain S of length zero
236: and the contribution of this chain is a strictly positive integer. This is because OutF (S) is a
237: p′ -group.
238:   We consider next Brauer’s height zero conjecture.
239: 
240: 6        RADHA KESSAR, MARKUS LINCKELMANN, JUSTIN LYND, AND JASON SEMERARO
241: 
242: Proposition 2.7. Let F be a saturated fusion system on a finite p-group S and let α be an
243: F -compatible family. Suppose that S is abelian of order pd . Then m(F , α, d′) = 0 for all
244: d′ 6= d.
245: Proof. Since S is abelian, S is the only F -centric subgroup of S, and all characters of S are
246: linear, hence of defect d. The result follows.                                              
247: Conjecture 2.8. Let F be a saturated fusion system on a finite p-group S and let α be an
248: F -compatible family. Suppose that S is nonabelian of order pd . Then m(F , α, d′) 6= 0 for
249: some d′ 6= d.
250:    If S is non-abelian and (S, F , α) is realized by a block B satisfying OWC, then the above
251: is equivalent to the statement that B satisfies Brauer’s height zero conjecture. Note that
252: Navarro and Tiep [NT13] have proved that the height zero conjecture is a consequence of
253: the Dade projective conjecture and of the fact that the Brauer height zero conjecture has
254: been checked for finite quasi-simple groups [KM17]. Eaton has proved in [Eat04] that the
255: Dade projective conjecture is equivalent to the OWC in the sense that a minimal counter-
256: example to one is a minimal counter-example to the other. Thus the above conjecture for
257: block realizable triples is a consequence of OWC.
258: Conjecture 2.9. Let F be a saturated fusion system on a finite p-group S and let α be an
259: F -compatible family. Suppose that S is nonabelian of order pd . Let r > 0 be the smallest
260: positive integer such that S has a character of degree pr . Then r is the smallest positive
261: integer such that m(F , α, d − r) 6= 0.
262:   If (S, F , α) is realized by a block B satisfying OWC, then the above is equivalent to the
263: statement that B satisfies the conjecture by Eaton and Moreto in [EM14].
264: Conjecture 2.10. Let F be a saturated fusion system on a finite p-group S of order pd and
265: let α be an F -compatible family. Then
266:     (1) k(F , α)/m(F , α, d) is at most the number of conjugacy classes of [S, S].
267:     (2) k(F , α)/w(F , α) is at most the number of conjugacy classes of S.
268:   If (S, F , α) is realized by a block B satisfying OWC, then the above is equivalent to
269: the statement that B satisfies the conjecture of Malle and Navarro in [MN06]. Similar to
270: Conjecture 2.1, one could consider versions of the above inequalities with k(F , α) replaced
271: by m(F , α) or m∗ (F , α).
272:    If F is p-solvable (i.e. if F is constrained with p-solvable model) then for any F -compatible
273: family α, the triple (S, F , α) is realizable by a block of a p-solvable group (see Proposition
274: 4.8). The OWC has been shown to hold for blocks of p-solvable groups by Robinson, and
275: AWC for p-solvable groups was proved earlier by Okuyama. The k(B) conjecture for finite p-
276: solvable groups was proved in [GMRS04] and the height zero conjecture for p-solvable groups
277: was shown to hold by Gluck and Wolf [GW84]. Thus Conjectures 2.1, 2.3,2.5, 2.8 all hold
278: for solvable fusion systems. If moreover F = NF (S), then for any F -compatible family α,
279: the triple (S, F , α) is realizable by a block of a finite group G containing S as a normal (and
280: Sylow) subgroup, hence Conjecture 2.10 holds by [MR17, Theorem 2] and Conjecture 2.9
281: holds by [EM14].
282:

319: Lemma 3.1 (Thompson’s A × B Lemma). Let S be a finite p-group and A × B ≤ Aut(S)
320: be such that A is a p′ -group and B is a p-group. If A centralizes CS (B), then A = 1.
321: Proof. See [Gor80, Theorem 5.3.4].                                                          
322:    We will use standard terminology on saturated fusion systems, as can be found in many
362: extension τ .
363: Proof. Since AutS (Q′ ) is the full inverse image of OutS (Q′ ) under the canonical surjection
364: AutF (Q′ ) → OutF (Q′ ), the two conditions on the image of R are indeed equivalent. Hence,
365: R ⩽ Nϕ in the notation of [AKO11, Definition 2.2]. Since each F -centric subgroup is fully
366: F -centralised, the extension axiom of saturation yields the first assertion.
367:     If τ and τ ′ are two F -morphisms extending ϕ, then one may find z ∈ Z(Q) such that
368: τ ′ = τ ◦ cz by [BLO03, Lemma A.8]. Since z ∈ Q ⩽ R, this shows the second assertion. 

45: 
46:    There is a massive gap between Conjecture 1 and the unconditional results. Through
47: an equidistribution theorem for Hecke eigenvalues, Serre [Ser97] was the first to es-
48: tablish that dk,N → ∞ as k + N → ∞. Subsequently, by making Serre’s equidis-
49: tribution theorem
50:             √       effective, Royer [Roy00] and Murty–Sinha [MS09] showed that
51: dk,N ≫k,p log log N for any p ∤ N. In the particular case where N lies in a re-
52: stricted set of primes, this bound has been improved by several authors. Extending
53: a method of Mazur to all even weights, Billerey and Menares [BM16, Theorem 2]
54: obtained that dk,N ≫k log N when N ≥ (k + 1)4 is in a explicit set primes of lower
55: natural density ≥ 3/4. When the lower bound is fixed in advance and one looks for
56: a level with a given number of prime divisors attaining it, see also [DJUR15]. When
57: N ≡ 7 (mod 8) is prime, Lipnowski–Schaeffer [LS18, Corollary 1.7] also showed that
58: d2,N ≫ log log N, which can be significantly improved for N in certain subsets of the
59: primes under certain well-known conjectures and heuristics.

257: The notion of ω-stable structure will appear a couple of times in this paper, but will not play
258: an important role and can be treated as a black box. For the sake of completeness, we give a
259: definition and state the few facts that we will use.
260: Definition 2.7. Let M be ω-categorical and D ⊆ M k a parameter-definable set. We define the
261: Morley rank of D—denoted MR( D )—which takes values in Ord ∪ {∞} by induction as follows:
262: 
263:    • MR( D ) ≥ 0 if and only if D is non-empty;
264:    • MR( D ) ≥ α + 1, for an ordinal α, if and only if there are disjoint parameter-definable
265:      subsets F0 , F1 , F2 , . . . of D each of Morley rank at least α;
266:    • MR( D ) ≥ λ, for λ a limit ordinal if and only if MR( D ) ≥ α for all α < λ.
267: 
268: 
269:                                                      5
270: 
271:     We say that M is ω-stable if MR( M ) < α for some ordinal α.
272:     If M is ω-stable, then it is stable. The ω-stable, ω-categorical structures have been studied in a
273: sequence of fundamental works (see e.g., [Zil], [CHL85], [Lac87], [Hru89]) and are now very well

747: 4      Anti-Ld(2, 1)-labeling of graphs
748: 
749: Given a simple graph G = (V, E) and a positive number d, an Ld (2, 1)-labeling of G is a function
750: f : V (G) → [0, ∞) such that whenever x, y ∈ V are adjacent, if |f (x)−f (Y )| ≥ 2d, and whenever
751: the distance between x and y is two, if |f (x) − f (Y )| ≥ d. The Ld (2, 1)-labeling number λ(G, d)
752: is the smallest number m such that G has an Ld (2, 1)-labeling f with max{f (v) : v ∈ V } = m.
753: When d = 1, the Ld (2, 1)-labeling problem is the L(2, 1)-labeling problem. The L(2, 1)-labeling
754: problem of graphs has been discussed for many graph families, see [2, 3, 4, 7, 8, 9].

SOURCE 1810.10044.txt
74: term more precisely is not easy, even for relatively simple graphs H. It is plausible to conjecture
75: (see [3]) that for every fixed graph H there is a constant cH such that every H-free graph G with
76: m edges has a cut with surplus at least Θ(mcH ), i.e., there is both a lower bound and an infinite
77: sequence of example showing that exponent cH can not be improved. This conjecture is very
78: difficult. Even in the case H = K3 determining the correct error term took almost twenty years.
79: Following the works of [12, 19, 20], Alon [1] proved that every m-edge triangle free graph has a cut
80: with surplus of order m4/5 and that this is tight up to constant factors. There are several other
81: forbidden graphs H for which we know quite accurately the error term for the extremal Max-Cut
82: problem in H-free graphs. For example, it was proved in [3], that if H = Cr for r = 4, 6, 10 then
83: cH = r+1
84:        r+2 . The answer is also known in the case when H is a complete bipartite graph K2,s or K3,s
85: (see [3] for details).
86:

1259: 
1260:  C     Max-Cut upper bound matching Conjecture 1.4
1261:     Alon, Krivelevich, and Sudakov [3] showed that, when H is a forest, the Max-Cut of H-free
1262:  graphs is ( 21 + c) · m for some c > 0 independent of m. This result holds independently of the
1263:  density of the graph, and in particular also applies to d-degenerate graphs, where the constant in
1264:  the lower order term is independent of d.
1265:     For d-degenerate graphs, we observe that forests are the only graphs for which this is true:
1266:  whenever H contains a cycle, there exist infinitely many H-free d-degenerate (and, in fact, maximum
1267:  degree d) graphs G on n vertices with Max-Cut no larger than ( 12 + √cd ) · m(G). In particular,
1268:  Conjecture 1.4 is optimal (up to a constant depending on H in the lower order term) if it is true
1269:  when H is not a forest.

79: Definition 1.1 Let G be an r-graph on [n] and let ~x = (x1 , . . . , xn ) ∈ [0, ∞)n . For every subgraph
80: H ⊆ G, define the Lagrangian function
81:                                                  X Y

83:                                                        e∈E(H) i∈e
84: 
85: The Lagrangian of G, denoted by λ(G), is defined as
86: 
87:                                        λ(G) = max{λ(G, ~x) : ~x ∈ ∆},
88: 
89: where
90:                      ∆ = {~x = (x1 , x2 , . . . , xn ) ∈ [0, 1])n : x1 + x2 + · · · + xn = 1}.
91: 
92:    The value xi is called the weight of the vertex i and a vector ~x ∈ ∆ is called a feasible weight vector
146: 
147:     For example, to determine the Turán density of K43 (a long standing conjecture of Turán) is equivalent
148: to determine the Lagrangian density of K43 .
149:     The Lagrangian density of the enlargement of a tree satisfying Erdős-Sos’s conjecture is determined
150: by Sidorenko [20] and Brandt-Irwin-Jiang [2]. Pikhurko [18] determined the Lagrangian density of a
151: 4-uniform tight path of length 2 and this led to confirm the conjecture of Frankl-Füredi on the Turán
152: number of its extension, the r-uniform genearlized triangle for the case r = 4. Norin and Yepremyan [17]
153: determined for r = 5 or 6 by extending the earlier result of Frankl-Füredi in [5]. Jenssen [10] determined
154: the Lagrangian density of a path of length 2 formed by two edges intersecting at r − 2 vertices for
155: r = 3, 4, 5, 6, 7. Hefetz and Keevash [8] determined the Lagrangian density of a 3-uniform matching of
156: size 2. Jiang-Peng-Wu [11] obtained for any 3-uniform matching. The case for an r-uniform matching
157: of size 2 was given in [1] (independently, in [24] for r = 4). In [25, 9, 3], we obtained the Lagrangian
158: densities of a 3-uniform linear path of length 3 or 4, the disjoint union of a 3-uniform linear path of
159: length 2 or 3 and a 3-uniform matching, and the disjoint union of a 3-uniform tight path of length 2 and
160: a 3-uniform matching.These were all the known results on Lagrangian densities.

326: r-wise (t + r − 1)-intersecting.

1382: min |F \ C(n, k, t)|, where the minimum is taken over all possible orderings of the ground set.

672: Later an asymptotic version was defined by Guardo, Harbourne, and Van Tuyl [17] as
694: the polynomial ring. This study was continued for ideals whose generators are powers of general
695: linear forms in [29]. There the following conjecture was proposed in order to complete this line of
696: investigations. Throughout this section K denotes a field of characteristic zero.
697: Conjecture 6.1 ([29, Conjecture 6.6]). Consider the ideal I = (xd0 , · · · , xdn , Ld ) of the polynomial
698: ring R = K[x0 , . . . , xn ], where L ∈ R is a general linear form. If n ≥ 8 and n is even, then the
699: ring R/I fails the WLP if and only if d > 1.
700:   If confirmed we have the following complete description of the presence of the weak Lefschetz
701: property for almost complete intersections generated by uniform powers of general linear forms.
702: Remark 6.2. We adopt the notation of the above conjecture and list references for the statements
703: that have been established. We assume d ≥ 1 (since the case d = 0 is not interesting).
704:       • If n ≤ 2 then R/I has the weak Lefschetz property for every d ≥ 1 (see [20, 34, 29]).
705:       • If n = 3 then R/I has the weak Lefschetz property if and only if d ∈ {1, 2} (see [29]).
706:       • If n = 4 then R/I has the weak Lefschetz property if and only if d ∈ {1, 2, 3} (see [29]).
707:       • If n ≥ 5 is odd then R/I has the weak Lefschetz property if and only if d = 1 (see [29]).
708:                                                    13
709: 
710:     • If n = 6 then R/I has the weak Lefschetz property if and only if d ∈ {1, 2} (see [7] for
711:        d = 3 and [29] for d 6= 3) .
712:     • If n ≥ 8 is even then R/I has the weak Lefschetz property if and only if d = 1 (known for
713:        d = 2 by [26]).
714:   The last item is a restatement of Conjecture 6.1. It is open for d ≥ 3.
715:    The goal of this section is to provide further evidence by proving Conjecture 6.1 in new cases
716: and by reducing it to establishing numerical statements for the most part. The verification of these
717: numerical properties has eluded us for the most part. We hope that highlighting them will motivate
718: further investigations.

985:                             7. F INAL C OMMENTS AND O PEN P ROBLEMS
986:    We conclude by discussing some problems motivated by this work. We begin with a combina-
987: torial problem concerning Eulerian numbers.
988:    It is well-known that the Eulerian numbers satisfy
989:                                      A(n, k) = A(n, n − k − 1).
990: Moreover, fixing n the sequence (A(n, k))k∈N0 is unimodal. More precisely, if n = 2m − 1 is
991: odd, then (A(n, k))k∈N0 is strictly increasing on the interval [0, m − 1] and strictly decreasing on
992: [m − 1, 2m − 1]. Thus, the maximum value of the sequence is attained precisely if k = m − 1.

70: order such that for all i, fmax (Gi ) is exponentially smaller than 2µ(Gi )/2 .

74: even order groups G with |G| > n0 ,

106: 2.2. Number theoretic tools.
107: Definition 2.1. Let G be an abelian group of order n.
108:     • If n is divisible by a prime p ≡ 2 (mod 3), then we say that G is type I(p), for
109:       smallest such p.
110:     • If n is not divisible by any prime p ≡ 2 (mod 3), but 3|n, then we say that G is
111:        type II.
112:     • Otherwise, G is type III.
113:   The following theorem was proved for type I and II groups by Diananda and Yap [6].
114: Later, it was proved for some special type III groups (see [13, 16, 17]), and for all
115: type III groups in [9].
116: Theorem 2.2. For all finite abelian groups G, if G is type I(p) then µ(G) = 13 + 3p   1
117:                                                                                         .
118:                                        1                                         1    1
119: Otherwise, if G is type II then µ(G) = 3 . Finally, if G is type III then µ(G) = 3 − 3m ,
120: where m is the exponent (largest order of any element) of G.

106:    We refer to [14] for the definition of the terms above, however we will not need
107: these notions in this paper. Informally, the conjecture asserts that on most short
108: intervals, λ does not exhibit significant correlation with any s-step nilsequence (of
109: bounded complexity). The estimate (4) proven in [24] essentially corresponds to the
110: case s = 0 of Conjecture 1.1; this is currently the only case of the conjecture that is
111: completely settled.
112:    In this paper we make a first step in going beyond the case of s = 0 and establish
113: the case s = 1 of Conjecture 1.1 when H = X θ with θ > 0 fixed but otherwise
114: arbitrarily small. Let us first re-state our main result for the Liouville function in a
115: more elementary fashion.
116: Theorem 1.2 (Local Fourier Uniformity for s = 1 at scale X θ ). Let θ ∈ (0, 1) be
117: given and set H = X θ . Then

124:    We restrict attention here to the regime θ ∈ (0, 1), since the case θ ≥ 1 follows
125: from the classical work of Davenport [5] (and see [11], [13] for the s = 2 and s > 2
126: cases respectively of Conjecture 1.1 for this range of θ). Informally, Theorem 1.2
127: asserts that on most intervals of the form [x, x + xθ ], the Liouville function λ(n) does
128: not exhibit singificant correlation with linear phases e(αn); it can easily be shown to
129: imply the s = 1 case of Conjecture 1.1 in the range H ≥ X θ by approximating the
130: 1-step nilsequence n 7→ F (g n−bxc x0 ) by a Fourier series.
131:    Previously, Theorem 1.2 was known unconditionally only      P for θ > 5/8 from the
132: work of Zhan [36], who showed that as X → ∞ the bound x<n≤x+H λ(n)e(−αn) =
133: o(XH) holds pointwise in x ∈ [X, 2X] for H > X 5/8+ε . It is likely that our method
134: can be pushed to reach H = exp((log x)1−δ ) for some δ > 0, and conditionally on
135: the Riemann Hypothesis one should in principle be able to reach H = (log X)ψ(X)
136: for any function ψ(X) going to infinity arbitrarily slowly with X, although this may
137: require a more careful reworking of the arguments here. It may be possible to extend
138: the methods to this paper to also cover the s > 1 case (again with H = X θ for any
139: fixed θ > 0); we plan to investigate this direction in future work.

78:    To investigate the behavior of a graph-theoretical parameter on d-regular graphs
79: avoiding the influence of particular substructures, two traditional ways are to consider
80: its typical value, namely its value for a randomly chosen d-regular graph, and to consider
81: its value on graphs with large girth, where the girth of a graph is the length of a shortest
82: cycle in the graph. Properties of random regular graphs have been intensively studied
83: (see [27] for a survey of results in this direction). The effect of the girth on the value
84: of graph parameters has also been widely studied, two classical references involving the
85: chromatic number are Erdős [11] and Grötzsch [13].
86:    Regarding the effect of large girth on the total domination number of a d-regular
87: graph G, Henning and Yeo [17]     showed  that, if G is an n-vertex graph with δ(G) ≥ 2

109: Definition 1.1. Let I 6= (0) be a graded ideal of S. The function δI : N+ × N+ → Z given by
110:                          (
111:                           deg(S/I) − max{deg(S/(I, F )) | F ∈ Fd,r } if Fd,r 6= ∅,
112:             δI (d, r) :=
113:                           deg(S/I)                                     if Fd,r = ∅,
114: is called the generalized minimum distance function of I, or simply the GMD function of I.
115: 
116:    This notion recovers (Proposition 3.14) and refines the algebraic-geometric notion of degree.
117: If r = 1 one obtains the minimum distance function of I [24]. In this case we denote δI (d, 1)
118: simply by δI (d) and Fd,r by Fd .
119:    The aims of this paper are to study the behavior of δI , to introduce algebraic methods to
120: estimate this function, and to study the algebraic invariants (minimum distance function, v-
121: number, regularity, socle degrees) of special ideals that we call Geramita ideals. Recall that an
122: ideal I ⊂ S is called unmixed if all its associated primes have the same height; this notion is
125: forms. The defining ideal of the scheme of a finite sets of projective fat points and the unmixed
126: monomial ideals of dimension 1 are examples of Geramita ideals.
127:    The following function is closely related to δI as illustrated in Eq. (1.1).
128: Definition 1.2. Let I be a graded ideal of S. The function hypI : N+ × N+ → N, given by
129:                                (
130:                                 max{deg(S/(I, F )) | F ∈ Fd,r } if Fd,r 6= ∅,
131:                 hypI (d, r) :=
132:                                 0                               if Fd,r = ∅,
133: is called the hyp function of I.
134: 
135:    If r = 1, we denote hypI (d, 1) by hypI (d). Finding upper bounds for hypI (d, r) is equivalent
136: to finding lower bounds for δI (d, r). If I(X) is the vanishing ideal of a finite set X of reduced
137: projective points, then hypI(X) (d, 1) is the maximum number of points of X contained in a hyper-
138: surface of degree d (see [33, Remarks 2.7 and 3.4]). There is a similar geometric interpretation
139: for hypI(X) (d, r) [14, Lemma 3.4].
140:    To compute δI (d, r) is a difficult problem even when K is a finite field and r = 1. However,
141: we show that a generalized footprint function, which is more computationally tractable, gives
144: to ≺. The footprint of S/I is also called the Gröbner éscalier of I. Given integers d, r ≥ 1, let
145: M≺,d,r be the set of all subsets M of ∆≺ (I)d := ∆≺ (I) ∩ Sd with r distinct elements such that
146: (in≺ (I) : (M )) 6= in≺ (I).
147: Definition 1.3. The generalized footprint function of I, denoted fpI , is the function fpI : N+ ×
148: N+ → Z given by
149:                    (
150:                     deg(S/I) − max{deg(S/(in≺ (I), M )) | M ∈ M≺,d,r } if M≺,d,r 6= ∅,
151:      fpI (d, r) :=                                                                        .
152:                     deg(S/I)                                               if M≺,d,r = ∅.
153: 
154:    If r = 1 one obtains the footprint function of I that was studied in [28] from a theoretical
155: point of view (see [24, 25] for some applications). In this case we denote fpI (d, 1) simply by
156: fpI (d) and M≺,d,r by M≺,d . The importance of the footprint function is that it gives a lower
157: bound on the generalized minimum degree function (Theorem 3.9) and it is computationally
158: 
159: 4      S. M. COOPER, A. SECELEANU, S. O. TOHĂNEANU, M. VAZ PINTO, AND R. H. VILLARREAL
160: 
180: function stabilizes (Remark 3.10), named by analogy with the regularity index for the Hilbert
181: function of a fat point scheme Z which is the index where the Hilbert function HZ of Z stabilizes.
182:    In order to study the behavior of δI we introduce a numerical invariant called the v-number
183: (Definition 4.1). We give a description for this invariant in Proposition 4.2 that will allow us to
184: compute it using computer algebra systems, e.g. Macaulay2 [17] (Example 4.3).
185: Proposition 4.6 Let I ( m ⊂ S be an unmixed graded ideal whose associated primes are
186: generated by linear forms. Then reg(δI ) = v(I).
187:    From the viewpoint of algebraic coding theory it is important to determine reg(δI ). Indeed let
188: X be a set of projective points over a finite field K, let CX (d) be its corresponding Reed-Muller
189: type code, and let δX (d) be the minimum distance of CX (d) (see Section 5), then δX (d) ≥ 2 if
190: and only if 1 ≤ d < reg(δI(X) ). Our results give an effective method—that can be applied to any
191: Reed-Muller type code—to compute the regularity index of the minimum distance (Corollary 5.6,
192: Example 4.5).
193:    The minimum socle degree s(I) of S/I (Definition 2.7) was used in [33] to obtain homological
194: lower bounds for the minimum distance of a fat point scheme Z in Ps−1 . We relate the minimum
195: socle degree, the v-number and the Castelnuovo-Mumford regularity for Geramita ideals in
196: Theorem 4.10. For radical ideals it is an open problem whether or not reg(δI ) ≤ reg(S/I)
197: [28, Conjecture 4.2]. In dimension 1, the conjecture is true because of Proposition 4.6 and
198: Theorem 4.10. Moreover, via Theorem 4.10, we can extend the notion of a Cayley–Bacharach
199: scheme [11] by defining the notion of a Cayley–Bacharach ideal (Definition 4.14). It turns out
200: that Cayley-Bacharach ideals are connected to Reed–Muller type codes and to minimum distance
201: functions.
202:    Letting HI be the Hilbert function of I, we have δI (d) > deg(S/I) − HI (d) + 1 for some d ≥ 1
203: when I is unmixed of dimension at least 2 (Proposition 4.21). One of our main results is:
204: Theorem 4.19 If I ⊂ S is a Geramita ideal and there exists h ∈ S1 regular on S/I, then
205: 
206:                                    δI (d) ≤ deg(S/I) − HI (d) + 1
207: 
208:                           ALGEBRAIC INVARIANTS OF GERAMITA IDEALS                                   5
209: 
210: for d ≥ 1 or equivalently HI (d) − 1 ≤ hypI (d) for d ≥ 1.
211:    This inequality is well known when I is the vanishing ideal of a finite set of projective points
212: [30, p. 82]. In this case the inequality is called the Singleton bound [34, Corollary 1.1.65].
218: for d ≥ 1 (Corollary 5.7).
219:    In Section 6 we examine minimum distance functions of complete intersection ideals and show
220: some special cases of the following two conjectures.
221: Conjecture 6.3 Let X be a finite in Ps−1 and suppose that I = I(X) is a complete intersection
222: generated by f1 , . . . , fc , c = s − 1, with di = deg(fi ), and 2 ≤ di ≤ di+1 for all i.
223:    (a) (Tohăneanu–Van Tuyl [33, Conjecture 4.9]) δI (1) ≥ (d1 − 1)d2 · · · dc .
224:    (b) (Eisenbud-Green-Harris [8, Conjecture CB10]) If f1 , . . . , fc are quadratic forms, then
225:        hypI (d) ≤ 2c − 2c−d for 1 ≤ d ≤ c or equivalently δI (d) ≥ 2c−d for 1 ≤ d ≤ c.
226:    We prove part (a) of this conjecture, in a more general setting, when I is equigenerated, that
227: is, all minimal homogeneous generators have the same degree (Proposition 6.4, Remark 6.6).
228: The conjecture also holds for P2 [33, Theorem 4.10] (Corollary 6.5). According to [8], part (b)
229: of this conjecture is true for the following values of d: 1, c − 1, c.
230:    For all unexplained terminology and additional information we refer to [4, 6, 27] (for the
231: theory of Gröbner bases, commutative algebra, and Hilbert functions), and [23, 34] (for the
232: theory of error-correcting codes and linear codes).

239: Commutative algebra. Let I 6= (0) be a graded ideal of S of Krull dimension k. The Hilbert
240: function of S/I is: HI (d) := dimK (Sd /Id ) for d = 0, 1, 2, . . ., where Id = I ∩ Sd . By a theorem
241: of Hilbert [32, p. 58], there is a unique polynomial PI (x) ∈ Q[x] of degree k − 1 such that
242: HI (d) = PI (d) for d ≫ 0. By convention the degree of the zero polynomial is −1.

292: The Castelnuovo–Mumford regularity of S/I (regularity of S/I for short) and the minimum socle
293: degree (s-number for short) of S/I are defined as
294:                 reg(S/I) = max{j − i | bi,j 6= 0} and s(I) = min{j − g | bg,j 6= 0}.
295: If S/I is Cohen-Macaulay (i.e. g = dim(S) − dim(S/I)) and there is a unique j such that
296: bg,j 6= 0, then the ring S/I is called level. In particular, a level ring for which the unique j such
297: that bg,j 6= 0 is bg,j = 1 is called Gorenstein.
298: 
299:     An excellent reference for the regularity of graded ideals is the book of Eisenbud [7].
300: Definition 2.8. The regularity index of the Hilbert function of S/I, or simply the regularity
301: index of S/I, denoted ri(S/I), is the least integer n ≥ 0 such that HI (d) = PI (d) for d ≥ n.

552: 4.1. Minimum distance function for unmixed ideals. We begin by introducing the fol-

555: Definition 4.1. The v-number of a graded ideal I, denoted v(I), is given by
556:            (
557:             min{d ≥ 1 | there exists f ∈ Sd and p ∈ Ass(I) with (I : f ) = p}                                         if I ( m,
558:    v(I) :=
559:             0                                                                                                         if I = m,
560: where Ass(I) is the set of associated primes of S/I and m = (t1 , . . . , ts ) is the irrelevant maximal
561: ideal of S.
562: 
563:    The v-number is finite for any graded ideal by the definition of associated primes. If p is a
564: prime ideal and p 6= m, then v(p) = 1.
565:    Let I ( m ⊂ S be a graded ideal and let p1 , . . . , pm be its associated primes. One can define
566: the v-number of I locally at each pi by
567:                                vpi (I) := min{d ≥ 1 | ∃f ∈ Sd with (I : f ) = pi }.
568:    The v-number of I is equal to min{vp1 (I), . . . , vpm (I)}. If I = I(X) is the vanishing ideal of a
569: finite set X = {P1 , . . . , Pm } of reduced projective points and pi is the vanishing ideal of Pi , then
570: vpi (I) is the degree of Pi in X in the sense of [11, Definition 2.1].
571:    We give an alternate description for the v-number using initial degrees of certain modules.
572: This will allow us to compute the v-number using Macaulay2 [17] (see Example 4.3). For a
573: graded module M 6= 0 we denote α(M ) = min{deg(f ) | f ∈ M, f 6= 0}. By convention, for
574: M = 0 we set α(0) = 0.

739:    A finite set X = {P1 , . . . , Pm } of reduced points in Ps−1 is Cayley-Bacharach if every hyper-
740: surface of degree less than reg(S/I(X)) which contains all but one point of X must contain all
741: the points of X or equivalently if degX (Pi ) = reg(S/I(X)) for all i = 1, . . . , m [11, Definition 2.7].
742: Since degX (Pi ) = vpi (I), where pi is the vanishing ideal of Pi , by Theorem 4.10 one can extend
743: this notion to Geramita ideals.
744: Definition 4.14. A Geramita ideal I ⊂ S is called Cayley–Bacharach if vp (I) is equal to
745: reg(S/I) for all p ∈ Ass(I).

1095: Proposition 6.9. The following statements are equivalent:
1096:      (1) [Conjecture 6.3(b)] Let I be a complete intersection generated by c quadratic forms. Then
1097:          δI (d) ≥ 2c−d for 1 ≤ d ≤ c or equivalently hypI (d) ≤ 2c − 2c−d for 1 ≤ d ≤ c.

1099:          in Ps−1 and f ∈ S := K[t1 , . . . , ts ] is a homogeneous polynomial of degree d such that
1100:          deg(S/(I(X), f )) > 2c − 2c−d , then f ∈ IX .
1101: 
1102: Proof. Let I = I(X) be a complete intersection ideal of c quadratic homogeneous polynomials.
1103: Then deg(S/I) = 2c and (2) is equivalent to the statement for any f ∈ Fd , deg(S/(I(X), f )) ≤
1104: 2c − 2c−d . Using Definition 1.1, this is in turn equivalent to δI (d) ≥ 2c−d , for d = 1, . . . , c, which
1105: is precisely the statement of (1).                                                                       

1118: reg(S/I) is the regularity index of δI . Thus Case d = c follows since reg(S/I) = c.          

1120:    If X ⊂ Pc is a reduced set of points such that I(X) is a complete intersection ideal and the
1121: points of X are in linearly general position, i.e., any c + 1 points of X span Pc , then [1, Theorem
1122: 1] adds more cases when [8, Conjecture CB10] holds. If we assume that the quadrics that cut
1123: out X are generic, then the assumptions of that result are satisfied, and we can conclude by
1124: Proposition 6.9 that if 1 ≤ d ≤ c − 1, then δI (d) ≥ c(c − 1 − d) + 2. Suppose d := c − k, for some
1125: 1 ≤ k ≤ c − 1. Then this inequality becomes δI (c − k) ≥ c(k − 1) + 2. We observe that this
1126: inequality is sufficiently strong to establish [Conjecture 6.3(b)] asymptotically for c sufficiently
1127: large:
1128:      • k = 2. If c ≥ 2, then δI (c − 2) ≥ c + 2 ≥ 22 .
1129:      • k = 3. If c ≥ 3, then δI (c − 3) ≥ 2c + 2 ≥ 23 .
1130:      • k = 4. If c ≥ 5, then δI (c − 4) ≥ 3c + 2 ≥ 24 .
1131:      • k ≥ 5. If c ≥ (2k − 2)/(k − 1), then δI (c − k) ≥ 2k .

1426: I.3.3    Conjectures
1427: We also make the following conjectures
1428: 
1429: Conjecture I.3.3.1. Every presented associative n-category is naturally an associative
1430: n-category, up to some arbitrary (contractible) choices for morphisms witnessing
1431: homotopies.
1432: 
1433: This is a conjecture because a priori presented associative n-category and associative
1434: n-category are very different structures. It will be further discussed in Section 11.1.5.
1435: 
1436: Conjecture I.3.3.2. There is a one-to-one correspondence of presentations of CW-
1437: complexes (up to homotopy of the attaching maps) and presented associative ∞-
1438: groupoids (up to choices of “generalised direction” for each generator).
1439: 
1440: This conjecture will be discussed again in Section A.2.4 after a translation of CW-
1441: complexes into presented associative ∞-groupoids has been sketched.
1442: 
1443: Conjecture I.3.3.3. The theory of associative n-categories is equivalent to a theory
1444: weak n-categories.
1445: 
1446: Preliminary evidence for this conjecture is discussed in Section C.2, where a theory of
1447: presented weak n-categories will be constructed.
