4461: 1.1.4     Posets
4462: We recall the basic notion of posets and related concepts.
4463: 
4464: Definition 1.1.4.1 (Preorders and posets).
4465: 
4466:   • A preorder X is a category whose hom sets are either the empty set ∅ or the
4467:     singleton set { ∗ }. Let x, y ∈ X. If the hom-set X(x, y) is non-empty, i.e. it
4468:     equals { ∗ }, then (x → y) denotes morphism ∗ : x → y. Otherwise we read
4469:     (x → y) as false.
4470: 
4471:   • A poset X is a preorder such that if x, y ∈ X and both (x → y) ∈ mor(X) and
4472:     (y → x) ∈ mor(X) then x = y.
4473: 
4474:   • A functor of posets F : X → Y is a functor X → Y between posets X and Y .
4475:     Poset and their functors form the category Pos which is a subcategory of Cat.
4476:     The inclusion Pos ,→ Cat has a left adjoint PSk : Cat → Pos associating to a
4477:     category its posetal skeleton.
4579: Having seen relations and posets, we now introduce Bool-enriched profunctors as
4580: follows.
4581: 
4582: Definition 1.1.6.1 (Profunctorial relations). Let X, Y , Z be posets. A profunctorial
4583: relation R : X −7→ Y is a functor
4584: 
4585:                                 R : X op × Y → Bool
4586: 
4587: Denote the set of profunctorial relations R : X −7→ Y by PRel(X, Y ).
4588: 
4589: Notation 1.1.6.2 (Depicting (profunctorial) relations). A (profunctorial) relation R :
4590: X op × Y → Bool can by depicted as follows: we depict the (po)sets X and Y
4591: individually as points and arrows, and draw an edge between x ∈ X and y ∈ Y if and
4592: only if R(x, y) is true. For instance
4593: 
4594: 
4595: 
4604: indicate that X is the domain and Y the codomain of R.
4605:    Composition of profunctorial relations can be derived from the general theory
4606: of enriched profunctors (which uses a definition analogous to the ones given in
4607: Definition 1.1.3.1). It can also be characterised more directly, as done in the following
4608: construction.
4609: Construction 1.1.6.3 (Composition of profunctorial relations and the category
4610: PRel). We construct the composite of two (composable) profunctorial relations and
4611: use this to define the category PRel. Given R ∈ PRel(X, Y ) first note that we have
4612: obj(R) ∈ Rel(X, Y ). Conversely, given a relation R ∈ Rel(X, Y ), then there is a
4613: (necessarily unique) R0 ∈ PRel(X, Y ) with obj(R0 ) = R if and only if R satisfies the
4614: profunctoriality conditions
4615:                           if x → x0 and R(x0 , y) then R(x, y)                  (1.1.6.4)
4616:                                     0       0
4617:                           if R(x, y ) and y → y then R(x, y)
4618: Now, given two profunctorial relations R : X −7→ Y , S : Y −7→ Z, we observe that the
4619: composite relation obj(R) obj(S) again satisfies the profunctoriality conditions. We
4620: can thus define R S by setting (cf. Remark 1.1.4.2)
4650: We conclude this section by remarking that, just as profunctors can be obtained as
4651: conjoints (or companions) from functors (cf. [36]), profunctorial relations can be
4652: obtained from functors of posets. Explicitly this means the following.
4653: Definition 1.1.6.6 (Profunctorial relations from functors of posets). Given a functor
4654: of posets F : X → Y , we define grphF to be the profunctorial relation obtained as
4655: HomY (F −, −), that is, the composite
4656:                              F ×id       YHom        P−1
4657:                      X × Y −−−→ Y × Y −−−→ Set −−→ Bool
4658: 
4659: Here, we used that HomY only takes values in im(P). grphF is called the graph of
4660: F . Similarly, for a function of sets f : X → Y we define grphf to be the relation
4661: grphdis(f ) which is the usual graph of a function f .

4663: 1.1.7    Families of profunctorial relations
4664: We recall (and extend terminology of) the construction inSection S.2.2. This is a
4665: Grothendieck construction for profunctorial relations which can be seen as a (de-
4666: categorified) analogue of the Grothendieck construction for profunctors. We can also
4830: 
4831:                                             109
4832: 
4833: Definition 1.2.0.1 (The category Pos/C ). Let C be a category. We define the
4834: category Pos/C , called the category of (C-)labelled posets to be the full subcategory of
4835: the over-category Cat/C whose image under the forgetful functor is Pos. In other
4836: words: objects are tuples (X, F ) such that F : X → C is functor from the poset X
4837: to C. F is called the labelling of X. Morphisms h : (Y, G) → (X, F ) are maps of
4838: posets h : Y → X such that G = F h. The forgetful functor U : Pos/C → Pos is
4839: obtain as the restriction of the forgetful U : Cat/C → Cat. In other words: it maps
4840: h : (Y, G) → (X, F ) to h : Y → X.
4841: 
4842: Remark 1.2.0.2. There is an isomorphism of categories Pos and Pos/1 where 1 is the
4843: terminal category. Denoting by ! : C → 1 the terminal functor to 1, this isomorphism
4844: identifies X and (X, !).
4845: 
4846: 1.2.1     Maps with lifts and downward closed subposets
4848: Under many different circumstances, this condition will allow us to construct certain
4849: labelling functors which otherwise would not exist in general.
4850: 
4851: Definition 1.2.1.1 (Poset maps with lifts). A functor of posets F : X → Y has
4852: lifts if whenever F (x) = y and y 0 → y ∈ mor(Y ), then there is x0 ∈ X such that
4853: x0 → x ∈ mor(X) and F (x0 ) = y 0 .
4854: 
4855: Example 1.2.1.2. δ 22 : 2 → 3 is an example of a functor of posets with lifts. As a
4856: non-example, neither δ 21 : 2 → 3 nor δ 20 : 2 → 3 has lifts.
4857: 
4858: Definition 1.2.1.3 (Downward closed subposets). Let X be a poset and Y ⊂ X be
4859: a subposet of X. We say Y is downward closed if whenever x → y, x ∈ X and y ∈ Y
4860: then x ∈ Y .
4861: 
4862: Remark 1.2.1.4. Let H : Z → X be a functor of posets. Then im(H) is a downward
4863: closed subposet of X if and only if H has lifts.
4864: Remark 1.2.1.5. Every downward closed subposet is full.
4865: 
4866: Claim 1.2.1.6. Let X, Y be posets, and assume functors F : X → PRel, G : Y →
4867:  PRel, H : X → Y such that F = GH. If H has lifts, then G(H) (as defined in
4868: Construction 1.1.8.2) has lifts.
4869: 
4870: Proof. Assume (y, a) → (H(x), b) ∈ mor(G(G)) and note (H(x), b) = G(H)(x, b).
4871: Then (y → H(x)) ∈ mor(Y ) and G(y → H(x))(a, b). Since H has lifts we find x0 such

5015: instead.

5017: Definition 2.1.1.1 (Singular intervals). A singular interval I is a poset with objects
5018: being a set of natural numbers
5019: 
5020:                          obj(I) = { 0, 1, 2, . . . , 2HI − 1, 2HI }
5021: 
5022: 
5023:                                            115
5024: 
5025: for some HI ∈ N. HI is called the height of I. The poset order → of I, also called the
5026: singular order on I, is generated by the morphisms a → a + 1 if a ∈ I is even, and
5027: a ← a + 1 if a ∈ I is odd. The unique interval of height n ∈ N is denoted by In . I0 is
5028: called the initial singular interval, I1 is called the terminal singular interval.

5078: 
5079: Definition 2.1.1.5 (Morphisms of singular intervals). Let I, J be singular intervals.
5080: 
5081:   (i) A (singular-height) morphism f between singular intervals I and J, also called SI-
5082:       morphism and denoted by f : I →SI J, is a monotone map f : sing(I) → sing(J).
5083:       Morphisms of singular intervals compose as functions of sets. Singular intervals
5084:       and their morphisms form the category SI.
5085: 
5086:   (ii) A regular-segment morphism g between singular intervals I and J, also called
5087:        an SIreg -morphism and denoted by g : I →reg SI J, is monotone map g : reg(I) →
5088:         reg(J) that also preserves endpoints, i.e. g(0) = 0 and g(2HI ) = 2HJ . Regular-
5089:        segment morphisms of singular intervals compose as functions of sets. Singular
5090:        intervals and their regular-segment morphisms form a category SIreg .

5297: Definition 2.1.3.1 (Profunctorial realisation). Given f : I →SI J we define a relation
5298: R(f ) : I −7→ J, called the profunctorial realisation of f , by a case distinction in the
5299: first argument as follows
5300: 
5301:                      R(f )(a ∈ sing(I), b ∈ J)      ⇐⇒      f (a) = b           (Sing1)
5302: 
5303: and
5304:          R(f )(a ∈ reg(I), b ∈ J)    ⇐⇒      fb(a − 1) ≤ b ≤ fb(a + 1)          (Sing2)
5305: Relations of the form R(f ) : I −7→ J are called SI-relations, and f is called the
5306: underlying SI-morphism of R(f ).
5307: 
5308: Example 2.1.3.2 (Profunctorial realisation). Using f1 and f2 as defined in Exam-
5309: ples 2.1.1.7 we can construct R(f1 ) and R(f2 ) to be the following relations
5310: 
5327: 
5328: and
5329:          R(g)(a ∈ I, b ∈ sing(J))     ⇐⇒         g(b − 1) ≤ a ≤ g(b + 1)        (Reg2)
5330: Then we claim R(f ) = R(g) where R(f ) was defined in Definition 2.1.3.1.
5331: 
5332: Proof. The proof is straight-forward. We argue by case distinction:
5333: 
5334:                                           123
5335: 
5336:   (i) a ∈ sing(I), b ∈ sing(J). Then R(f )(a, b) if and only if f (a) = b and R(g)(a, b) if
5337:       and only if g(b − 1) < a < g(b + 1). By (2.1.2.3) both are equivalent.
5338: 
5339:  (ii) a ∈ sing(I), b ∈ reg(J). Then R(f )(a, b) if and only if f (a) = b and R(g)(a, b) if
5340:       and only if g(b) = a. Both are impossible by Definition 2.1.1.5.
5341: 
5342:  (iii) a ∈ reg(J), b ∈ reg(J). Then R(f )(a, b) if and only if fb(a − 1) < b < fb(a + 1)
5343:        and R(g)(a, b) if and only if g(b) = a. By (2.1.2.2) both are equivalent.
5344: 
5345:  (iv) a ∈ reg(J), b ∈ sing(J). Then R(f )(a, b) if and only if fb(a − 1) ≤ b ≤ fb(a + 1)
5346:       and R(g)(a, b) if and only if g(b − 1) ≤ a ≤ g(b + 1). Equivalence follows since
5347:       firstly fb(a−1) ≤ b ⇐⇒ a ≤ g(b+1) and secondly b ≤ fb(a+1) ⇐⇒ g(b−1) ≤
5348:       a. We argue for the first statement (the second follows similarly). Assume
5349:       fb(a − 1) ≤ b < b + 1. Then ambidexterity (2.1.2.8) yields a − 1 < g(b + 1) and
5350:       thus a ≤ g(b + 1). Conversely, assume a − 1 < a ≤ g(b + 1). Then ambidexterity
5351:       (2.1.2.8) yields fb(a − 1) < b + 1 and thus fb(a − 1) ≤ b as required.
5352:

5381: 
5382: Lemma 2.1.3.6 (Profunctorial realisation functor).
5383: 
5384:   (i) The relation R(f ) : I −7→ J as defined in Definition 2.1.3.1 is a profunctorial
5385:       relation between the posets I and J (in singular order)
5386: 
5387:  (ii) The assignment f 7→ R(f ) is functorial, giving rise to a functor R : SI → PRel
5388:       called the profunctorial realisation.
5389: 
5390: Proof.     (i) The proof is straight-forward. For profunctoriality of R(f ) we need to
5391:       verify (1.1.6.4). Let a0 → a in I and R(f )(a, b). We need to show R(f )(a0 , b).
5392:        Excluding the case where a0 = a (in which the statement holds trivially), by
5393:        Definition 2.1.1.1 we either have a0 = a − 1 or a0 = a + 1, and a0 ∈ reg(I), a ∈
5394:         sing(I) in both cases. We assume a0 = a − 1 → a (and argue similarly in the
5395:        second case). Since a ∈ sing(I) we have b = f (a) = f (a0 + 1) by (Sing1). Thus
5396:        f (a0 − 1) ≤ b ≤ f (a0 + 1) by monotonicity of f and we deduce R(f )(a0 , b) by
5397:       (Sing2) as required. Next assume b → b0 in J and R(f )(a, b). We need to show
5398:        R(f )(a, b0 ). Excluding again b = b0 we either have b0 = b + 1 or b0 = b − 1 and
5399:        argue in the former case. Since b ∈ reg(J) we have a = f reg (b) = f reg (b0 + 1) by
5400: 
5401: 
5402:                                            125
5403: 
5404:    (Reg1). Thus f reg (b0 − 1) ≤ a ≤ f reg (b0 + 1) by monotonicity of f reg and we
5405:    deduce R(f )(a, b0 ) by (Reg2) as required.
5406:

5415:    relation. The statement then follows by comparison of (Sing1) and (Sing2)
5416:    (in the case that f = id and thus fb = idsing(I)

5419:     For the second statement we show for a ∈ I1 , c ∈ I3 that

5748:  Definition 2.2.1.3 (SI-families). For X a poset, a functor A : X → SI is called a
5749:  singular interval family, or more concisely, an SI-family, with base poset X. G(RA) is
5750:  called its total poset and π RA is called an SI-bundle.
5751:      Given two SI-families A1 , A2 : X → SI, a map of SI-bundles G : π RA1 → π RA2 is
5752:  a map of bundles G : π RA1 → π RA2 that preserves direction order: in other words, we
5753:  require for each x ∈ X that G|x is monotone.
5754:

6561: Definition 3.2.1.1 (Towers of SI-families). A (height n) tower T of SI-families over
6562: a poset X is an ordered sequence of SI-families { U n−1 , U n−2 , ..., U 0 } of length n such
6563: that dom(U k ) = cod(π U k−1 ) (0 < k < n) and dom(U 0 ) = X.
6564: 
6565:   • U k is called the k-level labelling and π U k the k-level bundle of T
6566: 
6567:   • G(U k−1 ) is called the k-level total poset (of T ), X is called the base poset or
6568:     0-level total poset of T
6569: 
6570:   • The height k tower T k := U k−1 , U k−2 , ..., U 0 is called the k-level truncation
6571:                                 
6572: 
6573:     of T

7243: Definition 4.1.1.1 (Injections). Let X be a poset, and let A, B : X → SI be singular
7244: interval families. An injection λ : A ,→ B of singular interval families A, B : X → SI
7245: is a natural transformation A → B such that each component λx : A(x) →SI B(x) is
7246: monic, that is, each component λx , x ∈ X, is an injective map

7601: Definition 4.1.3.3 (Collapse maps of singular intervals). Given two singular intervals
7602: I, J ∈ SI, a collapse functor s is a functor s : J → I that is open and surjective.

7633: 
7634: Construction 4.1.3.6 (Underlying morphism of collapse functors). Let s be a collapse
7635: functor. This implies that the graph grphs of s (cf. Definition 1.1.6.6) has an converse
7636:                                                                 −1
7637: relation grph>
7638:              s that restricts to give the graph of a function ssing on singular heights.
7639: In other words,
7640:                              grphs−1 = grph> s sing(I)×singJ
7641:                                    sing
7642: 
7643: 
7644: Indeed arguing by contradiction first assume that grph>   s sing(I)×J was a non-functional
7645: relation. By surjectivity of s, this means there is b, b0 ∈ I such that b < b0 and
7646: s(b) = s(b0 ) = a ∈ sing(J). There is at least one c ∈ [b, b0 ] such that c ∈ reg(I).
7647: Monotonicity of s implies s(c) = a which contradicts openness of s. Openness similarly
7648: implies that the functional relation yields a function with image in sing(J), which
7681: the assumed property of t and openness of s.
7682:     For the second statement of (4.1.3.8) we first proof ⇒. Assume s(a) = b and
7683: b ∈ reg(J), a ∈ I. We prove tb(b − 1) < a. If b = 0, then tb(b − 1) < a follows from the
7684: boundary cases in Definition 2.1.1.9. If b > 0, then s(tb(b − 1)) = b − 1. If a < tb(b − 1)
7685: this would contradict monotonicity of s. Thus we must have tb(b − 1) < a. Similarly,
7686: we find a < tb(b + 1) and thus the implication ⇒ follows.
7687:     Next we prove ⇐. Assume (tb(b − 1) < a < tb(b + 1)) for b ∈ reg(J), a ∈ I. Using
7688: monotonicity of s, the first statement of (4.1.3.8) as well as the boundary cases in
7689: Definition 2.1.1.9 we find b − 1 ≤ s(a) ≤ b + 1. Since b ± 1 are singular heights,
7690: s(a) = b ± 1 the first statement of (4.1.3.8) would then imply a = t(b ± 1) contradicting
7691: our assumption. Thus we must have s(a) = b.
7692:     Finally, note that treg = s|reg(J) . This follows from comparing (4.1.3.8) with
7693: (2.1.2.2). But treg is endpoint-preserving. Thus 0 and 2HJ are in the image of s. Since
7694: the domain of s is connected, so is its image and thus s must be surjective, it is a
7695: collapse functor.
7696: 
7697:     The previous claim shows that there is a (possibly partial) left inverse to the
7698: underlying monomorphism construction. The next claim shows that this inverse is in
7699: fact total, and two-sided.
7700: 
7701: Claim 4.1.3.9 (Singular collapse functors from monomorphisms). Given two singular
7702: intervals I, J ∈ SI, and a monomorphism t : I →SI J, we claim that s defined from t
7749: “minimal data” for collapse functors. This makes injections sometimes easier to work
7750: with in proofs.
7751: 
7752: Definition 4.1.4.1 (Family collapse functors). Given two singular interval families
7753: A, B : X → SI, a (family) collapse functor S of B to A is a map of bundles
7754: S : π B → π A such that that S|x : B(x) → A(x) is a collapse functor of intervals for
7755: each x ∈ X.
7756: 
7757:     The following two constructions show that family collapse functors are fully and
7758: faithfully described by injections.

9486: We start by defining embedding functors. These will be the fibrewise mappings of the
9487: components of embeddings.
9488: 
9489: Definition 6.1.1.1 (Embedding functors). An open functor f : I → J of singular
9490: intervals I, J (cf. Definition 4.1.3.1) is called an (singular) embedding functor if it is
9491: injective on objects.
9492:

9497: Claim 6.1.1.2 (Embedding functors are linear). Let f : I → J be an embedding
9498: functor. Then f maps
9499:                               d 7→ d + f (0)
9500: 
9501: Proof. Note that since f preserves regular segments, we have f (0) ∈ reg(J). The
9502: proof of the statement is by induction (it holds for d = 0), in each step showing
9503: f (d + 1) = f (d) + 1. Assume d is a regular segment, d + 1 is a singular height and
9504: thus d → d + 1 in I) (argue similarly if d is singular). Then, since f is monotone
9505: and injective we must have f (d + 1) > f (d). But since it is a functor of posets, we
9506: also have f (d) → f (d + 1). By Definition 2.1.1.1 this implies f (d + 1) = f (d) + 1 as
9507: required.
9508: 
9509:     Recall that open surjective functors (that is, collapse functors) are classified by
9510: their underlying monomorphisms which record the mappings between singular heights.
9511: We will see that open injective functors (that is, embedding functors) are classified by
9512: their mapping of (regular) interval endpoints. This is foreshadowed by the following
9513: remark.
9514:

9516: and q− , q+ ∈ reg(J), q− < q+ then the functor J [q− ,q+ ] : I q+ −q− → J defined by

9775: 6.2.1     Endpoint sections
9776: We start with the definition of (open) sections.
9777: 
9778: Definition 6.2.1.1 (Sections and open sections). Let A : X → SI be an SI-family.
9779: A section q in A is a functor q : X → G(A) such that
9780: 
9781:                                        π A q = idX
9782: 
9783: Note that to any such section we can associate a function q : X → N (cf. Nota-
9784: tion 1.1.7.4) defined to map
9785:                                    x → q(x)
9786: A section q is called open if for all x ∈ X, q(x) ∈ reg(A(x)).
9787: 
9788:                                           239
9789: 
9790: Notation 6.2.1.2 (Depicting sections). Since a section is determined by its value in
9791: each fibre, we will depict sections by marking points in each fibre of an SI-bundle.
9792: This is illustrated by the next section.
9793: Remark 6.2.1.3 (Open sections have lifts). Open sections q : X → G(A) have lifts (cf.
9794: Definition 1.2.1.1). Indeed, if (y, a) → (x, q(x)) then y → x and (a, q(x)) ∈ E(A(y →
9795: x)). By (Reg1) a is the unique element of A(y) with that property. By functoriality
9796: of q we must thus have q(y) = a.
9797: 
9798: 6.2.2     Source and target section
9799: Source and target section are special open section that any SI-family admits. We
9800: define them, and give examples.
9801:

10014: 6.2.4     k-level embeddings
10630: 6.4      Normalisation on embeddings
10631: In this section we show that given θ : B  A, then collapse of a parent family A
10632: induces a collapse on its subfamily B. In more abstract terms we will show that the
10633: subcategory of CubenC (cf. Definition 3.2.8.1) generated by multi-level collapses and
10634: embeddings admits an “(epi, mono) factorization”: Every morphism in this subcategory
10635: can be uniquely factored in a multi-level collapse followed by an embedding.
10636:

12020: Definition 7.1.5.1 (Locally and globally trivial families). Let A : X → SI//Cn . We

12101: Lemma 7.1.5.5 (Locally normalised implies normalised). Let A : X → SI//C . If A is
12102: locally normalised, then it is normalised.
12103: 
12104: Proof. Assume by contradiction a non-identity λ : A k B for some 0 < k ≤ n. Then
12105: there is x ∈ X such that for some y ∈ G k (A|x ) ⊂ G(A) we have λx 6= id. Using
12106: Construction 6.4.1.2, we find an induced collapse rest∗x λ : A|x → A0 on the embedding
12107: restx : A|x ⊂ A, and by choice of x (cf. (6.4.1.3)) this collapse is non-trivial. This
12108: contradicts A being locally normalised.

12354: 7.2.1     Globular cubes
12355: We start with a definition of ∂-globularity.
12356: 
12357: Definition 7.2.1.1 (∂-globularity). Let A : X → SI//Cn and 1 ≤ k ≤ n. We say that
12358: A is k-level ∂-globular if U kA is locally trivial on both the image of sU k−1 and the image
12359:                                                                            A
12360: of tU k−1 .
12361:       A
12362:     We say A is ∂-globular if it is k-level ∂-globular for all k.
12363: 
12364:     We now turn to a stricter version of globularity. We first give the definition of
12365: singular and regular content which will enable us to formulate this stricter definition.
12366: 
12367: Definition 7.2.1.2 (Singular/regular content of SI-families). Let X be a poset, and
12368: A : X → SI a singular interval family.
12369: 
12370:   • We define the singular content sing(G(A)) of A to be the full subposet of G(A)
12371:     generated by the objects (x, a) ∈ G(A) such that a ∈ sing(A(x)).
12372: 
12373:   • Similarly, we define the regular content reg(G(A)) of A to be the full subposet of
12374:     G(A) generated by the objects in (x, b) ∈ G(A) such that b ∈ reg(A(x)).
12375: 
12376: Example 7.2.1.3 (Singular/regular content). Using previous examples we give two
12377: SI-bundles and in each case circle points in the regular content in blue, and points in
12378: the singular content in red. First consider the following SI-bundle
12379: 
12380: 
12396:  E(A(x → y)) and b ∈ reg(A(y)) and thus by (Reg1) we must have a = A(x → y)reg (b)
12397: implying a ∈ reg(A(x)) as required for (x, a) ∈ reg(G(A)).
12398: 
12399: Definition 7.2.1.5 (Globular families). Let A : X → SI//Cn and 1 ≤ k ≤ n. We say
12400: A is k-level globular if U kA is locally trivial on reg(G k (A)).
12401:     If X itself is explicitly assumed to be of the form G(F ) for some F : Y → SI, then
12402: we further say A is 0-level globular if A is locally trivial on reg(G(F )). Otherwise, A
12403: is 0-level globular by default.
12404:     We say A is globular if it is k-level globular for all k, 0 ≤ k ≤ n.
12405: 
12406:    A globular C-labelled n-cube A : 1 → SI//Cn is also called a (C-labelled) n-globe.
12407: Remark 7.2.1.6 (Source, target inclusions are in regular content). To compare Def-
12408: inition 7.2.1.1 and Definition 7.2.1.5, recall that sA , tA are open sections and thus
12409: im(sA ), im(tA ) ⊂ reg(G(A)). Further note that both im(sA ), im(tA ) are downward
12410: closed as well as connected if the base space X of A : X → SI is connected (cf.
12411: Remark 2.2.1.8).
12412: 
12413: Examples 7.2.1.7 (Globular families). The reader is invited to verify that all families
12414: in Chapter 6 are globular. Recall the example of a cube Ab : 1 → SI//C3 from
12415: Example 6.1.2.1. We want to see that Ab is 2-level globular and thus pick some
12416: (x → y) ∈ reg(G 2 (Ab )) and need to check that U 2Ab restricted to (x → y) normalises
12417: to the constant family: indeed, U 2Ab x→y for a choice of (x → y) is highlighted in the
12418: below illustration (the rest of the parent family is greyed out)

13290: The following construction shows the converse. Given well-typed sources and targets,
13291: we can define a well-typed type for a new generator using the double cone construction.
13292: The crucial technical difficulty is to show that the double cone is well-typed, which we
13293: will do in the following.

14140: Remark 9.1.1.4 (Computability of TI). For general k, the condition Prk (|x|) is decidable
14141: in general only if r = −1, in which case it reduces to checking non-emptiness of
14142: (U kD )−1 (x). For all other r the condition becomes undecidable. However, it remains
14143: semi-decidable (which would be sufficient e.g. for the purposes of a proof assistant).

15875: 10.2.9      Elementary generic morphisms
15876: Definition 10.2.9.1 (Elementary generic morphism). Let C ∈ Catpres        ∞ . We say
15877:                                                k
15878: f ∈ GComp(C )n is elementary if f = f1 .n f2 implies that either one of f1 , f2 is an
15879: identity (that is, it is of the form fi = IdJgK for g ∈ GComp(C )). Otherwise, f is said
15880: to be non-elementary.

15988: Claim 10.2.11.1. Let C ∈ Catpres
15989:                             ∞ . Then (cf. Definition 10.1.2.3, Construc-
15990: tion 10.2.8.1)
15991:                         GComp(C ) = GComp∗ (C )
15992: 
15993: Proof sketch. For
15994:                         f ∈ GComp∗ (C )n ⇒ f ∈ GComp(C )n
15995: we argue inductively on the dimension of f . First assume f has a single singular
15996: height (that is, G 1 (f ) = I1 ). Then f1 in θ1 : f1 → f (cf. Definition 10.1.2.3) can be
15997: shown to be either the type of an n-generator or an elementary homotopy. Further,
15998: 
15999:                                           396
16000: 
16001: the remainder of f can be k-level whiskered onto f1 , proceeding step by step for
16002: k = n, n − 1, ..., 3, 2, to yield f . Using the inductive assumption, this shows that f
16003: can be written as an element in GComp(C ). For general f , we can proceed in this
16004: way for each singular height each time obtaining an element in GComp(C ), and then
16005: construct f by 1-level whiskering of those elements, showing f itself is also an element
16006: of GComp(C ).
16007:    Conversely, for
16008:                            f ∈ GComp(C )n ⇒ f ∈ GComp∗ (C )n
16009: we argue inductively on dimension and the structure of f . If f is elementary than the
16010: statement follows trivially. If f is non-elementary, then one can show that whiskering
16011: preserves the conditions of Definition 10.1.2.3.

16065:    Generalising Section S.8.1 we introduce the following definition of an n-truncated
16066: algebras.
16067: 
16068: Definition 11.1.1.2 (Algebras of GComp). A (unital) n-truncated GComp-algebra
16069: (S, M) is a tuple of an n-truncated S ∈ GSet and M : GComp(S)≤n → S such that
16070: 
16071:                                       S
16072:                                                 id
16073:                                    J−KS
16074:                                                         &/
16075:                                 GComp(S)≤n           M
16076:                                                               S
16077: 
16078:    The (coherent) resolution of M is then constructed by replacing all algebraic
16079: equations M(f ) = g by coherently invertible generators (apart from those forced by
16080: unitality). Concretely, we have the following.
16081: 
16193: 
16194:                           R(0) (M) ,→ R(1) (M) ,→ R(2) (M) ,→ . . .
16195: 
16196: Definition 11.1.1.4 (Compositional depth). Note that there is a canonical inclu-
16197: sion of presentations iM : C(S) ,→ R(M). Elements of GComp(R(M)) not in the
16198: image of GComp(iM ) : GComp(C(S))≤n ,→ GComp(R(M))≤n are called composites of
16199: compositional depth 1. Otherwise, composites are said to be of compositional depth 0.
16200: 
16201:     Morally the resolution of M is a presented higher category whose composites repre-
16202: sent the “free interactions of algebra laws with elements of the algebra”. Composites
16203: of compositional depth 1 are those that involve algebra laws (and their coherences).
16204: Depth 0 composite are nothing but regular composites build from the elements of the
16205: algebra.
16206: Remark 11.1.1.5 (Algebras and resolutions for Comp). The preceding definition and
16207: construction have analogues obtained when replacing GComp by Comp.

16417: 
16418:    Unlike in associative n-categories, MkC are now choices of data: Indeed, previously
16419: compositional and coherence associativity laws determined such choices, making the
16420: higher category associative. Now MkC pick out coherence cells in C , which makes C
16421: weaker, in the sense of requiring more coherence data to be chosen. In fact, it is possible
16422: from this to retrieve usual coherence cells, such as associators and pentagonators from
16423: the above iterated resolutions, which is illustrated in Appendix C.
16424:    If m = ∞, we say C is a fully weak n-category. Note that in this case, passing to

16449: Conjecture 11.1.5.1 (Presented categories are categories). Given a presented as-
16450: sociative n-category C then Comp(C )∼  ≤n naturally admits structure of an associative
16451: n-categories (up to certain arbitrary choices for MC on homotopies).
16452: 
16453: Remark 11.1.5.2 (Weakifications). A different but less direct approach of possibly
16454: deriving an associative n-category from a presented associative n-category, is to work
16455: with the globular subset of terminal-globe-like generator types of the weakification
16456: (based on techniques mentioned in Appendix C) of a presented associative n-categories.

16459: 11.2      Examples
16460: In this section we describe examples of Definition 11.1.3.1 in low dimensions. In each
16461: dimension (> 0), we will proceed as follows to define an associative n-category C
16462: 
16463:   (i) Morphism data: We describe the underlying globular set of C
16464: 
16465:   (ii) Composition data: We define the value of MC on .kn operations of two (higher)
16466:        morphisms in C and on each elementary homotopy. By the unit law, as well as
16467:        the associativity and coherence laws this then fully determines MC
16468: 
16469:  (iii) Properties of composition: We discuss implications of the associativity and
16470:        coherence laws, and how MC satisfies them
16471: 
16472: Notation 11.2.0.1 (Omitting J−K for types). To simplify notation, for a ∈ C , C ∈ GSet,
16473: we will write a in place of JaK for the type of a in C(C ).
16476: An associative 0-category C is a 0-truncated GComp satisfying the associativity and
16477: coherence laws. Since GComp(C )0 ∼   = C canonically, MC is fully determined by the
16478: unit law. In other words an associative 0-category is nothing but a set. Note also that
16479: M∞C = MC in Definition 11.1.3.1 in this case.
16480: 
16481: 
16482: 
16483: 
16484:                                          407

16486: 11.2.2     Dimension 1
16487: An associative 1-category C is a 1-truncated GComp-algebra satisfying the associativity
16488: and coherence laws. As an example consider the following
16489: 
16490:   (i) Morphism data: Let C be the globular set with
16491: 
16492:                                    C0 = { a, b }
17612:    This motivates this section’s claim that n-manifolds associated to an invertible
17613: (m+1)-generator g (and its associated singularities cgS≡T ) have a deformation equivalent
17614: representative that admits (n + m + 1)-framing. Since every k-cell g ∈ X k in a higher
17615: groupoid X k is invertible, we consequently make the central conjecture that any
17616: manifold diagram of a k-morphisms in X has a deformation equivalent representative
17617: that admits framing on all its submanifolds (where again we treat generators together
17618: with their singularities as a single manifold as before). We call such a manifold diagrams
17619: framed. In particular, up to identifying the k-cube and the k-disk, any framed manifold
17620: diagram is in fact a framed stratification in ΨfrX (Dk ) (cf. Definition A.2.1.7).
17621: 
17622: A.2.3      Translating CW-complexes to ∞-groupoids
17623: The translation from presentations of CW-complexes X to presented associative n-
17624: groupoids X is now straight-forward. We summarise the steps that it involves and
17625: then proceed to give examples.
17626:    The construction of X is inductive in k. Assume X m , m < k has been defined.
17627: 
17628:   (i) For each h ∈ NkX find the framed k-stratification M(h) of Dk via the previous
17743: 
17744:                                              441
17745: 
17746: Conjecture A.2.4.1 (Combinatorial representation of stratifications). Let X be a
17747: CW-complex. There is a (non-unique) ∞-groupoid X such that Xk = IkX , and for
17748: each h ∈ IkX , M(h) is equivalent to JhK. Conversely, if X arises from X in this
17749: way, then X as a CW-complex can be uniquely recovered from X by a mapping from
17750: groupoids to CW-complexes based on (the inverse) of the generalised Thom-Pontryagin
17751: construction.
17752: 
17753: Natural tools for attempting to prove such a conjecture (which involves in particular
17754: a formalisation of the lower arrow in the above square), are Morse theory and Cerf
17755: theory.
17756:     This is a fine-grained version of the homotopy hypothesis, as it gives a (many-to-
17757: one) correspondence at the level of presentations of groupoids and spaces. Note that
17758: the “classical homotopy hypothesis” [5] asks for a correspondence of spaces and higher
17759: groupoids up to equivalence. We have neither given a definition of functors nor of
17825: before.
17826:     Note that for n < ∞ and α = (β, 1) (that is, g is a generating α-equality),
17827: then we could also add proof-irrelevance and symmetry conditions similar to those
17828: in Definition 8.1.1.3, which would guarantee for instance the existence of a unique
17829: “inverse” g −1 . We leave stating these conditions to the diligent reader, as the work
17830: needed to formulate them mostly doesn’t provide valuable new insights.

91:    Theorem 2(ii) and the results in [1] hint at their common strengthening.
92: Conjecture 3. Given ε > 0, there exists f0 such that, if f0 ≤ f ≤ ∆2 + 1,
93: then any graph of maximum degree ∆ in which the neighbourhood of every
94: vertex spans at most ∆2 /f edges has (list) chromatic number at most (2 +
95: ε)∆/ log f .

99:       OCCUPANCY FRACTION, FRACTIONAL COLOURING, AND TRIANGLES                   3
100: 
101: 1.1. Notation and preliminaries. We write I(G) for the set of indepen-
102: dent sets (including the empty set) of a graph G.
103:    Given λ > 0, the hard-core model on G at fugacity λ is a probability distri-
104: bution on I(G), where each I ∈ I(G) occurs with probability proportional
105: to λ|I| . Writing I for the random independent set, we have
598: length depends on some “local” parameters, such as its degree. A version
599: of the present work expressed in this extra generality can be found in the
600: doctoral thesis of the last author [15].
601:    Moreover, following this work, Conjecture 3 has been confirmed in a
602: stronger form within a general framework (which also includes local colour-
603: ing among other things) due to Sereni and three of the authors [11].

23: 
24:                                            In this paper we find the number of different signatures of P (3, 1), P (5, 1) and P (7, 1) upto switching
25:                                            isomorphism, where P (n, k) denotes the generalised Petersen graph, 2k < n. We also count the number of
26:                                            non-isomorphic signatures on P (2n + 1, 1) of size two for all n ≥ 1, and we conjecture that any signature
27:                                            of P (2n + 1, 1), upto switching, is of size at most n + 1.
28:                                            Keywords: Signed graph, generalised Petersen graph, balance, switching, switching isomorphism.
29: 
30: 
31: 
32:                                            1    Introduction
33:                                            Throughout the paper we consider simple graphs. For all the graph-theoretic terms that have not been
34:                                            defined but are used in the paper, see Bondy [1]. Harary was the first to introduced signed graph and
35:                                            balance [4]. Harary [2] used them to model social stress in small groups of people in social psychology.
36:                                            Subsequently, signed graphs have turned out to be valuable. The fundamental property of signed graphs
37:                                            is balance. A signed graph is balanced if all its cycles have positive sign product. The second basic
38:                                            property of signed graphs is switching equivalence. Switching is a way of turning one signature of a graph
39: 
983: isomorphism. In Section 7, we noticed that P (7, 1) has no matching of sizes 5, 6 and 7, upto switching
984: isomorphism. On the basis of these observations, we propose the following conjecture.
985: 
986: Conjecture 8.2. Any matching in P (2n + 1, 1) can be of size at most n + 1, where n ≥ 4.
987: 
988:    Now Problem 8.2 can be reformulated as follows.
989: 
990: Problem 8.3. Can we find the number of non-isomorphic matchings of size 3, 4, 5, . . . , n+1 in P (2n+1, 1)
991: for all n ≥ 4 ?
992: 
993: 
994: 
995: References
996:  [1] J. A. Bondy, U. S. R. Murty, Graph Theory,Graduate Text in Mathematics, Springer, 2007.
997: 
998:  [2] D. Cartwright, Frank Harary, Structural balance: a generalization of Heiders theory, Psychol. Rev.
999:     63 (1956) 277-293.

145: Stern’s triangle the generating function is rational, while for Pascal’s triangle it is only algebraic.
146:    How do we prove the recurrence (3)? From the definition of Stern’s triangle we have

423: minimum polynomial of A is not divisible by (x − θ)2 . Equivalently, all the Jordan blocks with eigenvalue θ
424: of the Jordan canonical form of A have size one.

43:                                               In 1999 Lyngsø and Pedersen formulated the following conjecture motivated by analysis of an approxi-
44:                                          mation algorithm for a 2D protein folding problem [8].
45:                                          Conjecture 1 (Lyngsø and Pedersen, 1999). Every binary circular word of length n divisible by 6 with equal
46:                                          number of zeros and ones has an antipalindromic subsequence of length at least 32 n.
47:                                            ∗ This research was suported by the European Research Council (ERC) under the European Unions Horizon 2020 research

56:     To the best of our knowledge, no progress has been achieved in proving this conjecture, even though it
57: has drawn substantial attention from the combinatorics of words community. However, it is a source of other
58: interesting conjectures.
60: trary. A strengthening is to require the two halves of the subsequence to lie on different halves of the circle
61: according to some partition of the circle into two parts of equal length. Surprisingly, experiments show that
62: this does not change the bound.
63: Conjecture 2 (Brevier, Preissmann and Sebő, [3]). Let w be a binary circular word of length n divisible
64: by 6 with equal number of zeros and ones. Then w can be partitioned into two linear words w1 , w2 of equal
65: length, w = w1 w2 , having subsequences s1 , s2 such that s1 s2 is an antipalindrome and |s1 | = |s2 | = 31 |w|.
66:     We checked this conjecture up to n = 30 by computer. The worst known case for the both conjectures
67: is provided by the word w = 0i 1i (01)i 1i 0i showing the tightness of the conjectured bound (by tightness
68: everywhere in this note we understand the existence of a lower bound different from the conjectured bound
69: by at most a small additive constant). The bound 21 n instead of 32 n can be easily proved, but no better
70: bound is known.
71: Proposition 3 (Brevier, Preissmann and Sebő, [3]). Conjecture 2 is true when replacing |s1 | = |s2 | = 31 |w|
72: by |s1 | = |s2 | = 41 |w|.
73: Proof. Consider an arbitrary partition of w into two linear words w1 , w2 of equal length, w = w1 w2 . The
74: number of zeros in w1 is the same as the number of ones in w2 and vice versa. Thus we can pick an
75: antipalindromic subsequence 0k 1k or 1k 0k with k ≥ 14 n having the required properties.
76: 
77: 
78: 2     Circular Words
79: A natural idea is to look at palindromic subsequences instead of antipalindromic ones. This leads to a number
80: of interesting conjectures which we describe in this section. First, we formulate palindromic counterparts to
81: Conjectures 1 and 2.
82: Conjecture 4. Every binary circular word of length n has a palindromic subsequence of length at least 32 n.
83: Conjecture 5. Let w be a binary circular word of length n divisible by 6. Then w can be partitioned into 2
84: linear words w1 , w2 of equal length, w = w1 w2 , having subsequences s1 , s2 such that s1 = sR
85:                                                                                               2 (that is, s1 s2 is
86: a palindrome) and |s1 s2 | = 23 |w|.
87:     We checked both conjectures up to n = 30 by computer. The worst known case for Conjecture 5 is
88: provided by the word 02i (10)i 12i , showing the tightness of the conjectured bound. The word 0i (10)i 1i
89: provides an upper bound of 43 n for Conjecture 4. A better bound is discussed in Section 3.
90:     In Conjecture 4 it is enough to pick the subsequence consisting of all appearances of the letter with the
91: largest frequency to get the 12 n lower bound. It is also easy to prove the 12 n bound for Conjecture 5. No
92: better bounds are known to be proved.
93: Proposition 6. Conjecture 5 is true when replacing |s1 s2 | = 32 |w| by |s1 s2 | = 21 |w|.
94: Proof. Consider an arbitrary partition of w into two linear words w1 , w2 of equal length, w = w1 w2 . Assume
95: that the number of ones in w is at least |w|
96:                                            2 , and w1 has less ones than w2 . By changing the partition by one
97: letter each time (by adding a subsequent letter to the end of w1 and removing one from the beginning), we
98: get an opposite situation in 21 n steps. That means that there exists a partition w = w1′ w2′ , |w1′ | = |w2′ |, such
99: that the number of ones in w1′ is the same as the number of ones in w2′ . Thus, we can pick an antipalindromic
100: subsequence 1k with k ≥ 21 n having the required properties.
101:     Conjecture 5 is about a palindromic subsequence aligned with some cut of the circular word into two
102: equal halves. There are n2 such cuts, so one attempt to simplify the conjecture is to look at only two cuts
103: which are “orthogonal”. This way we attempt to switch from the circular case to something close to the
104: linear case, which is often easier to deal with.
105: 
106: 
107:                                                          2
108: 
109:    Let w be a circular word of length n divisible by 4. Let w1 w2 w3 w4 be some partition of w into four linear
110: words of equal length. Let p1 p′1 and p2 p′2 , |p1 | = |p′1 |, |p2 | = |p′2 |, be the longest palindromic subsequences
111: of w such that p1 , p′1 , p2 , p′2 are subsequences of w1 w2 , w3 w4 , w2 w3 , w4 w1 respectively. Informally, these
112: two palindromes are aligned to two orthogonal cuts of the word w into two linear words of equal length.
113: The partitions w1 w2 , w3 w4 and w2 w3 , w4 w1 are two particular partitions (made by two orthogonal cuts)
114: considered among all n2 partitions in Conjecture 5.
115: Conjecture 7. For every word w of length n divisible by 4 and its every linear representation w = w1 w2 w3 w4 ,
116: the maximum of the lengths of p1 p′1 and p2 p′2 defined above is at least 21 n.
117:     We checked this conjecture up to n = 30 by computer. The worst known case is provided by the already
118: appeared word 0i (10)i 1i showing the tightness of the conjectured bound. The bound 31 n can be proved as
119: follows.
120: Proposition 8. For every word w of length n divisible by 4 and its every linear representation w =
121: w1 w2 w3 w4 , the maximum of the lengths of p1 p′1 and p2 p′2 is at least 13 n.
122: Proof. Suppose that |p1 p′1 | < 31 n. Then without loss of generality we can assume that the number of zeros
123: in w1 w2 and the number of ones in w3 w4 is less than 61 n. Then by the pigeonhole principle the number of
124:                                                                                    1
125: ones in both w1 and w2 , and the number of zeros in both w3 and w4 is at least 12    n. It means that we can
126:                            1                  1
127: pick a subsequence of 12     n zeros and then 12 n ones from w4 w1 and a symmetrical subsequence from w2 w3 .
128: Thus we get |p2 p′2 | ≥ 31 n.
129:    In fact, a slightly stronger statement that the total length of p1 p′1 and p2 p′2 is 23 n can be proved this way.
130: We conjecture the optimal bound for this value to be equal to n.
131:    Even being proved, the bound of 21 n in this conjecture would not improve the known bound for Conjecture
132: 5. However, Conjecture 7 deals with palindromic subsequences of only two linear words, and thus seems
133: to be easier to handle. Considering four regular cuts instead of two should already improve the bound for
134: Conjecture 5.

137: 3     Showing Asymptotic Tightness of Conjecture 4
138: In this section we present the main technical contribution of this paper, which is an infinite family of words
139: providing a better upper bound for Conjecture 4. In fact, we show a stronger result for words over an
140: arbitrary alphabet. Below we consider words over the alphabet {0, . . . , k − 1}, i.e. w ∈ {0, . . . , k − 1}∗ .
141: Definition 9. We say that w′ is a consecutive subword of a word w if there exist words u, v with w = uw′ v.
142:    We call a word w ∈ {0, . . . , k − 1}∗ of type n if it is a consecutive subword of (0n 1n . . . (k − 1)n )∗ or a
143: consecutive subword of ((k − 1)n . . . 1n 0n )∗ . In the first case we write w ∈ Sn′ , in the second case we write
144: w ∈ Sn′′ .
145:    Furthermore, we define Sn = Sn′ ∪ Sn′′ .
146:     Thus w ∈ Sn′ if it is a concatenation of blocks (0n 1n . . . (k − 1)n ), where the first and the last blocks may
147: be shorter, and analogously for w ∈ Sn′′ .
148:     We denote by w the word we get when exchanging every letter ℓ by (k − 1 − ℓ), e.g. 01 . . . (k − 1) =
149: (k − 1)(k − 2) . . . 0. We see directly that w ∈ Sn if and only if w ∈ Sn . Furthermore, we have that w ∈ Sn if
150: and only if wR ∈ Sn .
151: Lemma 10. Let w1 ∈ Sn1 be a word of length ℓ1 and w2 ∈ Sn2 be a word of length ℓ2 , where n1 > n2 . Then,
152: the length of the longest common subsequence of w1 and w2 is at most ℓk+1
153:                                                                       1 +ℓ2
154:                                                                             + ℓ1 nn12 + 2n2 .
211:     For the following considerations we will need a generalization of the notion of antipalindromes to the case
212: of non-binary alphabet. One natural version would be to say that w is an antipalindrome if w and wR differ
213: at every position. However, we work with a stronger notion, which still provides an interesting bound.
214: Definition 12. We call a word w ∈ {0, . . . , k − 1}∗ a strong antipalindrome if w = w R .
215: Theorem 13. For every ε > 0 there exists a circular word over the alphabet {0, . . . , k − 1} with equal
216: number of 0’s, 1’s, . . ., (k − 1)’s (n occurences of each letter) such that any palindromic and any strongly
217:                                                            2
218: antipalindromic subsequence of it is of length at most ( k+1  + ε)kn.

316: 3 n to be tight for words with equal number of zeros and ones, but we could not find an example providing
317: tightness). For palindromic subsequences we conjecture a stronger bound.
318: Conjecture 15. Every binary word of length n without three consecutive equal letters has a palindromic
319: subsequence of length at least 34 (n − 2).
320:    We checked this conjecture up to n = 30. The worst known cases are provided by the word (001)i (011)i ,
321: showing the tightness of the conjectured bound.
322:    Note that every binary word without two consecutive equal letters is a sequence of alternating zeros and
323: ones, and thus has a palindromic subsequence of length n − 1, where n is the length of the word. For a
324: three-letter alphabet it is not hard to prove the following.
325: Proposition 16. Let w be a word of length n over a three-letter alphabet. If w has no two consecutive equal
326: letters, then it has a palindromic subsequence of length at least 12 (n − 1).
327: Proof. Assume that the number of letters in w is even (otherwise, remove the last letter). Let w =
328: w1 w2 . . . wm where wi is a word of length 2. Each such word contains two different letters. Then for
329: each pair wi , wm−i+1 there exists a letter present in both words. By taking such a letter from every pair,
330: we get a palindrome of length m = 12 (n − 1).
331:     Based on these observations and computer experiments, we formulate the following conjecture.
332: Conjecture 17. Let w be a word of length n over an alphabet of size k, k ≥ 2. If w has no two consecutive
333:                                                                          1
334: equal letters, then it has a palindromic subsequence of length at least k−1 (n − 1).
335:     We checked this conjecture up to n = 21 for k = 4 and n = 18 for k = 5 by computer. A critical
336: example for this conjecture is provided by a word which is a concatenation of the word (a1 a2 )i and words
337: (aℓ+1 aℓ )i−1 aℓ+1 for 1 < ℓ < k − 1. This word shows that the conjectured bound is tight.
338: 
339: 
340: 5     Further Work
341: There are some questions besides the conjectures above that are worth mentioning. First, there is no known
342: reduction between the palindromic and antipalindromic conjectures. Thus, it is interesting to know whether
343: a bound for one of them implies some bound for the other one. Second, no non-trivial relation is known for
344: the bounds for the same conjecture but different size of alphabets.

276:   We close with some conjectures that illustrate the limits of our knowledge: As indicated
277: above, the band number of a ribbon knot is a lower bound on its ribbon fusion number.
278: We conjecture that the two are not always equal, i.e that there exists a ribbon knot that
279: bounds a ribbon surface with b bands, yet any ribbon disc for it has more than b bands.
280:   For example, the Whitehead double of any knot has Seifert genus one, and therefore has
281: band number at most two. Moreover, the untwisted Whitehead pattern is a saddle move
282: 
283: 8                                       CLAYTON MCDONALD
284: 
285: away from the 0-framed 2-cable pattern. Therefore we can construct a ribbon disc for the
286: double of a ribbon knot by doing this saddle move and then appending two ribbon discs for
287: the 2-cable. The natural band presentation of the ribbon disc for the untwisted Whitehead
288: double has 2b ` 1 bands if the original ribbon disc had b bands, making these Whitehead
289: doubles natural candidates for this conjecture. However, untwisted Whitehead doubles of
290: ribbon knots are all superslice [4], so they have band presentations with homotopically
291: trivial bands. Therefore, many of the properties of these knots will be hard to detect
292: algebraically.
293:    More generally, we expect a similar statement is true for every genus:
294: Conjecture 5. For every genus g, there exists a knot K with ribbon genus g for which
295: bpKq is lower than the number of bands in any ribbon surface of genus g.
296:   Demonstrating this difference seems challenging, as the lower bounds we know how to
297: prove for ribbon fusion number give lower bounds for band number as well.

22:                                                   enough, this is not true for non-orientable surfaces of even genus, for such surfaces
23:                                                   one has Ik (M ) > Ik (S2 ). We also discuss the connection between the Friedlander-

29:                                                                                  1. Introduction
30:                                            1.1. Preliminaries. Let (M, g) be a closed n-dimensional Riemannian manifold.
31:                                            Consider the Laplace-Beltrami operator ∆ = − divg ◦ gradg . It is an elliptic self-
32:                                            adjoint operator of second order. Its spectrum is a discrete collection of non-negative
33:                                            eigenvalues with finite multiplicities,
34: 
35:                                                                     0 = λ0 (g) < λ1 (g) ⩽ λ2 (g) ⩽ ... % +∞.
36:                                              We are interested in studying the extremal properties of λk (g). To this end we
37:                                            consider λk (g) as a functional on the space R(M ) of Riemannian metrics on M ,
191: 
192:             ON THE FRIEDLANDER-NADIRASHVILI INVARIANTS OF SURFACES                       5
193: 
194: Conjecture 1.7. For all even γ one has
195:                                      Ik (γ) = Ik (0).
196: The infimum is attained iff γ = 0.
197:    Another natural question is: why do the quantities Ik (γ) take different values for
198: odd and even γ? Careful analysis of the proof suggests that the answer lies in the
199: theory of cobordisms. We recall that two closed manifolds M and M 0 of the same
200: dimension are called cobordant if there exists a manifold with boundary W such that
201: the boundary ∂W is the disjoint union M tM 0 . Similarly, M is cobordant to 0 or null
202: cobordant if there exists W such that ∂W = M . One of the basic facts of cobordism
203: theory is that two manifolds are cobordant iff they can be obtained from one another
204: by a sequence of surgeries, see e.g. [Mil65]. In dimension 2 it implies that attaching
205: a handle does not change the cobordism class. This makes the cobordism theory for
206: surfaces rather straightforward. Indeed, since S2 and KL are obviously cobordant to
207: 0, one concludes that all orientable surfaces and all non-orientable surfaces of odd
208: genus are cobordant to 0. By the same token, all non-orientable surfaces of even
209: genus are cobordant to RP2 . The fact that RP2 is not cobordant to 0 can be shown
210: using Stiefel-Whitney characteristic classes, see e.g. [MS74].
211:    Assuming Conjecture 1.7, the quantity Ik is a cobordism invariant in dimension 2.
212: Inequality (1.5) can be interpreted as monotinicity of Ik with respect to addition of
213: a handle. The monotonicity then can be shown by choosing a degenerate sequence
214: of conformal classes such that the handle collapses in the limit. It turns out that
215: for such sequence the functional Λk (M, c) is continuous, see Remark 2.4. We believe
216: that the same phenomenon occurs in higher dimensions and propose the following
217: extension of Conjecture 1.7.
218: Conjecture 1.8. The quantities Ik are cobordism invariants, i.e. if M is cobordant
219: to M 0 then Ik (M ) = Ik (M 0 ). In particular, if M is cobordant to 0 then Ik (M ) =
220: Ik (Sdim M ) = Λk (Sdim M , [gcan ]).
221:    We remark that the cobordism theory has been used by Jammes in the paper [Jam08]
222: to study upper bounds on I1 . We plan to tackle Conjectures 1.7, 1.8 in the subsequent
223: papers.
224: Notation. Let us remind the reader that Σ      e γ denotes an orientable closed surface of
225: genus γ and Σγ denotes a non-orientable closed surface of genus γ, Ik (γ) = Ik (Σγ )
226: and Iek (γ) = Ik ( e
227:                   Σγ ). In general, we use tilde to denote anything related to orientable
228: objects. For example, π : Σ   e γ → Σγ denotes an orientable double cover. Moreover,
229: the notation Σ is usually used to denote a non-orientable surface and e      Σ is used to
230: denote an orientable surface. If we do not want to specify orientablity of the surface,
231: we denote it by M .
260: corresponding to hyperbolic metrics.
261: 2.1. Orientable hyperbolic surfaces: collar theorem. We start with the defini-
262: tion.
263: Definition 2.1. A Riemannian metric h of constant Gaussian curvature −1 is called
264: hyperbolic. A Riemannian surface (M, h) endowed with a hyperbolic metric h is called
265: a hyperbolic surface.
266:    Note that a hyperbolic surface necessarily has negative Euler characteristic. We
267: recall one of the underlying facts of this theory: the Collar Theorem. Orientable case
268: is well-known and can be found e.g. in [Bus92].
269: Definition 2.2. A compact Riemann surface Y of genus 0 with 3 boundary compo-
270: nents is called a pair of pants.
271: Theorem 2.3 (Collar theorem). Let (Σ,          e h) be an orientable compact hyperbolic sur-
272: face of genus γ ⩾ 2 and let c1 , c2 , . . . , cm be pairwise disjoint simple closed geodesics
273: on (Σ,
274:      e h). Then the following holds
275:   (i) m ⩽ 3γ − 3.
276:  (ii) There exist simple closed geodesics cm+1 , . . . , c3γ−3 which, together with c1 , . . . , cm ,
277:       decompose Σ e into pairs of pants.

522: Definition 2.7. Let M be a surface and let {cn } be a sequence of conformal classes
523: on M . Let hn ∈ cn be a canonical representative, i.e. h is hyperbolic if χ(M ) < 0 and
524: h is flat of unit volume if χ(M ) = 0. We say that cn degenerates if inj(M, hn ) → 0.
525: Furthermore, if (M, hn ) → (M d  ∞ , h∞ ) in the sense of Proposition 2.6 (if χ(M ) < 0) or
526:                                      c

529:    In [CKM19] it is shown that if the sequence cn does not degenerate and converges
530: to c then one has Λk (M, cn ) → Λk (M, c). The main technical result of the present
531: paper establishes the value of the limit of Λk (M, cn ) when the sequence of conformal
532: classes cn degenerates.
533: Theorem 2.8. Let M be a closed compact (orientable or non-orientable) surface

224:    The variance bound in Theorem 2 is sharp for a diagonal matrix. This observation
225: and several examples in Section 5 motivate the following conjecture.
226: Conjecture 1 (Diagonal matrices maximize log-minor variance). Let Mκ be the set of
227: positive-definite n×n matrices with condition number κ. For all k < n, κ, and M ∈ Mκ ,
228: there exists a diagonal matrix D ∈ Mκ such that
229:                                   var(Yk (M )) ≤ var(Yk (D)) .                            (6)

232:     The variance bounds (see Eqs. (3)–(5)) have important implications for the accuracy
233: of sample means of log-minors. We discuss these implications in Section 6.

23:                                                 utive words that are pairwise distinct and have the same length. They ask for the maximum k
24:                                                 such that every aperiodic recurrent word must contain a k-anti-power, and they prove that this
25:                                                 maximum must be 3, 4, or 5. We resolve this question by demonstrating that the maximum
26:                                                 is 5. We also conjecture that if W is a reasonably nice aperiodic morphic word, then there is
27:                                                 some constant C = C(W ) such that for all i, k ≥ 1, W contains a k-anti-power with blocks of
28:                                                 length at most Ck beginning at its ith position. We settle this conjecture for binary words that
29:                                                 are generated by a uniform morphism, characterizing the small exceptional set of words for
30:                                                 which such a constant cannot be found. This generalizes recent results of the second author,
31:                                                 Gaetz, and Narayanan that have been proven for the Thue-Morse word, which also show that
32:                                                 such a linear bound is the best one can hope for in general.
33: 
34: 
35: 
36:                                           1     Introduction
37:                                           The problems we are concerned with in this paper arise in the study of combinatorics on infinite
38:                                           words, or anti-Ramsey theory on Z. The original conception of Ramsey theory focused on un-
39:                                           avoidable structures in colored graphs and began with Ramsey’s work in 1930. Its extension to
101: automatic sequences.
102:    We state a conjecture here, left purposefully vague.
103: 
104: Conjecture 3. If W is a sufficiently well-behaved aperiodic morphic word, then there is a constant
105: C = C(W ) such that for all positive integers i and k, W contains a k-anti-power with blocks of
106: length at most Ck beginning at its ith position.
107: 
108:    Corollary 7 of [8] gives a similar result without the uniform linear bound. The works of the
109: second author and Narayanan [5, 11] confirm and extend this conjecture when W = t is the famous
110: Thue-Morse word and i = 1. The subsequent results of Gaetz [9] confirm this conjecture for W = t
111: and for every fixed i with a constant C that could depend on i. More precisely, let γi−1 (k) denote
112: the smallest positive integer m such that the factor of t of length km beginning at the ith position
113: of t is a k-anti-power. Gaetz proved that
114:                      1            γi−1 (k)    9             1           γi−1 (k)   3
115:                         ≤ lim inf          ≤         and      ≤ lim sup          ≤
116:                      10    k→∞       k       10             5     k→∞      k       2
117: for every positive integer i. The lower bounds in these estimates show that the linear upper bound
118: in Conjecture 3 is the best one can hope to prove in general.
119:    In Section 3, we settle Conjecture 3 in the case in which W is a binary word that is generated by a
120: uniform morphism. More precisely, we will see that the conjecture holds in all but a few exceptional
121: cases that are characterized in the following proposition. In this proposition, we assume our binary
122: word begins with 0, but the analogous statement certainly holds if the word starts with 1 and we
123: switch the roles of the letters 0 and 1 everywhere.
124: 
125: Proposition 4. Let W be a binary word that starts with 0 and is generated by an r-uniform
126: morphism µ. Then:
127: 
128:    • W is aperiodic if and only if µ(0) 6= µ(1) and W 6∈ {0000 · · · , 0111 · · · , 0101 · · · }.
129: 
130:    • W is uniformly recurrent if and only if it is 0000 · · · or µ(1) 6= 11 · · · 1.
131: 
132:    Let us remark that Conjecture 3 is easily seen to fail in both of these exceptional cases. Observing
133: the words W that fail to be aperiodic, we see that each has at most r distinct factors of any length,
134: 
135: 
136:                                                      3
137: 
138: and so cannot have k-anti-powers for k > r. If W fails to be uniformly recurrent, it follows from the
139: above characterization that W has constant factors of arbitrary length, inside which one certainly
140: cannot find anti-powers of bounded length.
141:   The following theorem verifies Conjecture 3 for all binary words that are generated by a uniform
142: morphism and that do not lie in the set of exceptional words listed in Proposition 4.
143: 
144: Theorem 5. If W is a uniformly recurrent aperiodic binary word that is generated by a uniform
145: morphism, then there is a constant C = C(W ) such that for all positive integers i and k, W contains
146: a k-anti-power with blocks of length at most Ck beginning at its ith position.
147: 
148: 
149: Terminology
150: Our words are always taken to be sequences of characters (letters) from a finite alphabet A. We
151: say a word is binary if it is a word over the two-element alphabet {0, 1}. By “infinite,” we always
152: mean infinite to the right. To reiterate, a factor of a word is a contiguous subword. Throughout
153: this article, we let [i, j] denote the factor of the infinite word W that starts in the ith position of
154: W and ends in the j th position. A prefix of a word is a factor that contains the first character, and
300: write [i, j] to refer to the factor of W beginning at index i and ending at index j. One important
301: point to keep in mind is that for each nonnegative integer t, the factor [tr + 1, tr + r] is equal to
302: either A or B because it is the image under µ of the (t + 1)st letter in W .
303:     We now proceed to prove Theorem 5, which states that Conjecture 3 holds if W is uniformly
304: recurrent. We start with some lemmas.
305: 
306: Lemma 8. Let W be an aperiodic binary word generated by an r-uniform morphism µ with µ(0) = A
307: and µ(1) = B. If [γ + 1, γ + 3r] is equal to AAB or BBA, then r divides γ.
308: 
309: Proof. We only consider the case in which [γ + 1, γ + 3r] = AAB; the proof is similar when
310: [γ + 1, γ + 3r] = BBA. Suppose instead that r does not divide γ, and let h ∈ {1, . . . , r − 1} be
311: such that r divides γ + h. Let D, E, and F be the three consecutive factors of length r starting
312: at index γ + h + 1. That is, D = [γ + h + 1, γ + h + r], E = [γ + h + r + 1, γ + h + 2r], and
313: F = [γ + h + 2r + 1, γ + h + 3r]. Then D, E, and F are each images of a single letter under µ, so
314: each is equal to either A or B.
315:     Because W is aperiodic, we know that A 6= B. We are going to prove by induction on j that
316: 
437: 
438: 
439: 4     Further Work
440: In Section 3, we settled part of our vague Conjecture 3. An obvious next step would involve proving
441: additional cases of this conjecture. One way to do this would be to remove the “binary” condition
442: from Theorem 5. That theorem also specifies that the word under consideration is generated by
443: a uniform morphism; one could attempt to remove this “uniform” condition. Of course, we would
444: certainly like to see the conjecture proved in its entirety. Since the conjecture is not completely
445: precise, this would amount to classifying those morphic words W for which there exists a constant
446: C(W ) satisfying the property stated in the conjecture.

211:

791: 2.1. Foams.
792: 
793: Definition 2.1. A gl N -foam (or simply foam) F is a collection of facets F (F ) = (Σ i ) i∈ I ,
794: that is a finite collection of oriented connected surfaces with boundary, together with
795: the following data:
796:        • A labeling ℓ : (Σ i ) i∈ I → {0,... , N }.
797:        • A set of decorations, that is, for each facet f , a symmetric polynomial P f in
798:          ℓ( f ) variables with rational coefficients. For a given facet f , we say that f is
799:          trivially decorated if P f = 1.
800:        • A “gluing recipe” of the facets along their boundaries such that when glued
801:          together using the recipe we have the three possible local models: the neigh-
802:          borhood of a point is homeomorphic to a surface, a tripod times an interval
803:          or the cone of the 1-skeleton of the tetrahedron as depicted below.
804: 
805:                                                                            b       a
806:                                              a                                             a+b
852: is given by the concept of canopolis of foams; we refer to [BN05, ETW17] for more
853: details about this approach.
854: 
855: Definition 2.2. The category Foam consists of the following data:
856:       • Objects are closed MOY graphs in R2 ,
857:       • Morphisms from Γ0 to Γ1 are (ambient isotopy class relatively to the bound-
858:          ary of) decorated foams in R2 × [0, 1] whose boundary is contained in the
859: 
860: 3This agrees with Khovanov’s convention used in [Kho04].
861: 
862:                  A QUANTUM CATEGORIFICATION OF THE ALEXANDER POLYNOMIAL                           15
863: 
864: 
865:          R2 × {0, 1}. The part of the boundary in R2 × {0} (resp. R2 × {1}) is required
866:          to be equal to −Γ0 (resp. Γ1 ).
867: Composition of morphisms is given by stacking foams and rescaling.
868:

892: Definition 2.3. Let k be a non-negative integer and Γ0 and Γ1 two vinyl graphs
893: of level k. Let F be a foam with boundary embedded in A × [0, 1]. Suppose that
894: F ∩ (A ×{0}) = −Γ0 and F ∩ (A ×{1}) = Γ1 . We say F is a vinyl (Γ1 , Γ0 )-foam of level k if
895: for every point x of F , the normal line to F at x is not contained in P x . See Figure 5
896: for an example.

919: Definition 2.4. The category TLFk of vinyl foams of level k consists of the following
920: data:
921:       • The objects are elements of Vk i. e. vinyl graphs of level k,
922:       • Morphisms from Γ0 to Γ1 are (ambient isotopy classes of) vinyl (Γ1 , Γ0 )-foams.
923: Composition is given by stacking vinyl foams together and rescaling. By this process,
924: some facets may receive different decorations (i. e. symmetric polynomials), the new
925: 
926: 16                        LOUIS-HADRIEN ROBERT AND EMMANUEL WAGNER
927: 
928: 
929: decorations of these facets are the products of all the decorations they receive. In the
930: category TLFk we have one distinguished object which consists of a single essential
931: circle with label k. We denote it by S k . The degree degT (F ) of a vinyl foam F is equal
932: to degΛ0 ( F ) (see below Definition 2.5). Note that the additive under the composition
933: in TLFk .

937: Definition 2.5. We define the degree degΛ
938:                                         N of a decorated foam F as the sum of the
939: following contributions:
940:        • For each facet f with label a, set d ( f ) = a( N − a)χ( f ), where χ stands for the
941:          Euler characteristic;
942:        • For each interval binding b (i. e. not circle-like binding) surrounded by facets
943:          with labels a, b and a + b, set d (b) = ab + (a + b)( N − a − b);
944:        • For each singular point p surrounded with facets with labels a, b, c, a + b,
945:          b + c, a + b + c, set d ( p) = ab + bc + cd + da + ac + bd with d = N − a − b − c;
946:        • Finally set
947:                                    X           X       X        X
948:                    degΛ N (F ) = −    d ( f ) + d (b) − d ( p) + deg(P f ),
949:                                     f          b          p          f

953:      The degree can be thought of as an analogue of the Euler characteristic.

955: Definition 2.6. Let N be a positive integer and F a closed foam. A gl N -coloring
956: (or simply coloring) of a foam F is a map c from F (F ), the set of facets of F to
957: P ({1,... , N }), the powerset of {1,... , N }, such that:
958:        • For each facet f , the number of elements # c( f ) of c( f ) is equal to l ( f ).
959:        • For each binding joining a facet f 1 with label a, a facet f 2 with label b, and a
960:           facet f 3 with label a + b, we have c( f 1 ) ∪ c( f 2 ) = c( f 3 ). This condition is called
961:           the flow condition.
962: A gl N -colored foam is a foam together with a gl N -coloring. A pigment is an element
963: of {1,... , N }.
964: 
965: Lemma 2.7 ([RW17, Lemma 2.5]).                 (1) If (F, c) is a colored foam and i is an el-
966:        ement of {1,... , N }, the union (with the identification coming from the gluing
967:        procedure) of all the facets which contain the pigment i in their color set is a
968:        surface. The restriction we imposed on the orientations of facets ensures that
986:           of these circles is a union of bindings; for every circle the bindings are either
987:           all positive or all negative with respect to ( i, j).

989: Definition 2.8. Let (F, c) be a colored foam.
990:     (1) If i an element of {1,... , N }, the union (with the identification coming from
991:         the gluing procedure) of all the facets which contain the pigment i in their
992:         colors is a surface. This surface is called the monochrome surface of (F, c)
993:         associated with i and denoted by F i ( c).
994:     (2) If i < j is are two elements of {1,... , N }, the symmetric difference of F i ( c) and
995:         F j ( c) is called the bichrome surface of (F, c) associated with i, j and denoted
996:         by F i j ( c).
997:     (3) If i < j are two elements of {1,... , N }, a circle in F i ( c) ∩ F j ( c) ∩ F i j ( c) is positive
998:         (resp. negative) of type ( i, j) if it consists of positive (resp. negative) bindings
999:         with respect to ( i, j). We denote by θ+           ij

1097: Definition 2.14. Let Γ be a vinyl graph of level k and F a vinyl (Γ, S k )-foam. The
1098: foam F is tree-like if for any θ in [0, 2π], Pθ ∩ F is a tree.

1100: Proposition 2.15 ([RW18, Lemmas 3.36 and 3.38]).           • Let Γ be a vinyl graph
1101:       of level k and F a vinyl (Γ, S k )-foam. Then it is ∞-equivalent to a Q-linear
1102:       combination of tree-like foams with non-trivial decorations only on facets
1103:       bounding Γ.
1104:     • Let Γ be a vinyl graph of level k and F1 and F2 be two tree-like foams with
1105:       non-trivial decorations only on facets bounding Γ. If on those facets the deco-
1106:       rations of F1 and F2 coincide then F1 and F2 are ∞-equivalent.
1107:

1109: is especially simple. In Subsection 2.5, we derive from this proposition a new con-
1110: struction of the state spaces associated with vinyl graphs which only deal with 1-

1122: we take the polynomial 〈cl(F )〉k and set x1 = · · · = x k to get an rational number.

1140: Definition 2.17. Let Γ be a vinyl graph. Define TLF (Γ) to be the Q-vector space
1141: generated by all vinyl (Γ, S k )-foams. Consider the bilinear map (•, •)Γ : TLF (Γ) ⊗
1142: TLF (Γ) → Q given by:
1143:                                     (F,G )Γ = ⟪FG ⟫1 ,

1145: S 1 (Γ) the Q-vector space TLF (Γ)/ker(•, •).

1148: any vinyl graph Γ of level k, the graded Q-vector space S 1 (Γ) is equal to S k,1 (Γ) ⊗Q[T ]
1149: Q where T acts on Q by 0. Hence, most properties of S k,1 translate to similar ones
1150: for S 1 . We list some of them below.

1384: Notation 2.26. If e has label 1, its space of decorations is Q[ x1 ]. The monomial x1 is
1385: represented by a dot •. Similarly the monomial x1a is represented by a dots or by •a .

1388: Definition 2.27. Let Γ be a vinyl graph of level k and denote by E (Γ) its set of edges.
1389: An omnichrome coloring of Γ is a map c : E (Γ) → P ({ X 1 ,... , X k }) such that:
1390:       • For all e in E (Γ), # c( e) = ℓ( e).
1391:       • For each ray r of A , the union of the c( e) for the edges e intersecting r is
1392:         equal to { X 1 ,... , X k }.
1393:       • If e 1 , e 2 and e 3 are three adjacent edges with ℓ( e 1 ) = ℓ( e 2 )+ℓ( e 3 ), then c( e 1 ) =
1394:         c( e 2 ) ⊔ c( e 3 ).
1395: The set c( e) is called the color of e.
1396: 
1397: Remark 2.28. The definition of omnichrome colorings implies that:
1398:       • For each vertex of Γ, the color of the big edge is the disjoint union of the
1399:         colors of the two small edges.
1400:       • For each ray r of A , the union of sets c( e) for the edges e intersecting r is
1401:         actually a disjoint union.

1586: 3.1. The category of marked foams.
1587: 
1588: Definition 3.1. Let Γb and Γ t be two marked vinyl graphs. A marked (Γ t , Γb )-foam
1589: is a vinyl (Γ t , Γb )-foam F with a smooth path γ from the base point of Γb to the base
1590: point of Γ t such that γ does not intersect any singular locus of F .
1591: 
1592: Remark 3.2.       (1) All vinyl graphs with an edge labeled 1 can be considered as
1593:       marked vinyl graphs by simply choosing a base point. Such a statement is
1594:       not true for foams since such path γ may not exists.
1595:   (2) Note that for a marked (Γ t , Γb )-foam to exist, it is necessary (and actually
1596:       sufficient) that Γb and Γ t have the same level and depth.
1597: 
1598: Definition 3.3. For each level k and depth d , define the category Foamk,d : objects
1599: are marked vinyl graphs of level k and depth d and morphisms from Γb to Γ t are (for-
1600: mal linear combination of) isotopy classes (fixing the boundaries on top and bottom)
1601: of marked (Γ t , Γb )-foams.
1602: 
1603: 3.2. Three times the same space.
1604: 
1605: Notation 3.4. Let i be a non-negative integer, E i denotes the i th elementary sym-
1606: metric polynomial and H i the i th complete homogeneous symmetric polynomial (the
1607: set of variables will be clear from the context). If i is strictly larger that the number
1608: of variables, it is understood that E i = 0.
1609:

1611: X e (Γ) the vinyl foam Γ × [0, 1] decorated by 1 on every facet but on e × [0, 1] where the
1613: notations and still denote by X e the endomorphism of S 1 (Γ) induced by X e (Γ) (i. e.
1614: S 1 ( X e (Γ))).
