2706: composition:
2900:    Using the equivariant version of the symmetric gl1 -homology, one could define an
2901: equivariant gl0 -homology. The different equivalent definitions given in Section 3.2
2902: may not be equivalent in the equivariant context anymore. Presumably, one should
2903: use the definition given by the ⋆-evaluation (see Definition 3.14).
2904: 
2905: 46                   LOUIS-HADRIEN ROBERT AND EMMANUEL WAGNER
2906: 
2907: 
2908: 6.3. Colored homology. In this paper, we are only concerned with uncolored in-
2909: variant. This means that every strand of the braids we look at has label 1. The
2910: vocabulary of vinyl graphs enables to speak about more colors. There is a standard
2911: way to deal with the braiding in the colored case (see for instance [ETW17]). One
2912: should then give the meaning of the base point on an edge with a label different from
2913: 1. We believe that replacing a base point on an edge of label ℓ by a small digon of
2914: type (ℓ, ℓ − 1, 1) with a base point as depicted on Figure 15 would give a reasonable
2915: definition.
2916: 
2928: The proof relies on the fact that provided the base point is close enough from the
2929: right-most strand, then the dimension of the space associated with a marked vinyl
2930: graph is known not to depend on the base point. Such a statement is not known
2931: when the base point is arbitrary. This is essentially the content of Conjecture 3.25.
2932:    Assume that such a construction would work. It is reasonable to wonder what
2933: it would categorify. From the very definition, we would have that it is the colored
2934: HOMFLY-PT polynomial specialized at a = 1. The HOMFLY-PT polynomial at a = 1
2935: of a knot with color n is equal to the Alexander polynomial at q n . This follows from
2936: the work of Morton and Lukac [ML03] and a classical formula of Torres [Tor53] for
2937: the Alexander polynomial of cablings. This fact was explained to us by Hugh Morton.
2938: 
2939: Conjecture 6.1. The construction described above yields a knot invariant categori-
2940: fying the Alexander polynomial.
2941: 
2942:    If this were true, the colored gl0 -homology would give an infinite family of cate-
2943: gorifications of the Alexander polynomial. Then it would be interesting to know if all
2944: these categorifications are equivalent or not.
2945: 
2946: 6.4. Links. The theory described in this paper is only concerned with knots: we
2947: need a base point and we can only move the base point along a given component.
2948: Hence it could be extended for free to links with a marked component. However, we
2949: believe that the construction is independent of the marked component.

123: (iii) a path v1 v2 v3 and a further vertex v4 , such that degG (v1 ) = degG (v3 ) = 2,
124:       degG (v2 ) = 3, v2 v4 is a cut-edge of G, and the component containing v2 in
125:       G − v2 v4 has order at least 4.

128: it impossible to satisfy the parity conditions with a spanninng subgraph of all-positive
129: degrees.
267:        Corollary 14. If G is a connected K1,r -free graph with δ(G) ≥ r ≥ 3, then G has the
268:        strong parity property.
269:           We think that the following strengthening of Theorems 11 and 12 is also true.
270:        Conjecture 1. Every 2-edge-connected graph of minimum degree at least three has the
271:        strong parity property.
272:           To prove the conjecture for a graph G, it would be enough to find a p-factor F ⊂ G
273:        mentioned in Theorem 9. However, the condition δ(G) ≥ 3 is not strong enough to
274:        ensure the existence of such a factor. A general counterexample is the class of 3-regular
275:        graphs having no Hamiltonian path. Indeed, in those graphs any spanning tree contains
276:        a vertex of degree three because the graphs of maximum degree less than 3 are disjoint
277:        unions of paths and cycles. On the other hand, for 3-regular graphs we can prove the
278:        conjecture, even in a slightly stronger form.

280:        contained in a path, then G has the strong parity property.

14: 
15: 
16:                                                                                                Abstract
17:                                                     The sunflower conjecture is one of the most well-known open problems in combinatorics. It has several
18:                                                  applications in theoretical computer science, one of which is DNF compression, due to Gopalan, Meka
19:                                                  and Reingold (Computational Complexity, 2013). In this paper, we show that improved bounds for DNF
20:                                                  compression imply improved bounds for the sunflower conjecture, which is the reverse direction of the
21:                                                  DNF compression result. The main approach is based on regularity of set systems and a structure-vs-
22:                                                  pseudorandomness approach to the sunflower conjecture.
23: 
24: 
25:                                           1      Introduction
26:                                           The sunflower conjecture is one of the most well-known open problems in combinatorics. An r-sunflower is
27:                                           a family of r sets S1 , . . . , Sr where all pairwise intersections are the same. A w-set system is a collection
28:                                           of sets where each set has size at most w. Erdős and Rado [ER60] asked how large can a w-set system be,
29:                                           without containing an r-sunflower. They proved an upper bound of w!(r − 1)w , and conjectured that the
30:                                           bound can be improved.
31:                                           Conjecture 1.1 (Sunflower conjecture, [ER60]). Let r ≥ 3. There is a constant cr such that any w-set
32:                                           system F of size |F | ≥ cw
33:                                                                    r contains an r-sunflower.
34: 
35:                                               60 years later, only lower order improvements have been achieved, and the best bounds are still of the
36:                                           order of magnitude of about ww for any fixed r, same as in the original theorem of Erdős and Rado. A good
37:                                           survey on the current bounds is [Kos00].
38:                                               Sunflowers have been useful in various areas in theoretical computer science. Some examples include
39:                                           monotone circuit lower bounds [Raz85, Ros10], barriers for improved algorithms for matrix multiplication
40:                                           [ASU13] and faster deterministic counting algorithms via DNF compression [GMR13]. The focus on this
41:                                           paper is on this latter application, in particular DNF compression.
42:                                               A DNF (Disjunctive Normal Form) is disjunction of conjunctive terms. The size of a DNF is the number
43:                                           of terms, and the width of a DNF is the maximal number of literals in a term. It is a folklore result that any
44:                                           DNF of size s can be approximated by another DNF of width O(log s), by removing all terms of larger width.
73: (iii) flower has size (1/ε)O(w) .
74:    It is natural to speculate that a similar bound holds for upper bound DNFs.
75: 
76: Conjecture 1.4 (Improved upper bound DNF compression). Let f be a width-w DNF. Then for every ε > 0
77: there exists a width-w DNF fupper such that
78:  (i) f (x) ≤ fupper (x) for all x.
79:  (ii) fupper and f are ε-close.
80: (iii) fupper has size (1/ε)O(w) .
81:    To study the connection between DNF compression and sunflowers, we would need an analog of Conjec-
82: ture 1.4 for monotone DNFs.
83: Conjecture 1.5 (Improved upper bound monotone DNF compression). In Conjecture 1.4, if f is a mono-
84: tone DNF, then fupper can also be taken to be a monotone DNF.
85:     The main result of this paper is that Conjecture 1.5 implies an improved bound for the sunflower con-
86: jecture, with a bound of (log w)O(w) instead of the current bound of O(w)w . Thus, the connection between
87: sunflower theorems and DNF compression goes both ways. We note that the proof of [GMR13] is also true
88: for monotone DNF compression.
89:     To simplify the presentation, we assume from now on that w ≥ 2. This will allow us to assume that
90: log w > 0. In any case, for w = 1 the sunflower conjecture is trivial, as any 1-set system of size r is an
91: r-sunflower.
92: Theorem 1.6 (Main theorem). Assume that Conjecture 1.5 holds. Then for any r ≥ 3 there exists a constant
93: cr such that the following holds. Any w-set system F of size |F | ≥ (log w)cr w contains an r-sunflower.
94:     In fact, Theorem 1.6 holds even with a slightly weaker conjecture instead of Conjecture 1.5, where the
95: size bound can be assumed to be ((log w)/ε)O(w) instead of (1/ε)O(w) .
96: 
97: 
98:                                                        2
99: 
100: 1.1    Proof overview
101: The proof of Erdős and Rado [ER60] is by a simple case analysis which we now recall. Let F be a w-set
102: system. Then either F contains r pairwise disjoint sets, which are in particular an r-sunflower; or at most
103:                                                                                                                1
104: r − 1 sets whose union intersects all other sets. In the latter case, there is an element that belongs to a (r−1)w
105: fraction of the sets in F . If we restrict to these sets, and remove the common element, then we reduced the
106:                                               |F |
107: problem to a (w − 1)-set system of size (r−1)w     . The proof concludes by induction.
118:   S∈F   i∈S xi . This equivalence will be useful in the proof, as at different stages one of these viewpoints is
119: more convenient.
120:    The notions of “lower bound DNF” flower and “upper bound DNF” fupper used in Theorem 1.2, Theo-
121: rem 1.3 and Conjecture 1.5 have analogs for set systems, which we refer to as proper lower bound and upper
122: bound DNFs (or set systems). For the purpose of this high level overview, we ignore this distinction here.
123: 
124: Section 3: Approximate sunflowers. The notion of approximate sunflowers was introduced by Rossman
125: [Ros10]. It relies on the notion of satisfying set systems.
126:    Let F be a set system on a universe X. We say that F is (p, ε)-satisfying if Prx∼Xp [fF (x) = 1] > 1 − ε,
127: where fF is the corresponding monotone DNF for F , and Xp is the p-biased distribution on X. The
128: importance of satisfying set systems in our context is that a (1/r, 1/r)-satisfying set system contains r
129: pairwise disjoint sets (Claim 3.4).
130:    Let K = ∩S∈F S be the intersection of all sets in F . We say that F is a (p, ε)-approximate sunflower if
131: the set system {S \K : S ∈ F } is (p, ε)-satisfying. An interesting connection between approximate sunflowers
132: and sunflowers is that a (1/r, 1/r)-approximate sunflower contains an r-sunflower (Corollary 3.5).
133: 
134: Section 4: Regular set systems. Let D be a distribution over subsets of X. We say that D is regular
139: lower bound set system (Claim 4.5). These facts will turn out to be useful later.
140: 
141: Section 5: Regular set systems are (1/2, 1/2)-satisfying. In this section, we focus on regular set
142: systems F , or equivalently regular DNFs f = fF . We show that, assuming Conjecture 1.5 (or the slightly
143: weaker Conjecture 5.2), any κ-regular DNF of width w, where κ = (log w)O(1) , is (1/2, 1/2)-satisfying.
144: Namely, Pr[f (x) = 1] ≥ 1/2, where x is uniformly chosen. In particular, this implies that F contains two
145: disjoint sets. However, our goal is to prove that F contains an r-sunflower for r ≥ 3, so we are not done yet.
146: 
147: Section 6: Intersecting regular set systems. Let α(w, r) denote the maximal κ such that there exists
148: a κ-regular w-set system without r pairwise disjoint sets. It is easy to prove that the sunflower theorem
149: holds for any set system of size |F | > α(w, r)w (Claim 6.4). However, our discussion so far only allows us to
150: bound β(w) = α(w, 2); concretely, assuming Conjecture 1.5 we have β(w) ≤ (log w)O(1) .
151: 
152: 
153: 
154: 
155:                                                         3
156: 
157:     We show (Lemma 6.6) that nontrivial upper bounds on β(w) imply related upper bounds on α(w, r)
158: for every r. Concretely, if β(w) ≤ (log w)O(1) then α(w, r) ≤ (log w)cr where cr > 0 are constants. This
159: concludes the proof, as we get that any w-set system of size |F | ≥ (log w)cr w must contain an r-sunflower.

161: Acknowledgements. We thank Ray Li for spotting a subtle mistake in the previous version (its solution
162: necessitated restricting some claims to non-trivial or non-redundant set systems). We also thank CCC
163: reviewers for pointing out some early mistakes.
178: elements over which F is defined then we write F ⊆ P(X).
179:    A DNF is non-redundant if no term implies another term. A DNF is non-trivial if it is not a constant
180: function. This motivates the following definitions for the corresponding set systems.
181: Definition 2.1 (Non-redundant set systems). A set system F is non-redundant if it is an anti-chain.
182: Namely, it does not contain two distinct sets S1 , S2 with S1 ⊂ S2 .
183: Definition 2.2 (Non-trivial set systems). A set system F is non-trivial if it is not empty, and doesn’t
184: contain the empty set.
185:    To recall, we consider both lower bound and upper bound DNFs. As our main motivation is to better
186: understand sunflowers, we restrict attention to monotone DNFs from now on; however, all the definitions
187: can be easily adapted for general DNFs.
188:    We next define proper upper and lower bound DNFs. Proper lower bound DNFs are obtained by removing
189: terms from the DNF, and proper upper bound DNFs are obtained by removing variables from terms in the
190: DNF. We describe both in terms of the corresponding set systems.
191: Definition 2.3 (Proper lower bound DNF / set system). Let F be a set system. A proper lower bound set
192: system for F is simply a sub set system F ′ ⊆ F . Observe that indeed
193: 
194:                                                  fF ′ (x) ≤ fF (x)       ∀x.
195: 
196: Definition 2.4 (Proper upper bound DNF / set system). Let F be a set system. A proper upper bound
197: set system for F is a set system F ′ that satisfies the following: for each S ∈ F there exists S ′ ∈ F ′ such
198: that S ′ ⊆ S. Observe that indeed
199:                                           fF ′ (x) ≥ fF (x)     ∀x.
200:     For monotone DNFs, upper bounds and proper upper bounds are the same.
201: Claim 2.5. Let F , F ′ be set systems over the same universe, such that
202: 
203:                                                  fF ′ (x) ≥ fF (x)       ∀x.
204: 
205: Then F ′ is a proper upper bound set for F .
206: 
207:                                                               4

209: Proof. Assume not. Then there exists S ∈ F such that there is no S ′ ∈ F ′ with S ′ ⊆ S. Let x = 1S be the
210: indicator vector for S. Then fF (x) = 1 but fF ′ (x) = 0, a contradiction.
211: Corollary 2.6. In Conjecture 1.5, we may assume that fupper is a proper upper bound DNF for f .
212:    We note that the lower and upper bound DNFs in [GMR13] are in fact proper lower and upper bounds,
213: and the same holds for the lower bound DNF in [LZ18].

216: 3     Approximate sunflowers
217: We introduce the notion of approximate sunflowers, first defined by Rossman [Ros10]. We first need some
218: notation. Given a finite set X and 0 < p < 1, we denote by Xp the p-biased distribution over X, where
219: W ∼ Xp is sampled by including each x ∈ X in W independently with probability p. The definition of
220: approximate sunflowers relies on the notion of a satisfying set system.
221: Definition 3.1 (Satisfying set system). Let F ⊆ P(X) be a set system and let 0 < p, ε < 1. We say that F
222: is (p, ε)-satisfying if
223:                                       Pr [∃S ∈ F : S ⊆ W ] > 1 − ε.
224:                                        W ∼Xp
225: 
226:     Equivalently, if fF : {0, 1}X → {0, 1} is the DNF corresponding to F , then F is (p, ε)-satisfying if
227: 
228:                                             Pr [fF (x) = 1] > 1 − ε.
229:                                            x∼Xp
230: 
231:     An approximate sunflower is a set system which is satisfying if we first remove the common intersection
232: of all the sets in the set system.
233: Definition 3.2 (Approximate sunflower). Let F ⊆ P(X) be a set system and let 0 < p, ε < 1. Let
234: K = ∩S∈F S. Then F is a (p, ε)-approximate sunflower if the set system {S \ K : S ∈ F } is (p, ε)-satisfying.
235:    Rossman proved an analog of the sunflower theorem for approximate sunflowers. Li, Lovett and Zhang
236: [LLZ18] reproved this theorem by using a connection to randomness extractors.
237: Theorem 3.3 (Approximate sunflower lemma [Ros10]). Let F be a w-set system and let ε > 0. If |F | ≥
238: w! · (1.71 log(1/ε)/p)w then F contains a (p, ε)-approximate sunflower.
239:    To conclude this section, we show that satisfying set systems contain many disjoint sets, and hence
240: approximate sunflowers contain sunflowers.
241: Claim 3.4. Let F be a non-trivial set system, r ≥ 2, and assume that F is a (1/r, 1/r)-satisfying. Then F
242: contains r pairwise disjoint sets.
243: Proof. Let F ⊆ P(X). Consider a uniform random coloring of X with r colors. A coloring induces a
244: partition of X into X = W1 ∪ . . . ∪ Wr , where Wc is the set of all elements that attain the color c. Given
245: a color c ∈ [r], a set S ∈ F is c-monochromatic if all its elements attain the color c. Observe that for each
246: color c,
268: element belongs to too many sets, no pair of elements belongs to too many sets, and so on. It is closely related
269: to the notion of block min-entropy studied in the context of lifting theorems in communication complexity
270: [GLM+ 16].
271: Definition 4.1 (Regular distribution). Let X be a finite set, and let D be a distribution on non-empty
272: subsets S ⊆ X. The distribution D is κ-regular if for any set T ⊆ X it holds that
273: 
274:                                                              Pr [T ⊆ S] ≤ κ−|T | .
275:                                                              S∼D
276: 
277: Remark 4.2. Note that we need to restrict D to be supported on non-empty sets, as otherwise the regularity
278: can be infinite.
279: Definition 4.3 (Regular set system). A non-trivial set system F is κ-regular if there exists a κ-regular
280: distribution D supported on the sets in F .
281:     The following claims show that if F is a κ-regular set system then any proper upper bound set system
282: for it is also κ-regular, and any “large” proper lower bound set system is approximately κ-regular.

382: Proof of Lemma 5.3. We will need several properties from κ0 in the proof. To simplify notations, we short-
383: hand κ0 (w/2, ε) for κ0 (⌊w/2⌋, ε) throughout. The constant c > 1 below is the absolute constant from
384: Conjecture 1.5. We need a constant c′ > 1 so that the following conditions are satisfied:
385:  (i) κ0 (w, ε) ≥ w(2w log(1/ε))2 for w = 1, 2 and ε > 0.

388: One can check that the function τ (w, ε) = (log w)/ε satisfies τ (w/2, ε(1 − 1/ log w)) ≤ τ (w, ε), with equality
389:                                                         ′

391: We then take c0 = 2c′ .
392:    The proof of lemma Lemma 5.3 is by induction on w. The base cases are w = 1 and w = 2 which follow

397: f1 = fF1 be the corresponding DNF. Applying Conjecture 5.2 to f1 with error parameter γ, we obtain that
398: there exists a γ-approximate proper upper bound DNF f2 for f1 of size s = ((log w)/γ)cw ≤ ((log w)/ε)2cw .
399: Let F2 be the corresponding set system to f2 , and observe that F2 is a proper upper bound set system for
400: F1 . Let F3 = (F \ F1 ) ∪ F2 and let f3 = fF3 be the corresponding DNF. Then
401:

405: and we are done.

407: then by Claim 4.4 we obtain that F3 is also κ-regular. Let D be a κ-regular distribution supported on F3 .
408: Let F4 = {S ∈ F3 : |S| ≥ w/2}, where F4 ⊆ F2 . As each set S ∈ F4 has size |S| ≥ w/2 then, since D is
409: κ-regular, we have
410:                                              D(S) ≤ κ−w/2 .
438: However we only proved that a regular enough set system is (1/2, 1/2)-satisfying so far. In this section, we
439: prove that this is enough to show the existence of an r-sunflower for any constant r, and with a comparable
440: condition of regularity. Our proof is based on a the study of regular intersecting set systems.
441: Definition 6.1 (Intersecting set system). A set system is intersecting if any two sets in it intersect. In
442: other words, it does not contain two disjoint sets.
443: Definition 6.2. For w ≥ 1, r ≥ 2 define
444: 
445:            α(w, r) = sup{κ : ∃non-trivial κ-regular w-set system without r pairwise disjoint sets}.
446: 
447: It will be convenient to shorthand β(w) = α(w, 2), which can equivalently be defined as
448: 
449:                       β(w) = sup{κ : ∃non-trivial κ-regular intersecting w-set system}.
450: 
451: Claim 6.3. α(w + 1, r) ≥ α(w, r) and α(w, r + 1) ≥ α(w, r) for all w ≥ 1, r ≥ 2.
452: Proof. The first claim follows by our definition that a w-set system is a set system where all sets have size
453: at most w. In particular, any w-set system is also a (w + 1)-set system and hence α(w + 1, r) ≥ α(w, r). The
454: second claim holds since a set system that does not contain r pairwise disjoint sets, also does not contain
455: r + 1 pairwise disjoint sets.
456:    We start by showing that upper bounds on α(w, r) directly translate to upper bounds on sunflowers.
506: for
507:                                            κ = min(t/2, m1/t ).
508: Setting t = Θ(log w/ log log w) gives κ = Θ(log w/ log log w).
509:    We conjecture that this is essentially tight. In fact, by Claim 3.4 we have that
510: 
511:                                                  β(w) ≤ γ(w)
512: 
513: As we proved, Conjecture 5.2 implies γ(w) = (log w)O(1) , thus it also implies β(w) = (log w)O(1) .
514: Proof of Lemma 6.6. For w, r ≥ 1 define
515: 
516:                                             η(w, r) = r2r+1 β(wr)r .
517: 
518: We will first prove that
519:                                       α(w, 2r) ≤ max (η(w, r), 2α(w, r))
520: 
521:                                                        10
522: 
523: and then that this implies the bound
524:                                                         α(w, r) ≤ η(w, r).
525:     Let F be a non-trivial κ-regular w-set system which does not contain 2r pairwise disjoint sets, where
526: κ > max (η(w, r), 2α(w, r)). We will show that this leads to a contradiction.

622: Proof of Theorem 1.6. We put all the pieces together. Let w ≥ 2. Assume Conjecture 5.2 holds. Lemma 5.3
623: gives that
624:                                              γ(w) ≤ (log w)c
625: for some constant c ≥ 1. Claim 5.5 then gives that
626: 
627:                                                        β(w) ≤ γ(w)
628: 
629: and Lemma 6.6 gives that
630: 
631:                             α(w, r) ≤ r2r+1 β(wr)r ≤ r2r+1 (log(wr))cr ≤ (log w)cr
632: 
633: for some constant cr ≥ 1. Finally, Claim 6.4 shows that if F is a w-set system of size |F | ≥ (log w)cr w then
634: F contains an r-sunflower.

637: 7     Further discussions
638: Recall that β(w) is the maximal κ such that there exists an intersecting κ-regular w-set system.
639: Conjecture 7.1. β(w) ≤ (log w)O(1) .
640: 
641: 
642: 
643:                                                             12
644: 
645:     We would like to point out that in Conjecture 7.1, the assumption that the set system is intersecting
646: cannot be replaced by a weaker assumption that it is almost intersecting, namely that most pairs of sets
647: intersect. To see that, consider the following example.
648: Example 7.2. Let F be the family of all sets of size w in a universe of size n = cw2 . By choosing an
649: appropriate constant c > 0, we get that 99% of the sets S, S ′ ∈ F intersect. However, F is (w/c)-regular.
650:    The following is an interesting family of examples, that might help shed light on Conjecture 7.1.
651: Example 7.3. Let Fp be a finite field and n ≥ 1. Let V ⊂ Fnp be a linear subspace of dimension k. Given
652: a set of coordinates I ⊆ [n], define VI = {(vi )i∈I : v ∈ V } to be the subspace obtained by restricting vectors
653: v ∈ V to coordinates I. We say that V is α-large if
654: 
655:                                          dim(VI ) ≥ α|I|        ∀I ⊆ [n].
656: 
657: In particular, this implies that k ≥ αn.
658:     Next, we define a set system corresponding to a subspace. Let X = {(i, a) : i ∈ [n], a ∈ Fp }. For any
659: vector v ∈ Fnp define its corresponding set
660: 
661:                                          S(v) = {(i, vi ) : i ∈ [n]} ⊂ X.
662: 
663: For a subspace V ⊂ Fnp define the set system
668:  (i) F (V ) is an n-set system of size pk .
669:  (ii) For any T ⊆ X it holds that |{S ∈ F (V ) : T ⊆ S}| ≤ p−α|T | |F |. Hence F (V ) is κ-regular for κ = pα .
670: (iii) F (V ) is intersecting iff any v ∈ V contains at least one zero coordinate.
671: If Conjecture 7.1 holds and p ≥ (log n)c for some absolute constant c > 0, then it must hold that V contains
672: a vector with no zero coordinates. This motivates the following problem.
673: Problem 7.4. Let V ⊂ Fnp be a α-large subspace. Prove that if p ≥ (log n)c , for some c = c(α), then V
674: must contain a vector with no zero coordinates.
675:    A previous version of this paper gave a more restricted version of Example 7.3, corresponding to the case
676: when V spans an MDS code. Namely, dim(VI ) = |I| for all I ⊆ [n] with |I| ≤ k. Ryan Alweiss [Alw19]
677: proved the analog of Problem 7.4 for this case, in fact where p ≥ p0 (n/k).
678:

25:                                                                                  1. Introduction
26:                                             In 2002 Fernando Rodriguez Villegas conjectured a surprising lower bound on a
27:                                           natural 1-norm of any non-trivial element of the j-th exterior power of the units of a
28:                                           number field. For j minimal, i.e., j = 1, Rodriguez Villegas’ conjecture is equivalent
29:                                           to Lehmer’s 1933 conjectural lower bound on the height of an algebraic number
30:                                           [Le] [Sm2]. For j maximal, i.e., j = rankZ (OL∗ ), it is equivalent to Zimmert’s 1981
31:                                           theorem stating that the regulator of a number field grows at least exponentially
32:                                           with the degree of the number field [Zi].
33:                                             We now state his conjecture in its strongest possible form.1
34:                                           RV Conjecture. (Rodriguez Villegas) There exist two absolute constants c0 > 0
35:                                           and c1 > 1 such that for any number field L and any j ∈ N,
36:                                                                                ^j              ^j              
37:                                                     kωk1 ≥ c0 cj1          ∀ω ∈     LOG(OL∗ ) ⊂    RAL , ω 6= 0 .         (1)
38: 
39:                                           Here j LOG(OL∗ ) denotes the j th exterior power of the lattice LOG(OL∗ ) ⊂ RAL ,
40:                                                V
41:                                           AL denotes the set of archimedean places of L, and LOG : OL∗ → RAL is defined by
52:                                           strom), U.S. N.S.F. SaTC Grants CNS-1513671/1701785 (Chinburg) and by Chilean FONDECYT
53:                                           grant 1170176 (Friedman).
54:                                              1 The original 2002 write-up of this conjecture was kindly supplied to us by F. Rodriguez Villegas
55:                                           and appears with his permission for the first time in print here (see §7). The 2002 conjecture is
56:                                           somewhat weaker, but F. Rodriguez Villegas later strengthened it to the form given here.

89: Euclidean norm are interchangeable for the purposes of Rodriguezp               Villegas’ conjec-
90: ture. This is simply because on any Euclidean space V , we have dim(V ) kvk2 ≥
91: kvk1 ≥ kvk2 , provided the 1-norm is taken with respect to an orthonormal basis for
92: V . In this paper we will work only with the Euclidean norm and j close to rL .

2137:    A straightforward calculation shows that the top invariant µ1,r (F ), with r = n+ −1
2138: the rank of the unit group OF∗ , equals the regulator of F . It is known [Zi], [Fr], [Sk]
2139: that the regulator of number fields is universally bounded below and hence the above
2140: conjecture is true for k = r.
2141:    In summary, we have seen (18) that Lehmer’s conjecture can be phrased in terms
2142: of the L1 -norm of units under the log map. The above conjecture is an attempt
2143: to quantify, in what seems to be the most natural way, the question of what is the
2144: general shape of L1 , the discrete group of units under the log map.

135:     2. The normalized distance Laplacian. As with the combinatorial Laplacian matrix, it is natural
136: to define a normalized version of the distance Laplacian matrix. In this section, we introduce this new matrix
137: and derive many proprieties of its eigenvalues.
138:     Definition 2.1. The normalized distance Laplacian matrix, denoted DL (G), or just DL , is the matrix
139: with entries                                  

147: of DL (G) the normalized distance Laplacian eigenvalues and denote them ∂1L ≤ · · · ≤ ∂nL .
148: 
149:     It is easy to draw parallels between the properties of A, L, Q, and L and the properties of D, DL , DQ ,
150: and DL . In the remainder of this section, we present results that are known to hold for the adjacency matrix
151: and its Laplacians, followed by their generalizations to the distance matrices.

320: is not. Specifically, we can see that Pn is a subgraph of KP Kn1 ,n2 ,n3 for all n1 + n2 + n3 = n + 2. However, it
321: has been verified using Sage [20] for n ≤ 20 that ρDL (Pn ) < ρDL (KP Kn1 ,n2 ,n3 ) for some n1 +n2 +n3 = n+2.

1033: in contrast to normalized Laplacian, which has the property µ = 2 if and only if the graph is bipartite. It
1034: is natural to ask the following further questions: what is the maximum DL spectral radius achieved by a
1035: graph on n vertices and which graphs achieve it? Based on the behavior of the family KP Kn1 ,n2 ,n3 for large
1036: n1 , n2 , n3 , we conjecture that the maximum DL spectral radius tends to 2 as n becomes large and that this
1037: value is achieved by some graph in the family KP Kn1 ,n2 ,n3 . We also found that the complete graph Kn
1038: achieves the minimal spectral radius and conjecture that is the only such graph.

1041: examples of DL -cospectral constructions producing DL -cospectral graphs, it seemes likely that a suitable
1042: additional restriction placed on a DL -cospectral construction may provide a DL -cospectral construction
1043: method.

589: chromatic number is at most 20 [5].
590: 
591:    In 2013, Wu, Zhang and Li [14] put forward two conjectures in their paper. Although Esperet,
592: Lemoine and Maffray [7] solved one in 2015, the other (Conjecture 5.1) is still open.
593: Conjecture 5.1. va∗eq (G) ≤ d ∆(G)+1
594:                                  2
595:                                      e for any simple graph G.
596: 
597:     As far as we know, Conjecture 5.1 has been verified for complete graphs [14], balanced com-
598: plete bipartite graphs [14], graphs with maximum degree ∆ ≥ (|G| − 1)/2 [18, 20], graphs with
599: 
600: 
601: 
602:                                                   11
603: 
604: maximum degree ∆ ≤ 3 [17], 5-degenerate graphs (so graphs with maximum degree ∆ ≤ 5) [6],
605: and d-degenerate graphs with maximum degree ∆ ≥ 10d [19].
606: 
607:     Looking back to Theorem 1.2, we immediately find that Conjecture 5.1 holds for any plane
608: graph with independent crossings and with maximum degree at least 14. Of course, we may do not
609: like the lower bound 14 for the maximum degree there. If we can pull this bound down to 6, then
610: Conjecture 5.1 holds for all plane graphs with independent crossings.
611: 
612:     Note that every plane graph with independent crossings is 6-degenerate (a graph is k-degenerate
613: if δ(H) ≤ k for any H ⊆ G). Therefore, an alternate task is to prove Conjecture 5.1 for all 6-
614: degenerate graphs directly. Actually, we propose the following conjecture (also see [12]).
615: Conjecture 5.2. va∗eq (G) ≤ k for any k-degenerate graph G.
616: 
617:     If Conjecture 5.2 can be verified, then the bound k for va∗eq (G) is sharp. This fact can be seen
618: from the graph G obtained from Kk via adding t ≥ 2k − 3 vertices, each of which is adjacent to all
619: vertices of Kk . Clearly, G is k-degenerate.
620: 
621:     If va∗eq (G) ≤ k − 1, then G has an equitable tree-(k − 1)-coloring ϕ. Under this coloring, two
622: vertices of Kk shall receive the same color, say 1, and all but these two vertices are not colored
623: with 1, because otherwise a monochromatic triangle appears. Since ϕ is equitable, each of the
624: colors in {2, 3, . . . , k − 1} appears at most three times in G. This implies that there are at most
625: 2 + 3(k − 2) = 3k − 4 colored vertices, contradicting the fact that |G| = k + t ≥ 3k − 3.
626: 
627:     Let d be a positive integer. An equitable d-defective tree-k-coloring of a graph G is an equitable
628: tree-k-coloring of G such that the subgraph induced by each color class has maximum degree at
629: most d.
630:

305: measures enjoy the following factorization property: conditioned on a fixed subconfig-
306: uration σn ∈ ΦVn , the values {σ(x)}x∈Wn+1 are independent under the law µh . Indeed,
307: using (1.6) and (1.9), it is easy to see that, for each n ∈ N0 and any ω ∈ ΦWn+1 ,

461:    Non-uniqueness results for a subclass of completely homogeneous SGMs (i.e., where
462: the reduced fields {ξ̌(x)} and {ȟ(x)} are constant) are summarized in Theorems 2.8,
463: 2.9 and 2.10. The number of such measures is estimated in several special cases by 2q −1
464: (Theorem 2.11), and we conjecture that this is a universal upper bound. In Section 3,
465: we record some auxiliary lemmas. The proofs of the uniqueness results (Theorems 2.2,
466: 2.3 and 2.5) are presented in Section 4. Section 5 is devoted to the in-depth analysis of
467: completely homogeneous SGMs, culminating in the proof of Theorems 2.8–2.11 (given
468: in Sections 5.2–5.5, respectively). In Section 6, we study some fine properties (such as
469: monotonicity, bounds and zeros) of the critical curves on the temperature–field plane,
470: summarized in Propositions 6.4, 6.5, 6.11–6.14, 6.16 and 6.17. Finally, Appendix A
471: presents the proof of Proposition 2.6, while Appendix B is devoted to the proof of a
472: technical Lemma 5.3 addressing the special case q = 3.

1165:    The general case q ≥ 3 with α ∈ R was first addressed by Peruggi et al. [41] (and

1208: these issues for the Potts model with external field, thus extending the results of [18].
1209: More specifically, our analysis (see Proposition 6.16) shows that the critical point θ0+
1210: is the signature of the upper critical function α+ (θ) at α = 0, which has a minimum
1211: at θ = θ0+ . Therefore, it is reasonable to conjecture that the α > 0 analogue of the
1212: interval of activities θ between the critical points θ0− and θcr is the interval [θα− , θα+ ],
1213: 
1214:                                POTTS MODEL ON A CAYLEY TREE                              23
1215: where θα− is the (sole) root of the equation α− (θ) = α and θα+ is the smaller root of the
1216: similar equation α+ (θ) = α. However, it is not clear as to what happens in the interval
1217: between the roots of the latter equation. The counterpart of this picture for α < 0 is
1218: likely to be simpler, as only the equation α− (θ) = α is involved. We intend to address
1219: these issues in our forthcoming work.

43: every sequence of m consecutive integers contains at least one element coprime to n
44: [4, 1].
45: 
46: Definition 1.1. Jacobsthal function.
47: For n ∈ N, the Jacobsthal function j(n) is defined as
48: 
49:              j(n) = min {m ∈ N | ∀ a ∈ Z ∃ x ∈ {1, . . . , m} : a + x ⊥ n}.
50: 
51:   The entire Jacobsthal function is therefore determined by its values for products
52: of distinct primes [4]. In his subsequent elaborations [4, 5, 6, 7, 8], Jacobsthal derived
53: explicit formulae for the calculation of function values for squarefree n containing
54: up to seven distinct prime factors, and bounds on the function for up to ten distinct
55: prime factors.
56: 
57:   There are two derived functions related to sqarefree numbers. The particular
58: case of primorial numbers is the first step when investigating squarefree numbers.
59: The Jacobsthal function applied to primorial numbers h(k) is therefore defined as
60: the smallest m ∈ N, such that every sequence of m consecutive integers contains an
61: integer coprime to the product of the first k primes.
62: 
63: Definition 1.2. Primorial Jacobsthal function.
64: For k ∈ N, the primorial Jacobsthal function h(k) is defined as
65: 
66:                                        h ( k ) = j ( p k # ).
67: 
68:   The more general case considers the product of k arbitrary but different primes.
69: Here, we initially ask for the maximum of the Jacobsthal function applied to any
70: natural number with k prime factors. The reduction to squarefree numbers is
71: sufficient again.
72: 
73: Definition 1.3. Maximum Jacobsthal function.
74: Let n, k ∈ N. Then H (k) is the maximum of the Jacobsthal function for products of k
75: different primes.                                           !
76:                                                                          k
77:                       H (k) = max j(n) =
78:                               dP (n )= k
79:                                                        max
80:                                                         π i ∈P
81:                                                                     j   ∏ πi   .
82:                                                      π1 <···< π k       i =1
83: 
84: 
85:   Obviously, H (k) ≥ h(k) follows by definition.
86: 
94: 
95:                                                  2
96: 
97: Conjecture 1.4. Jacobsthal conjecture.
98: For all k ∈ N,
99:                                          H ( k ) = h ( k ).
100: 
101:   This assumption seems obvious at a first glance because less elements of a sequence
102: are coprime to a smaller prime than to a larger one on average. Nevertheless,
103: Hajdu and Saradha [3] found a counterexample: H (24) > h(24). Moreover, they
104: demonstrated that the conjecture holds for k < 24.
105:   The disproof of Jacobsthal’s conjecture implies that there might be more cases
106: for which a particular choice of primes could lead to longer sequences without
107: coprimes than in case of only applying the smallest primes. In the following sections,
108: we provide exhaustive computational results for the maximum Jacobsthal function
109: H (k) for all k ≤ 43. In fact, there are quite a number of additional counterexamples.
110: The detailed results rather suggest conjectures in the opposite direction.
133:    A set of primes {πi }ik=1 is called covering of a series of consecutive integers haim
134: if every elements of the series is divisible by one of the given primes. We shortly say
135: {πi }ik=1 covers haim .
136: Definition 2.1. Covering.
137: Let a ∈ Z, m, k ∈ N and πi ∈ P, i = 1, . . . , k.
138: 
139:   {πi }ik=1 is a covering of haim ⇐⇒ ∀ x ∈ {1, . . . , m} ∃ i ∈ {1, . . . , k} : πi | (a + x ).
140: 
141: 
142: 
143: 
144:                                                  3
145: 
146:   The prime 2 plays a specific role in coverings. We formulate a lemma which makes
147: the separation of prime 2 straightforward in future considerations. Extending a set of
148: odd primes by 2 may more than double the length of the coverable sequence.
149: 
193: 
194:                                                 4
195: 
196: Definition 2.3. Maximum Jacobsthal function for odd numbers.
197: Let m, k ∈ N, k ≥ 2, and πi ∈ P > 2, i = 2, . . . , k.
198: 
199:    Ω(k) = max {m ∈ N | ∃ a ∈ Z ∀ x ∈ {1, . . . , m} ∃ i ∈ {2, . . . , k} : πi /(a + x )}.
200: 
201:    In other words, Ω(k) is the maximum m ∈ N such that a ∈ Z and πi ∈ P > 2,
202: i = 2, . . . , k exist where {πi }ik=2 covers haim .
203: 
204: Remark 2.4. We emphasise that the arguments k of H and Ω were harmonised for
205: the case of π1 = 2 is included in H. Furthermore, we remind that H is completely
206: determined by considering only squarefree numbers.

256: sequence of maximum length where any πi > pk covers at least two positions in the
257: sequence exclusively. We call such coverings balanced. In analogy with Hajdu and
258: Saradha [3], we prove that the primes of balanced coverings can be bounded.
259: Definition 2.6. Balanced covering.
260: Let {πi }ik=2 be a covering of haim with a ∈ Z, m, k ∈ N, k ≥ 3, and πi ∈ P > 2,
261: i = 2, . . . , k.
262:    {πi }ik=2 is called a balanced covering of haim if m = Ω(k) and for all π j > pk ,
263: 2 ≤ j ≤ k, there exist x, y ∈ N, 1 ≤ x < y ≤ m, with
264:

592: The conjecture 1.4 of Jacobsthal has previously been disproved. This paper provides
593: computational results extending far beyond. New questions arise with it. We dare
594: make the following three conjectures and put them up for discussion.
595: Conjecture 3.1.
596:                               H (k) > h(k)      for all k ≥ 33.
597:   With the detection of the first counterexample, Hajdu and Saradha [3] asked for
598: whether there are more or even infinitely many counterexamples or not. Our results,
599: as shown in table 2, rather imply questions in the opposite direction: Are there
600: infinitely many k satisfying Jacobsthal’s conjecture? Or might k = 32 be the last one?
601: Conjecture 3.2.
602:                            H ( k ) < 2 · H ( k − 1)   for all k ≥ 3.
603:    The specified assertion is equivalent to Ω(k) ≤ 2 · Ω(k − 1) + 1 for all k ≥ 3.
604: Its validity would simplify the calculation of H (k) as outlined in subsection 2.2. The
605: assumption was already made in [3]. All data known so far support the conjecture.
606:    A simple upper bound could be derived as a corollary of the conjecture: H (k) < 2k
607: for k ≥ 3. This bound is in fact not tight. However, the weaker version H (k) ≤ 2k has
608: always been proven by Kanold [10] by other means.
609: Conjecture 3.3.
610:                                 H ( k ) < k2    for all k ≥ 3.
611: 
612: 
613: 
614:                                                13
615: 
616:   This would be a fairly strong upper bound. Kanold proved H (k) ≤ k2 for the cases
617: 2 ≤ k ≤ 12 [10] without knowing the latest function values.
618:   An elementary proof of the assumption could directly be continued to an
619: elementary proof of Dirichlet’s theorem [11]. Furthermore, the theorem of Linnik
620: would also be a conclusion from this conjecture according to [9].
621:   All computed data satisfy the assumption.

157: Definition 1.1. A cluster algebra A associated with a cluster pattern v 7→ Σv is the
158: ZP-subalgebra of F generated by {xi;t }1≤i≤n,t∈Tn .
159:    The degree n of the regular tree Tn is called the rank of A, and F is the ambient field
160: of A.
161:    We also denote by A(x, y, B) a cluster algebra with the initial seed (x, y, B).

394: 1.8 does not hold generally. A counterexample is given by [FK10, Section 6.4] .
395:   We give an application of Theorem 1.8. Let us introduce the Uniqueness Conjecture in
396: [GY19]:
397: Conjecture 1.10 ([GY19, Conjecture 4.4]). In a cluster algebra A(B), for t, s ∈ Tn ,
398: FtB;t0 = FsB;t0 implies that xt and xs are the same non-labeled cluster.
399:    This conjecture is also studied in viewpoint of representation theory of algebras. An
400: f -vectors are a dimension vector of the corresponding indecomposable τ -rigid module over
401: an appropriate 2-Calabi-Yau tilted algebras in additive categorification by 2-Calabi-Yau
402: categories. By using the correspondences, Conjecture 1.10 is equivalent to the follow-
403: ing problem: support τ -tilting modules are uniquely determined by the set of dimension
404: vectors of these indecomposable direct summands. This problem was solved in the case
405: of skew-symmetric cluster algebras of finite type [GP12, Rin11], skew-symmetric cluster
406: algebras of affine type [FG17], and cluster algebras of Cn Dynkin type [FGL18].
407:    In the case that A is of (skew-symmetrizable) finite type or rank 2, we prove Conjecture
408: 1.10 by showing the following statement:
409: Theorem 1.11.       (1) In a cluster algebra of finite type, for any t, s ∈ Tn , if (f1;t , . . . , fn;t )
410:       coincides with (f1;s , . . . , fn;s ) up to order, then xt and xs are the same non-labeled
411:       cluster.
412:   (2) In a cluster algebra of rank 2, for t, s ∈ T2 , if (f1;t , f2;t ) coincides with (f1;s , f2;s )
413:       up to order, then xt and xs are the same non-labeled cluster.
414:    Theorem 1.11 is a theorem of slightly stronger form than Conjecture 1.10. In Conjecture
415: 1.10, the order of the f-vectors is fixed, but in Theorem 1.11, it is not.
416: Remark 1.12. In the case of cluster algebras of An or Dn type, Theorem 1.11 has already
417: been proved by using marked surfaces [GY19, Corollary 4.8].

265: vertex to some graph H ∈ L(n − 1; p; s′), s − 1 ≤ s′ ≤ s. In the special case, when G is a

480: 6    Concluding remarks
481:    In this section we consider the possibilities for improving the inequality Fe (3, 3; 4) ≥ 21. We
482: suppose the following conjecture is true:
483: 
484: Conjecture 6.1. min{α(G) : G ∈ He (3, 3; 4)} ≥ 5.
485: 
486:    If G ∈ He (3, 3; 4; n), n ≥ 25, according to the equality R(4, 5) = 25 we have α(G) ≥ 5. All
487: 24-vertex graphs with independence number 4 and clique number 3 are obtained in [1]. With
488: the help of a computer we check that none of these graphs belongs to He (3, 3; 4). This way, we
489: proved that if G ∈ He (3, 3; 4; n), n ≥ 24, then α(G) ≥ 5. To prove the conjecture it remains to
490: consider the cases n = 21, 22, and 23.
491:    By similar reasoning as in the proof of Theorem 1.1, but with more calculations, it could
492: be proved that
493:                                           α(G) ≤ n − 17.
494: From this inequality and Conjecture 6.1 it follows that Fe (3, 3; 4) ≥ 22.

1073: 4.1. Structural analysis of a neighbourhood. We start by introducing some terminology.
1074: Definition 1.
1075:    (1) A pattern of depth r is any graph P given with a root vertex v such that
1076:                                        ∀u ∈ V (G),            distG (u, v) ≤ r.
1077:        The layer at depth i of P is the set of vertices at distance i from its root vertex v.
1078:    (2) A pattern P of depth r and root v is d-regular if all its vertices have degree exactly d,
1079:        except maybe in the two deepest layers where the vertices are only required to have degree
1080:        at most d.

1082: independent set of P . We deﬁne ei (P ) := E I ∩ NPi (v) for each i ∈ {0, . . . , r}.
1083:    (1) The constraint associated to P is the pair c(P ) = (e(P ), n(P )), where e(P ) = (ei (P ))ri=0 ∈
1084:             r+1
1085:         Q+       , and n(P ) ∈ N is the cardinality of the constraint, which is the number of max-
1086:        imum independent sets of P . Most of the time, we only need to know the value of e(P ),
1087:        in which case we characterise the constraint c(P ) = (e(P ), n(P )) only by e(P ). The value
1088:        of n(P ) is only needed for a technical reason, in order to be able to compute constraints
1089:        inductively.

50: In rank-metric coding, codewords are instead taken from the set of matrices over a finite
51: field, with the distance between two matrices defined as the rank of their difference.
52: 
53: Definition 1. Let Fq denote the finite field with q elements, and Mn×m (Fq ) the set of n × m
54: matrices with entries in Fq , with m ≤ n. The rank-weight of a matrix X is defined to be
55: its usual column rank, and denoted by rank(X). The rank-distance between two matrices
56: X, Y ∈ Mn×m (Fq ) is denoted by d(X, Y ) and defined as
57: 
58:                                   d(X, Y ) = rank(X − Y ).
59: 
60: A rank-metric code is then a subset C of Mn×m (Fq ). The minimum distance of a set C with
61: at least two elements, denoted d(C) is
62: 
63:                          d(C) = min{d(X, Y ) : X, Y ∈ C, X 6= Y }.
64: 
65: 
66: 1.2    MRD codes
77: elements X, Y of C which coincide in every entry of their first m − d + 1 rows. Thus X − Y
78: has at least m − d + 1 zero rows, and thus rank(X − Y ) ≤ d − 1 < d, a contradiction.
79: 
80: Definition 2. A subset C of Mn×m (Fq ) satisfying |C| = q n(m−d(C)+1) is said to be a Maximum
81: Rank Distance code, or MRD-code for short. We say C is a [n × m, d(C)]-MRD code.
82: 
83: MRD-codes are the rank-metric analogue of MDS-codes in the Hamming metric. Unlike
84: MDS codes, MRD codes in fact exist for all values of q, n, m, and all d. This was first shown
85: by Delsarte [24]; indeed, he showed that the bound can be met by an additively-closed set.
86: 
87: Theorem 2 ([24]). There exists an MRD code in Mn×m (Fq ) with minimum distance d for
88: all q, n, m, and all d.
89: 
90: This article with focus on the case where C is an MRD-code which is additively-closed, which
91: we will introduce in Section 1.3. We will recall Delsarte’s construction in Section 3.

93: Remark 1. There are many equivalent ways of representing rank-metric codes. Funda-
99: and their equivalences in Section 9.
100: The most commonly used are vectors in (Fqn )m , and linearized polynomials in Fqn [x].
101: 
102: Definition 3. For a vector v = (v1 , v2 , . . . , vm ), its rank-weight is defined as the dimension
103: of the Fq -span of its entries, i.e.
104: 
105:                                   ω(v) = dimFq hv1 , v2 , . . . , vm i.
106: 
107: Then the rank-distance between two vectors u, v ∈ (Fqn )m is defined as
108: 
109:                                            d(u, v) = ω(u − v).
110: 
111: Many recent results have been obtained by representing rank-metric codes as sets of σ-
112: linearized polynomials. These have the advantage of giving results for a more general class of
113: fields, namely fields with a cyclic Galois extension (i.e. whose Galois group is a cyclic group
114: of order equal to the degree of the extension).
115: 
116: Definition 4. Let K be a field, and L a cyclic Galois extension of degree n, and σ a generator
117: for Gal(L : K). A σ-linearized polynomial is an expression of the form
118:                                                                           k
119:                                f (x) = f0 x + f1 xσ + · · · + fk xσ ,
120: 
121: where fi ∈ L, and k is some nonnegative integer. We denote the set of all such expressions
122: as Lσ . If k is the largest integer such that fk 6= 0, we say that k is the σ-degree of f .
123:                                        s
124: If K = Fq , L = Fqn , then aσ = aq for all a ∈ Fqn , and so a σ-linearized polynomial is a
125: linearized polynomial in the usual sense [59, Section 3.4]. A σ-linearized polynomial defines
126: a K-linear map from L to itself, and by [40], every such map can be represented by a unique
127: σ-linearized polynomial of degree at most n − 1.
128: 
129: Definition 5. The rank and nullity of a σ-linearized polynomial f are defined as the rank
130: and nullity of the K-linear map from L to itself determined by f .
131: 
132: From here on we will refer to a rank-metric code without specifying the representation unless
133: necessary.
134: 
135: 1.3    Linearity and Generator Matrices
136: Definition 6. A rank-metric code is additive if it is closed under addition; i.e. a, b ∈ C ⇒
137: a + b ∈ C.
138: 
139: Clearly C being additive coincides precisely with it being a subspace over the prime field. A
140: set of matrices may also be a subspace over a larger field, for example Fq . In fact, either of
141: the representations of Definition 3 or Definition 4 in fact allow us to consider codes to be
142: linear over fields larger than Fq .
143: 
144: Definition 7. A rank-metric code is F -linear if it is additive and closed under F -multiplication,
145: for some subfield F of Fqn .
146: 
147: Note that what we mean by “F -multiplication” will depend on the representation chosen.
148: We will clarify this further in Section 2.2.
149: Suppose C is an Fqn -linear code. Then we can view C as an Fqn -subspace of (Fqn )m , and
150: represent C by a generator matrix.
151: 
152: Definition 8. Let C be an Fqn -linear code in (Fqn )m with Fqn -dimension k. Then a k × m
153: matrix G with entries in Fqn is called a generator matrix for C if its rows form an Fqn -basis
154: for C.
155: 
156: For additive codes, we have the following simple extension of the Singleton-like bound to
157: codes over any field which is finite-dimensional over its prime subfield.
158: 
159: Theorem 3. Suppose C ⊂ Mn×m (K) is additive, and d(C) = d. Let E be the prime subfield
160: of K, and e = [K : E]. Then
161: 
162:                                    dimE (C) ≤ en(m − d + 1).
163: 
164: The proof follows from a simple dimension argument. If dimE (C) > en(m − d + 1), then C
165: must intersect nontrivially with the subspace of matrices with first m − d + 1 rows all zero,
286: Given an automorphism of Fq and a matrix A with (i, j)-entries Aij ∈ Fq , we define Aρ as
287: the matrix with (i, j)-entry Aρij .
288: 
289: Definition 9. Two codes C1 , C2 are said to be equivalent if there exist X ∈ GL(n, q), Y ∈
290: GL(m, q), and ρ ∈ Aut(Fq ) such that C2 = XC1ρ Y . If ρ = 1 we say they are properly
291: equivalent.
292: 
293: Definition 10. The automorphism group of C is denoted by Aut(C) and defined by Aut(C) =
294: {(X, Y, ρ) : XC ρ Y = C}. The proper automorphism group is denoted by Aut(C).
295: 
296: The corresponding action on a linearized polynomial f by a pair of linearized polynomials
297: g, h and an automorphism ρ is
298:                                                                      n
299:                                   f 7→ g ◦ f ρ ◦ h    mod (xσ − x),
300:                   Pm−1        i
301:                          fiρ xσ . Note that f ρ (x) = (f (xρ ))ρ for all x.
302:                                                                 −1
303: where f ρ (x) =    i=0
304: 
305: The corresponding action on a vector v ∈ Fmq n is by a linearized polynomial g, a matrix
306: Y ∈ GL(m, q), and an automorphism ρ as follows:
307: 
308:                                     v 7→ (g(v1 )ρ , · · · , g(vm )ρ )Y.
309: 
310: Definition 11. The left-idealiser of a code C is defined as
311: 
312:                             Iℓ (C) = {X : X ∈ Mn×n (Fq ), XC ⊆ C}
313: 
314: The right idealiser Ir (C) is defined as
315: 
316:                             Ir (C) = {Y : Y ∈ Mm×m (Fq ), CY ⊆ C}
317: 
318: If C is an additive MRD code with m = n = d, i.e. a semifield spread set, then the left- and
319: right-idealisers are isomorphic to the left- and middle-nuclei. This in fact predates the notion
320: of the idealisers of a code, but we adopt the terminology of idealisers here. Development of
321: these ideas is due to [66], [60], [98], [64]. These concepts are very useful when determining
322: the newness of constructions.
323: We can now directly determine when a code consisting of matrices is equivalent to an Fqn -
324: linear code in (Fqn )m .
325: Definition 12. A code C in Mn×m (Fq ) is said to be Fqn -linear if and only if its left-idealiser
326: contains a subring isomorphic to Fqn .
327: 
328: Since all subspaces of Mn×n (Fq ) isomorphic to Fqn are similar, given two Fqn -linear codes we
329: may assume without loss of generality that their left-idealisers contain the same subspace
330: isomorphic to Fqn . From now on we will fix such a subspace, and by abuse of notation refer
331: to it as C(Fqn ). This can be explicitly realised as the subring generated by the companion
332: matrix of an irreducible polynomial of degree n over Fq .
333: The set of equivalences preserving Fqn -linearity are those for which the matrix Y is in the
334: normaliser of C(Fqn ), which we denote by N(C(Fqn )). It is well known that this is a group
335: isomorphic to the semidirect product of F∗qn with a cyclic group of order n.
336:                                                                               i
337: In terms of linearized polynomials, this corresponds to having g(x) = αxq , where α ∈ F∗qn .
338: Definition 13. Two codes C1 , C2 with left-idealiser containing C(Fqn ) said to be semi-
339: linearly equivalent if there exist X ∈ N(C(Fqn )), Y ∈ GL(m, q), and ρ ∈ Aut(Fq ) such that
340: C2 = XC1ρ Y .
341: Theorem 6 ([60]). Let C be an Fq -linear code in Mn×m (Fq ). Then
342: 
343:                                Aut(C) ⊂ N(Iℓ (C)) × N(Ir (C)),
344: 
345: where N(Iℓ (C)) denotes the normaliser of Iℓ (C) in GL(m, q), and N(Ir (C)) denotes the
346: normaliser of Ir (C) in GL(n, q).
347: 
348: This implies that for an Fqn -linear code, the proper automorphism group is contained in
349: GL(m, q) × (F∗qn × Cn ).
350: Theorem 7 ([94]). Two Fqn -linear codes are semilinearly equivalent if and only if they are
351: equivalent.

405: Theorem 8. Let C be an Fq -linear MRD code in Mn×m (Fq ) with minimum distance d.
406: Then any row-shortening of C by an (m − s)-dimensional subspace of Fm
413: Suppose C ⊂ Mn×m (Fq ), and X ∈ Mn×n (Fq ) is a matrix of rank t. Then the set XC is a set
414: of matrices, each of whose right-image is contained in imr (X). Thus we may view XC as a
415: subset of Mt×m (Fq ).
416: Definition 15. A row-puncturing of a code C is the row-shortening of XC by imr (X), and
417: denoted by Prow,X (C).
418: A column-puncturing of a code C is the column-shortening of CY by iml (Y ), and denoted by
419: Pcol,Y (C).
420: Remark 4. Note that shortening changes the dimension but not the minimum distance,
421: while puncturing changes the minimum distance but not the dimension. Both change the
422: ambient space.
423: In [9] puncturing and shortening are defined slightly differently. However the definitions are
424: equivalent.
425: Another equivalent way to define puncturing is by the set XC for some X ∈ Mt×n (Fq ) or
426: the set CY for some X ∈ Mn×s (Fq ).

470: a Delsarte-Gabidulin code.

590: Further recent constructions for the case of Fqn -linear codes with m = n = d + 1 have been
591: obtained. This case has attracted particular attention due to the correspondence with linear
592: sets on a projective line.
593: Definition 17. A linearized polynomial is called scattered if

600: This definition is due to the fact that the Fq -subspace of F2qn defined by
601:                                   Uf = {(x, f (x)) : x ∈ Fqn }
602: is scattered with respect to the desarguesian spread; that is, it meets any space of the form
603: {(x, αx) : x ∈ Fqn } in at most a one-dimensional space over Fq . Scattered subspaces define
604: scattered linear sets, which are objects in finite geometry which appear in many contexts.
605: We refer to [55] for an overview of this.

786: 6.1    MacWilliams-like identity
787: Definition 18. Given an Fq -linear code C ⊆ Mn×m (Fq ), its Delsarte dual is the set
788:                       C ⊥ = {B : B ∈ Mn×m (Fq ), Tr(AB T ) = 0 ∀A ∈ C}.
789: 
790: We will denote the above bilinear form by (A, B) = Tr(AB T ).
